A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.3 pages)

Kenya remains one of the most vulnerable countries to climate change and increased climatic variability due to the continued importance of agriculture as an economic activity. The Lake Victoria Region, comprised of 14 counties, also represents a significant proportion of Kenya’s population (28%) and economy (25%). This region is facing impacts from the combination of increased temperatures, more frequent and intense droughts, and higher mean annual rainfall. These climate change impacts are most pronounced in the Lake Victoria Basin where over 72% of the working population in LREB. The overall adaptive capacity is low to moderate at best, as most smallholder farmers lack access to information on climate variability and adaptation strategies. In addition, due to time, low productivity creates pressure on land and natural resources, and copland expansion is accelerating the rate of deforestation. This project seeks to implement a transformation of agricultural value chains towards low-carbon, climate resilient pathways through locally-led approach. The project builds on the private sector, smallholder farming networks and the


---
**Page 7**
---

strength of the innovative movement in Kenya and creates a mechanism for technology transfer and upscale leveraging the knowledge and expertise that exists in national and international farming networks. Focusing on six priority value chains (coffee, tea, horticulture, dairy, livestock, and fish), the project will support farmers to adopt and upscale both adaptation and mitigation technology at each value chain’s production, harvesting, processing, and marketing stages.

The project is structured around 4 Outcomes. Outcome 1 builds on the devolution process to improve county-level government services that facilitate upscaling of climate resilient, low carbon, sustainable value chains with a particular focus on smallholder farmers. The project will support counties to develop and implement policies and regulatory frameworks for the sustainable management and restoration of agricultural landscapes and agroforestry systems. It will also support counties to invest in community-based sustainable management and restoration of agricultural landscapes and agroforestry systems, including the development of local value chains. Outcome 2 builds on the devolution process to improve county-level government services that facilitate upscaling of climate resilient, low carbon, sustainable value chains with a particular focus on smallholder farmers. The project will support counties to develop and implement policies and regulatory frameworks for the sustainable management and restoration of agricultural landscapes and agroforestry systems. It will also support counties to invest in community-based sustainable management and restoration of agricultural landscapes and agroforestry systems, including the development of local value chains.

Under Outcome 3, the project will improve the farmers’ access to markets by working with value chain actors, including cooperatives, input suppliers, traders, processors, and financial institutions. The project will also support the establishment of a Kenya Climate Exchange (KCEX), a Direct Access Entity (DAE) in Kenya. Cooperative Bank of Kenya and Equity Bank Africa are proposed as DAEs for the KCEX. Additionally, the project will work with the Kenya Agricultural and Livestock Inputs Regulatory Authority (KALIRA) to improve access to inputs by smallholder farmers.

Under Outcome 4, the project will support the devolution process in improving county-level government services that facilitate upscaling of climate resilient, low carbon, sustainable value chains with a particular focus on smallholder farmers. The project will support counties to develop and implement policies and regulatory frameworks for the sustainable management and restoration of agricultural landscapes and agroforestry systems. It will also support counties to invest in community-based sustainable management and restoration of agricultural landscapes and agroforestry systems, including the development of local value chains.

The project targets 572,000 direct beneficiaries, who are mainly smallholder farmers (men and women). The financial instruments that will be used include loans from commercial banks, microfinance institutions, cooperatives, and equity capital. The project will also contribute to the following target results in alignment with the GCF’s Updated Strategic Plan 8.2 and RPC 2.6 scenarios for a 20-year period at 15% discount rate. The project will be able to earn US $ 10.7 million from the sale of carbon credits generated by the project, which is expected to cover the project expenditure plus the beneficiaries’ investments after 9 years (pay-back period) of project implementation.

The project will directly contribute to the following target results in alignment with the GCF’s Updated Strategic Plan (USP-2). T1 (by supporting the country's adaptation and mitigation goals), T4 Ford (support 572,000 smallholder farmers to adopt climate resilient technologies and practices for sustainable land management, including soil restoration and sustainability managed) through TS locally-led approach. The Project will also support dissemination of climate change information and knowledge in the project area. The Project also aligns with T10 (by linking supported cooperatives and SMEs with the partner private banks for loans).

# B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## B.1 Climate Change Risks (100 words, approximately 2 pages)

### B.1.1 DEVELOPMENT PROBLEMS OF KENYA: AGRICULTURE PRODUCTIVITY, VULNERABLE SMALLHOLDERS AND POVERTY.

#### 1. Kenya's economy contributes to 29% of Kenya's GDP and 65% of Kenya's total exports. Out of Kenya’s 47.8 million people, 18.3 million (38%) are employed in the formal sector while the remaining 29.3 million (62%) work in agriculture, livestock, fishing, forestry, and mining. Smallholder farmers who largely depend on agriculture and livestock sectors (50% or 18.7 million people) are experiencing multidimensional poverty. The Lake Victoria Basin (LVB), a vital agricultural region in Kenya and one of the most densely populated regions in Kenya, is particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts. In this LRB, 72% of the population relies on agricultural output for food security and livelihoods.

#### 2. Lake Victoria Basin (LVB) is the second largest lake in the world, it is fed by rainwater and runoff from the highlands surrounding the lake is used for cropland cultivation, in addition to livestock grazing. The lake itself is not found. Within the LRB is historically known for its high agricultural productivity. However, recent trends of declining productivity in the agriculture sector have increased the vulnerability of smallholder farmers. These changes reduce environmental resilience, affecting both the livelihoods and food security of people living in LRB are smallholders with chronically low productivity! Livestock production systems are characterized by low capacity and low productivity. Developed cash crop value chains often exclude smallholder farmers. Smallholder farmers pooling their production through farmer organizations is a key strategy to improve access to agriculture in LRB, please refer to Feasibility Study (FS), Part 8, Section 12.

#### 3. The sector has witnessed reduction in productivity of the crop and livestock systems targeted by the project such as African beef, wheat, maize, and rice. This is due to high levels of poverty, resulting in unstable incomes, and threatening food security. In addition, climate change impacts are felt by over 10% of the population in the LVB who suffer chronic food insecurity. The project will contribute towards achieving Kenya’s long-term development programs! Key development Challenges of the agricultural sector exacerbating vulnerability include: low productivity; high levels of poverty and dependence; and limited financial resources. For additional details on socio-economic development indicators, please refer to Part 10 Section 12.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 5

## B

### 1.2 Climate-Development Nexus, Climate Drivers and Climate Change Impacts (14)

The LREB is a vital agricultural region in Kenya with the largest source of freshwater and host almost 30% of its population. The climate change impacts on this region are expected to be more severe than for other parts of the country. It is essential to establish sustainable and climate resilient pathways for agriculture in this region to benefit from the opportunities presented by climate change, while minimizing the adverse effects that will negatively impact agricultural production and productivity. Smallholders and the value chain actors are vulnerable to the impacts of climate change and need to be supported to address these challenges. Please refer to Annex 2, FS. Section 6.1 for more on the geographical targeting of the project.

#### B.1.2 Climate Hazard

##### B.1.2.1 Climate Hazards

The LREB is characterized by:

* **Climate Trends:** From 1981 to 2010, the annual average of both daily maximum and minimum surface temperatures increased by 0.8°C (0.5°C per year) in the eastern and southernmost areas, and by 1.4°C (0.4°C per year) in the western and northernmost areas.
* **Precipitation:** The LREB is greater than in the central and easternmost areas of Kenya. These increasing trends from the West to East are consistent with the observed increase in precipitation over this region, which has increased by 6mm (0%) decrease in precipitation. While the northern regions have a net increase in precipitation, rainfall during the wet season has decreased.
* **Floods:** Flooding is the most common extreme event in the LREB.

There is a high level of agreement among models regarding the climate change signal for total annual precipitation. It is projected to increase in the future under both scenarios, with an increase of approximately 20% (95th percentile) in the long-term (2070-2099) under RCP2.6 and increases by around 100% (95th percentile) in the long term under RCP8.5, when compared to the historical period (1975–2005). The projected increase is more pronounced for total annual precipitation than for any other climate variable.

The October-November-December (OND) rainy season increases under both scenarios by 2070, with a much greater increase in RCP8.5 compared to RCP2.6. This is due to the fact that the OND rainy season is more sensitive to the increase in total precipitation during the OND rainy season than the likelihood of seasonal flooding.

14. Extreme heat days, event spells, and heavy rainfall events are projected to increase in the future in the LREB. Under RCP2.6, the annual number of extreme heat days (Tmax>35°C) is expected to remain stable at 20 days or less per year for all countries except for Burundi, Rwanda, Uganda, and Tanzania. The number of days with rainfall exceeding 100 mm will increase from 40 days by 2070 to 50 days by 2100 for all LREB countries except for Botswana, Namibia, Kenya, and Tanzania (Figure 20). Annex 2, Feasibility Study Part A) in Box 3. Annex 2, Feasibility Study Part B) in Box 4. Annex 2, Feasibility Study Part B) in Box 4.

Under RCP2.6 and RCP8.5 it will take each reach 15-20 days for Trans-Nzara, Bugungu, Buika, Kakamega, and Nandi; and 1-2 weeks to reach the same number of days as currently experienced in the LREB. The projected increase under RCP2.6 and RCP8.5 show an increase in the number of heavy rainfall events per year which will average around 10 for RCP2.6 and 30 days by 2100, compared to the current average of 9 days (Figure 23). ## B.1.2 Climate exposure and vulnerability: observed and projected climate change impacts

Similarities between countries are evident when considering their exposure to climate change impacts of variable climate and variability. The smallholders in the LREB are characterized as follows (please refer to Annex 2, Feasibility Study Part A) in Box 3. Annex 2, Feasibility Study Part B) in Box 4 for more details):

- Working small land parcels, typically less than 2 acres.
- Depending on a single or few commodities for livelihoods with limited diversification.
- With limited access to farm employment.

All these countries are high-efficiency, suggesting that elevation may mitigate the number of extreme heat days and by extension, annual average maximum temperatures. The climate change signal is projected to increase in all countries. This encompasses a variety of concepts and elements including sensitivity or susceptibility to harm and lack of capacity to cope and adapt.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 7

B

## Smallholder farmers dominate Kenya's agricultural sector (9) comprising 72% of the total working population in the LREB. However, these farmers face chronic low productivity and limited alternative livelihoods, working on small plots of land with poor soils and water resources. They are also constrained by lack of access to few commodities for livelihoods, with limited access to farmland employment and asset ownership (20). Even in developing countries where the agricultural sector is dominated by smallholder farmers, their productivity remains low compared to large-scale commercial farms. This is partly due to poor land management practices, reliance on traditional farming systems and cooperatives. Further, the adoption of improved technologies and practices among smallholders has been slow because of limited access to inputs such as seeds, fertilizers, pesticides, irrigation utilization, and access to extension services remains relatively low. Productivity systems are typically extensive and have high levels of risk exposure to climate change (10). Smallholder farmers in Kenya are also constrained by lack of reliable information on weather patterns and poor decision-making tools.

### Smallholder farmers in the LREB are highly vulnerable to increasing temperatures due to interannual rainfall anomalies. These anomalous results in dry spells, drought events, extreme rainfall and flooding, which have a significant impact on crop yields (10). The frequency of these events has increased over time, with about 8% of the GDP every five years (3). For example, a study found that economic impacts of climate change on crops in Kenya are estimated to be between $2.5 billion and $4.7 billion per year (4) or between $1.6 billion and $1.95 billion. The severe drought from 1998 to 2000 cost about $2.8 billion in US losses through damage to crops and livestock.

### Smallholder farmers are also vulnerable to increasing temperatures, along with changes in the length and intensity of the long rainy season (MAM), which is the main growing period for most crops in Kenya. These changes have significant impacts on crop yields and other economic systems. These impacts particularly affect smallholder agribusinesses, leading to significant losses in revenue and income, increased food insecurity, and higher levels of poverty among farmers and their families (5). The country lacks climate-proofed infrastructure such as roads, bridges, dams, and water pipelines, which are critical for adaptation.

### In the 14 counties, smallholders face high levels of exposure and sensitivity to climate variability and climate change. This is due to limited access to assets and services, such as water (on average less than 30% have access), land (less than 25%), and financial services (only about 7% have access). Smallholder farmers are also stunting, and energy (only 33% have access to electricity). Gender and cross-sectional inequalities persist with women and youth being more vulnerable. Women and youth lack access to resources such as land, water, finance, and technology. They often do not participate in decision-making processes or activities, have less secure access to land, and have limited access to capital for engaging in income-generating activities.

### Crop and livestock production systems and related value chains in the LREB are vulnerable to climate change. Existing crop and livestock production systems are characterized by low productivity, high vulnerability, and experience financial and income losses due to price disruptions and the loss of crops. Observed and projected climate changes will further exacerbate these vulnerabilities (6). The impacts of climate change on smallholder farmers in Kenya are detailed in the Feasibility Study (Tables 8 & 15, Annex 2, Feasibility Study, Part A).

## Climate hazards (statistically significant climate change)

### Temperature
- Observed: Projected increasing temperatures in most regions.
- Observed: Projected increase in extreme heat events across the region.

### Precipitation - Drought
- Observed: Decreased number of days with rain in southern counties and projected increased frequency of dry spells.
- Observed: High interannual rainfall variability, especially during the OND season driven by ENSO and PDO phenomena during the OND season.

## Climate hazard (statistically significant climate change)
### Temperature
- Input supply, production, and harvest:
  - Early high temperatures reduce photosynthetic capacities in crops.
  - Reduced soil moisture, soil compaction, and higher concentration of contaminants in soils.
- Future impacts: Increased likelihood of crop failure; reduced yield.

### Precipitation - Heavy rainfall and flooding
- Observed: Decreased number of days with heavy rainfall events.
- Observed: High interannual rainfall variability, positive rainfall anomalies influenced by ENSO and PDO phenomena during the OND season.

## Analysis was also conducted for the 6 selected value chains. The 5 value chains are commercial VC (coffee, tea, dairy) and three food security VC (poultry, allied leafy vegetables and fruit trees). The selection of the 6 value chains was based on their importance to the economy and society in the prioritization exercise, based on best available knowledge**. The criteria used for prioritization included climate change vulnerability, economic importance, social importance, and level of potential participation in the value chains by economically and socially vulnerable groups.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 18

B

## CAPACITY TO ADAPT AND RESPOND TO CLIMATE CHANGE

### Farmers' adaptive capacity to climate change is limited due to inadequate access to water, limited income diversification and lack of information on climate change and climate information.

#### The majority of smallholders lack risk-reducing mechanisms such as social safety nets, financial resources, and alternative livelihoods. This makes them more vulnerable to the impacts of climate change.

### Despite previous interventions, the local adaptive capacity remains low, as most smallholder farmers continue to rely on traditional land use systems and management practices that are not climate-resilient. Mechanisms to make climate-responsive production choices. A survey conducted in Kakamega and Vihiga counties revealed that 80% of respondents were not aware of climate change and its impacts on their activities. Furthermore, the limited size of their landholdings also hampers their access to agricultural credit. Limited extension services are also a major challenge as they do not provide farmers with information on the use of supplementary traditional extension-based interventions with risk-sharing mechanisms such as cooperatives. These challenges have led to low adaptive capacity and vulnerability among smallholder farmers in the LUBE, despite adaptive assets (Refer to Annex 2, F3, Part B).

### There is a need for climate-resilient land use strategies that can carbonize into land use strategies and management plans.

#### Since the 1970s, land-use change in the LUBE has been driven by population pressure and low productivity. This has led to deforestation, soil erosion, and desertification. The degradation of land caused by soil erosion and degradation fueled by land use and land cover changes is considered high in many parts of LUBE. These are aggravated by climate change, urbanization, and water management systems and policies. These changes are accelerating the degradation of land resources and increasing vulnerability to climate change.

### Impacts from internalized climate hazards are exacerbated by anthropogenic pressure on key socio-economic activities in the region, such as the expansion of the production land causing deforestation. Fragmentation of forests and wetlands is also linked to the expansion of agriculture and livestock farming and agricultural development. These activities frequently increase soil erosion among villagers and farmers.

### The destruction of catchment forests and wetlands is linked to the disruption of rainfall patterns, droughts, and floods. Prompted drainage has negatively affected water availability and regeneration in the basin, loss of

vegetation and forest emissions[28], and some vegetation species take a long time to recover after extreme drought is Extreme flooding in parts of Buisa, Horo Bay, and Kitumu counties submerges productive lands, destroying crops and property.

## Climate change is partly responsible for crop failures, water shortages, reduced water quality, and decreased hydropower generation capacity in the basin due to low water levels. Scientific reports have confirmed that hydroelectric dams are not designed to withstand extreme flooding events[29].

### For more information on environmental conditions in the LREB, please refer to its Part B, Section 1.4.

#### The public services offered to farmers and cooperatives are insufficient to enable value chain transformation. In addition, there is a lack of market access for smallholder farmers. The government has established in Kenya, reflecting a commitment to addressing climate changes. However, the insufficient climate services and weak institutional capacity have limited the ability of the government to implement policies that would be strengthened, resulting in a gap in translating these objectives into actionable plans that directly support farmers.

#### There is also a lack of support from buyers and other actors in the value chain to help smallholders adopt climate actions and transition to low carbon pathways in their production. Additionally, there is a need for financial incentives to encourage adoption of sustainable agricultural practices. However, existing financial incentives may not currently align with climate action in agriculture. For example, there may be a lack of price premiums or subsidies that would incentivize farmers to adopt more sustainable agricultural practices and reduce emissions.

#### The costs of environmental externalities associated with conventional agricultural practices, such as the use of chemical fertilizers and pesticides, are often not internalized by producers. This can lead to credit, technical assistance, and market information can hinder efforts to adopt sustainable agricultural practices.


### B 4 GREENHOUSE GAS EMISSION PROFILE

#### Kenya's share of total GHG emissions in Kenya. According to Kenya's Updated NDC (2020), GHG emissions are projected to reach 143 MtCO2e by 2030, which is a 15% increase from 93.7 MtCO2e in 2018. The largest share of total GHG emissions comes from the energy sector (60%), followed by land use change and forestry (LUCF) (24%). The projected increase in GHG emissions in the country is expected to increase from 59.3 MtCO2e in 2015 to 90 MtCO2e in 2030, primarily driven by energy sector emissions.

#### Deforestation and land use change are major sources of GHG emissions in Kenya. # B.15. Alignment with country priorities

## The National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP) (2016)

### Kenya's Vision 2030 development plan
- The Kenya Vision 2030 (2007)
- Kenya's Second National Communication*
- Kenya's Third National Communication*

### National Climate Change Action Plan 2018 - 2022**
- National Environment Management and Coordination Act (NEMCA), 2019
- Nationally Determined Contribution (updated, 2020)
- The Water Act, 2016
- The Kenya Climate Smart Agriculture Strategy (KCSAS) - 2017/2028
- The National Environment Management and Coordination Act (1999)
- The National Environment Policy (NEP)*, 2013

### The National Livestock Policy of 2015
Recognizes the increasing frequency and severity of droughts as a result of climate change. This policy outlines measures to enhance the livestock sector's resilience against climate change impacts by improving livestock productivity and contributing to improved livelihoods.

The Agricultural Sector Transformation and Growth Strategy (ASTGS) (2018- 2035) recognizes that climate change is one of the major challenges facing agriculture, and it highlights the impact of climate change on all agricultural sub-sectors (crops and livestock). The KCASG highlights adaptation and resilience strategies to address these impacts.

### At the national level
Coordination and implementation of climate change adaptation policy and action plans are overseen by the National Climate Change Council (NCCC), headed by the President, the Cabinet Secretary of Climate Change, and the Director General of the Kenya Meteorological Department. The coordination and implementation of the climate change adaptation policy and action plans are overseen by the county governor and the climate change unit (CCU). Most county governments have enacted climate change laws, climate change policies, created climate change units, and established climate change committees.

The new National Climate Change Act is under development, and this proposal will update the Act to include it.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 13

B

**B.1.6 Related projects/initiatives**

The project builds on initiatives in Kenya and in the LNER that support climate adaptation and mitigation in the agriculture sector. This project builds on these initiatives as well as on the devolution and decentralized climate governance framework.

The list includes two GCF Readiness Initiatives that ended in 2012: The FAO-supported NAP initiative delivered studies, information and institutional capacity that is being directly used as a contribution to this project. This NAP initiative was implemented by the Kenya Agricultural Research Institute (KARI) and aimed at strengthening technical and institutional capacities for adaptation planning, built the KNA knowledge base, improved evidence-based decision-making in climate change adaptation, and strengthened the national NAP process.

The Readiness project conducted a two-year training that reached 338 climate change units across all departments and counties. The training was designed to build capacity of county government officials at the county level to integrate climate change risks and opportunities in planning and budgeting, and delivered climate adaptation knowledge and skills to county agricultural officers. The project also provided technical support at the staff level to county governments to integrate climate change risks and opportunities in planning and budgeting, and delivered climate adaptation knowledge and skills to county agricultural officers.

The NAP initiative was implemented by KARI and aimed at strengthening technical and institutional capacities for adaptation planning, built the KNA knowledge base, improved evidence-based decision-making in climate change adaptation, and strengthened the national NAP process. The project conducted a two-year training that reached 338 climate change units across all departments and counties.

The list below outlines several other initiatives:

**NDA Strengthening and Country Programming support for Kenya through the National Treasury (GCF Readiness Initiative)**

- Agricultural Climate Resilience Enhancement Initiative (ACREI) (USD$4,630,793, 2018–2024)
- Water Resources Management Project (WRMP) (USD$5,354,887, 2022–2027)
- Water Resources Management Project (WRMP) (USD$5,354,887, 2022–2027)

**Country Programming support for Kenya through the Kenya Integrated Agriculture Management Information System (USD $4,932,416, 2023-2028)**

- Government of Kenya’s Low-Latent City Action Plan (GLLCAp)
- The FAO-supported Livestock Commercialization Project (LocCoP)
- The National Agricultural Research and Extension Service (NARES)
- Africa Rural Climate Adaptation Finance Mechanism (ARCAFIM)
- FAO’s Country Programming support for Kenya through the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock, Fisheries and Irrigation

The project leverages loan financing from the Kenya Commercial Bank (KCB) as a Direct Access Entity that is joining the project as a partner. The project will leverage loan financing from the KCB to support smallholders and agribusinesses in accessing finance for climate-smart agriculture.

For a full list and analyses of synergies with ongoing or pipeline programs and projects, please refer to Feasibility Study Part 2. Further detail on value-chain specific programs and projects can also be found in Annex 23.

B

The Principles of UNA are: 1. devotion to decision-making in the best interest of all; 2. addressing structural inequalities; 3. providing predictable and accessible funding; 4. investing in local institutional capacity; 5. building an understanding of climate; 6. fostering a culture of innovation, experimentation, and learning; 7. ensuring that resources are used for action and investment. Although the project makes use of these principles, it is not yet clear how they will be operationalized and scaled up.

# Barrier 2: Barriers to integration of climate resilient and low carbon strategies into land use planning and landscape management at county level.

## Current environmental degradation trends show that cropland will continue to expand at the expense of the natural environment, unless action is taken now. This expansion will be driven by population growth and increasing food demand factors.

### What much is currently being done to strengthen county government capacity in all aspects of climate change, there remain key policy gaps in terms of managing land use, preventing further land use change, and conserving ecosystems. These gaps are exacerbated by the lack of a coherent national strategy for addressing climate change priorities, and the territorial approach to managing natural resources is un-coordinated. As a result, natural parks and reserves are often fragmented and isolated from each other, leaving them vulnerable to human encroachment and serious encroachments onto fragile areas, including wetlands. There is a need for a more integrated territorial landscape management strategy that addresses biodiversity conservation, land productivity, environmental management, natural resources conservation, climate adaptation and emissions reductions.

### There are, however, missing data and information at local level that would help inform the development of integrated landscape management strategies. In addition to the lack of data on ecosystem services (including carbon sequestration) (for example including the Kenya Atlas of biodiversity, the Atlas of wetlands), these are not regularly updated. These results in a fragmented approach to land use planning and landscape management at county level. There is also a need for all the information into coherent, climate-informed, landscape management strategies and plans.

## Bar 2: Barriers to integration of climate resilient and low carbon technologies.

### Farmers have access to technical knowledge, know-how as well as the material assets required to be able to change their habitual patterns of production towards climate resilient, low-carbon methods. While many of the farmers are aware that climate change is a reality and that it will have an impact on their livelihoods, few are adopting the full suite of available adaptation strategies. This is partly because most farmers lack access to information about climate variability and potential impacts of climate change on their livelihoods; few are adopting the full suite of available adaptation strategies. This is partly because most farmers lack access to information about climate variability and potential impacts of climate change on their livelihoods; few are adopting the full suite of available adaptation strategies. This is partly because most farmers lack access to information about climate variability and potential impacts of climate change on their livelihoods; few are adopting the full suite of available adaptation strategies.

### Climate extension methods have not proven entirely effective in getting the knowledge to farmers, and in several cases, they have been counterproductive. Farmers need more practical, hands-on training that is based on creativity, while supporting appropriate climate action through better rules, regulations, guidance, and norms. Traditional extension methods are often too slow and do not reach all farmers. In addition, many of the technologies are not well suited to farmer priorities and needs. Peer-to peer networks have in the past, and especially during the COVID-19 pandemic, been a very effective way for farmers to connect with each other and share knowledge that make economic, social as well as climate sense.

### However, the adoption of climate resilient, low-carbon technologies is also hindered by the sub-optimal organization of agricultural extension services. The majority of smallholder farmers are organized in informal groups or associations, while smallholders work on very small fragmented plots, in isolation from one another, or in informal groupings that bring little added value to their production and marketing activities. These factors make it difficult for extension officers to act as vehicles for risk sharing when adopting climate resilient, low-carbon technologies; save costs and reduce risks associated with the adoption of new technologies. In addition, many smallholder farmers face organizational challenges that prevent them from fully realizing their potential for members, in terms of economic, social and environmental benefits. These include lack of access to finance, poor infrastructure, and weak institutions that prevent cooperatives from leveraging finance that would enable scaling of climate resilient, low-carbon practices.

### The low access to formal rural finance for adoption of climate resilient, low-carbon technologies prevents smallholder farmers from accessing the financial resources they need to adopt these technologies. While some of the organizational gaps listed above, but also the risk aversion of financial institutions who, themselves, lack the technical capacity and knowledge on how to assess the risks associated with climate change adaptation projects, also play a key role as intermediaries, as a cooperative will always be in a better solvency position than individual farmers. However, there are still significant gaps in terms of access to finance for smallholder farmers, especially among men, women, and vulnerable groups. As a result of these gaps, commodities produced using climate resilient, low-carbon technologies are not yet reaching markets to their full potential.

### The Climate Change Survey and Cooperative Census were conducted by FAO and Agriplus in 2022.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 16

## Barrier 4: The mismatch between supply and demand of finance to support smallholders and their organizations in the transition towards climate resilient/low carbon pathways.

### 58. There are clear information gaps that prevent climate resilient, low-carbon commodities from reaching markets and being rewarded for their value. Smallholder farmers, who produce these commodities, market researchers and buyers are not yet aware of the potential value added of such products. Financial institutions are also not fully informed of the potential returns on investments in climate resilient, low carbon products and services. This is a barrier to scaling up climate resilient, low-carbon products, and as such, because they are not identified, they do not necessarily fetch a better price when sold at market. The lack of awareness among buyers is also a barrier for smallholder farmers, who face costs for adoption of technologies, but no guarantee of increased income – and of financial institutions, which are reluctant to invest in products that are not yet recognized as climate resilient or low carbon.

### 59. In the less organized value chains, because farmers operate in a less aggregated manner, links to markets are also more difficult to establish. This is particularly true for smallholder farmers who produce these commodities on their own, and this creates risks and costs, which are compounded by the inefficiencies in public service delivery mentioned above. The lack of awareness among buyers is also a barrier for smallholder farmers because it does not accurately market their climate resilient, low-carbon products for the transition to financially viable. Furthermore, financial institutions are reluctant to invest in products that are not yet recognized as climate resilient or low carbon.

### 60. Taken together, these barriers and gaps require a coordinated approach where governments and private sector actors can work together to address them, thereby contributing to their comparative advantage towards climate resilient, low-carbon value chains and ultimately, a transformation of the local economy.

## Theory of Change

This project is premised on the following theory of change:

### 61. Enabling farmers in the LREB are currently and will be vulnerable to the impacts of climate variability and climate change, regardless of the climatic scenario that materializes. This vulnerability is due to the over-reliance on rainfed agriculture, low levels of access to inputs and services, limited access to markets, limited access to climate-appropriate production and processing technologies, practices, information and services.

### 62. However, the same vulnerable farmers have at their disposal a set of adaptive mechanisms that, if used properly and in combination with other interventions, can help them adapt to these impacts. These include: farmer cooperatives, producer organizations, credit unions, microfinance institutions, input supply companies, insurance schemes, etc.

### 63. In addition to this, agricultural development in the region runs the risk of becoming a significant source of emissions given the current pressure towards the expansion of oil palm at the expense of forests, the degradation of forested areas and the conversion of land for agriculture. This is exacerbated by the widespread inefficient practices in key existing sectors such as livestock.

### 64. Achieving climate resilient/low carbon pathways towards a climate resilient, low carbon trajectory, taking advantage of the existing strengths of the cooperative movement will be key – while filling the gaps that exist and addressing the barriers to scaling up.

### 65. Therefore, if vulnerable smallholders and value chain actors have access to technologies, markets and finance to support production, processing and marketing of targeted VCs, and are supported by improved climate information, enhanced market linkages and better risk management tools, they will be able to adapt to the impacts of climate change.

### 66. This project is premised on the following theory of change:

#### 67. The project aims at addressing these barriers and gaps through a coordinated approach where governments and private sector actors can work together to address them, thereby contributing to their comparative advantage towards climate resilient, low-carbon value chains and ultimately, a transformation of the local economy.

B

tools and approaches. This project aims to create the conditions for complete transformation of key value chains. This includes working with value chain actors and stakeholders such as processors and buyers in addressing the barriers to adoption and emission reductions. The project will implement an inclusive and participatory approach to all activities.

73. Sustainable intensification: At the heart of the climate problem in the LREB is the need to ensure a minimum level of productivity per unit area, while reducing the environmental footprint of agriculture. This requires addressing trade-offs between various dimensions of agriculture, including food security, nutrition, income generation, and ecosystem services. The project will address these trade-offs by ensuring that agricultural practices are sustainable in terms of water, energy, and land, with the need to sustain ecological services for future generations throughout the broadening of production systems. This will be achieved through the development of new technologies, infrastructure expansion, while restoring or rehabilitating ecosystem services that support agricultural production.

74. Gender and social inclusion: The project will ensure that women and other marginalized groups are participating meaningfully in the transformation of agriculture, to ensure that they also derive resilience benefits. For women, this means ensuring their access to land, credit, inputs, markets, and extension services where possible. The project will not only integrate Gender and Social Inclusion perspectives in all its processes, activities and outputs but will also mainstream these perspectives into the project design towards addressing gender-based or socially-based inequities, in the particular context of addressing climate risks.

### B.3. Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

75. The project is structured around four components to address five co-benefits. Component 1 (Outcome 1) increases food security by enabling local communities to adopt climate-resilient and low-carbon, environmentally sustainable value chains. The purpose of this component is to provide the necessary institutional arrangements and capacities for the uptake of CRSLCA value chains. This will require strengthening the capacity of public institutions at all levels to support the development of policies, strategies, and plans that promote the adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices; improving access to information flows and knowledge systems, and filling the remaining gap in technical capacity in line with developing a national adaptation plan (NAP); and ensuring the effective implementation of the NAP. This will be achieved by adopting actions under Outcome 2.1, 2.2 and Outcome 3 by ensuring that local communities, farmers, and other value chain actors have access to information and adoption capacities climate-resilient practices and awareness for the climate change actions.

76. Component 2 (Outcomes 2.1 and 2.2) achieves direct emission reductions and supports the conservation of ecosystems through the development of a national land use plan that will address the impacts of population pressure and agricultural land expansion. Under this Component, which is mainly implemented by the Government of Kenya, an integrated land-use planning framework for the country’s agricultural landscapes will be formulated to inform future land use planning and land allocation based on climate change impact assessments.

77. Component 3 (Outcome 3) delivers adaptation and emissions benefits from the agro-food sector through the development of a national NAP that includes actions to reduce greenhouse gas emissions, while promoting low-carbon, environmentally sustainable agriculture practices in the value chains. This is reaching smallerholder farmers by providing them with access to information on climate change and adaptation.

78. Finally, Component 4 (Outcome 4) builds on the realization that financial viability of interventions is key to ensuring long-term adoption, scaling and replication, something that had been missing in previous interventions. Activities under this component will focus on developing a business model for the project’s landscape management models, and creates conditions for long-term sustainability of the project by supporting cooperatives and farmer organizations to develop their own financing mechanisms and enter into partnerships with private sector businesses.

79. Study areas within the project covers a vast territory; project activities will be deployed in a coordinated manner. Vulnerability hotspots will be identified through Outcome 2, which will guide the prioritization of landscape management models for each hotspot. The project will also ensure that the most vulnerable communities are reached through existing aggregations, such as informal groups, farmer field schools or cooperatives. Linkages between these aggregations and other stakeholders in the value chain will be strengthened to ensure that they ensure linkages between farmers, cooperatives and the overall agricultural landscapes in which they are nested for multiple benefits: adaptation and mitigation benefits. Please refer to the FS, Part C, section 5 for more information on targeting.

80. Further detail on each activity and sub-activity, and a detailed description of the baseline for each output, is found in Annex A.

## Activity 1.1.1 Develop and deploy innovative and efficient extension methods for disseminating and demonstrating CRLCSA knowledge technologies and practices in an inclusive, responsive and socially inclusive ways.

### Sub-activity 1.1.1.1 Will be to examine and analyze the state of extension services in the agriculture sector in each country, including their strengths and weaknesses with respect to public and private actors.

The purpose of this assessment is to determine gaps and challenges within and between countries and to develop more effective strategies for addressing these gaps and challenges. The project will also assess the potential for climate-resilient, low-carbon production systems and technologies in each country, including their potential to increase food security, reduce poverty, and improve livelihoods in each county and in the national government, engaging input by farmers, FPOs, and other value chain actors.

The assessment will also consider gender gaps in extension programming services.

### Sub-activity 1.1.1.2 Will be to develop new, more efficient, and more effective CRLCSA methods (sub-activity 1.1.1.2).

Following the selection of methods or methods of improvement, the project team will work with each country to develop a set of guidelines for the operation of procedures, operations manuals, and guidelines (sub-activity 1.1.1.3). Each county will receive training and capacity building in the use of these new extension methods.

### Sub-activity 1.1.1.3 Will be to provide training and capacity building on the development and dissemination of climate resilient, low carbon, sustainable agricultural knowledge, technologies, and practices (sub-activity 1.1.1.2).

The project will also ensure that gender and social inclusion issues are integrated within extension programs and will work with each country to develop a set of guidelines for the operation of procedures, operations manuals, and guidelines (including inequalities related to decision-making, labor burden, and access to agricultural information, finances, inputs, markets, etc.). The project team will also provide training and capacity building on the development and delivering specific modules within and in addition to agronomic training, which will include guidance on service provision for women and youth.

### Sub-activity 1.1.2 Will be focused on the delivery of climate information services.

### 10. Introduction of methodologies and test technologies for decentralized carbon accounting

In order to contribute directly and in direct relation to the implementation of the integrated landscape management strategies (Activities 2.1.1 and 2.2.2), the project will mobilize technical assistance to conduct training and demonstrations on the use of methodologies and technologies that can be used by local communities to achieve better integration of emissions reductions practices in future land use planning activities, green accounting, and multi-stakeholder engagement processes (sub-activity 1.3.4). The project will also work towards ensuring that locally specific data on emissions and emissions sources in the AFOCLU sector are not readily available, and therefore there is a need to develop methodologies and technologies for decentralized carbon accounting.

Among the methodologies being explored, the project will draw on IPCC guidance and any previous work undertaken by the Ministry of Environment and Forestry. The project will also consider how to integrate into the National Communications processes, to disseminate these at county level, with the supporting capacity building required in order for counties to undertake their own carbon balance accounting in line with the agricultural landscape management strategy.

A first step in this activity will be to conduct a participatory design workshop with the 14 counties (Meteorological Environmental Services) and other relevant stakeholders. This will include the development of a pilot project, and well adapted AFOCLU carbon accounting methodology (sub-activity 1.3.1). A process will then be established for accessing existing data on emissions sources in the agricultural landscape management sector (sub-activity 1.3.2). This will leverage existing capacity in the climate change units, including that which was provided by the RCMIP project. The project will also work towards harmonizing comparative reports across the 14 counties with county administrations and to ensure harmonized comparative reports across the 14 counties (sub-activity 1.3.3). ## Additional detailed actions for achieving this objective are listed in the Gender Action Plan.

### Activity 105: This activity will be executed by FAO.
#### Actions to address eligible climate solutions for the agriculture sector in relation to climate impacts.
The proposed climate solutions included in this project are based on current knowledge of climate trends and projections, and on currently available technologies for the 6 targeted value chains. However, there is some uncertainty about the future direction of these trends and the availability of new technologies that could create specific vulnerability to the impacts of climate change, given the dynamic and diverse nature of the LRB economy. Technologies are also subject to market forces and policy changes which can impact their adoption and diffusion.

### Activity 106: As a first step, the project will build on the results of the GCF NAP Readiness project and the G-FLOCCA project to identify climate solutions for the targeted value chains (sub-activity 1.1.5). This will include the selection, screening, and prioritization of climate solutions (sub-activity 1.1.5.1) considering available climate risk and vulnerability assessments, as well as the identification of potential co-benefits and trade-offs (sub-activity 1.1.5.2). Assessments will be conducted during this project’s feasibility assessment. The project will support training in the identification of affordable and scalable climate solutions for the targeted value chains at country level; though it is expected that the process will be expanded to other value chains after the project.

#### Assessment of climate solutions
Inform a participatory screening of acceptable climate solutions for the targeted value chains, which will be conducted by the project team in collaboration with the Kenya Agricultural and Livestock Research Organization (KALRO), and other research partners (e.g., CGIAR), as well as farmer organizations and cooperatives. The list of climate solutions will include those that are already being promoted by KALRO (e.g., Green La) that either promote resilience or help reduce emissions in the agriculture sector (sub-activity 1.1.5.2). This list will be shared with the project’s partners, including private sector actors and financial institutions to identify benefits and links to potential providers. The list may be maintained and updated regularly; in the 9th year of the project, the list will be updated based on any new occurring climate impacts, scenarios, and projections for the region.

#### Green lists
These “green lists” will be used under activities 3.1.1 and 3.1.2 to cooperate with cooperatives and farmers to independently develop and implement climate solutions in their value chains. The project will also support the development of green lists by financial institutions on lending towards climate resilient, low-carbon practices (reducing the risk of lending). These lists will be shared among project partners, including private sector actors and financial institutions, as well as private sector innovation and rapid pace of adaptation. These lists will also be shared across counties, and with the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Fisheries to inform policy development.

#### Gender equality and social inclusion
The gender equality and social inclusion objective for Activity 1.1.5 is to enable climate solutions for the targeted value chains that are inclusive of women’s needs and priorities (sub-activity 1.1.5.3). The project will include women, youth, and PLWD in climate solution workshops for futures planning and mobile outreach activities. Additional detailed actions for achieving this objective are listed in the Gender Action Plan.

### Activity 107: This activity will be executed by FAO.
#### Actions to ensure that lessons learned through existing platforms.
The purpose of this activity is to support the upscaling and broader adoption of project outputs and outcomes. In particular, the project will work with counties, cooperatives and smallholder farmers and other value chain actors to document and disseminate lessons learned from the project’s activities (sub-activity 1.2.1). The project will also transfer the project approaches to other value chains, sectors, and regions.

#### Transfer of project approaches
The project will work with the Kenya Climate Change Research Institute (KCCRI), the Kenya Multi-Sectoral Knowledge Platform, the Maafuria center and the Council of Governors, based on a Knowledge and Learning strategy that will be

*Under the FLOCCA program, counties will be supported in conducting county-wide risks and vulnerability assessments during this foreseen project.*

This will be done through desk-based analysis, field visits, and ground truthing with communiti

121. **Partnership mechanisms will be built-in to the development and subsequent revisions of the LMS** to make sure local populations, particularly smallholders who are currently using land for agriculture, have an opportunity to contribute their knowledge and experience in the planning process. This will help ensure that the LMS is not a document that may hinder the achievement of project objectives. The LMS will include interventions on public or communal land development and management, including those related to water resources (e.g., small reservoirs). It will draw from counties and FAO) and will focus on areas where such interventions can facilitate or leverage improved productivity and resilience in agriculture and other livelihood activities. This will also help ensure that the LMS creates a further opportunity to raise awareness among county administrations and elected officials on the benefits of integrating climate change considerations into land management planning, as well as the importance of CIPDS and climate change policies. The project will also support community awareness raising of the benefits of ensuring that their livelihoods are resilient in the face of climate change (see activity 2.1.4) and the LMS (activity 2.1.6), which will feed into the capacity of communities to formulate proposals for financing under activity 2.1.2.

122. **Counties will then come together to develop a LRES-wide landscape management strategy** (sub-activity 2.3) that will be informed by the LMS and other relevant information, including those from the county land use plans, strict land borders, population movements and ecological trends noted within the broader Lake Victoria Basin. The strategy will also include an assessment of existing institutional arrangements for managing landscapes in the region, as well as a review of existing policies and laws that may impact landscape management. This will be done through a consultative process involving county governments, local communities, civil society organizations, and other stakeholders. The County Government will participate in these meetings to make sure the benefits from integrated planning and management are shared with all relevant stakeholders. The strategy will also include an assessment of existing institutional arrangements for managing landscapes in the region.

123. **The project will ensure women, youth, and PLWD are consulted** in the development of the landscape management strategies and other related activities to ensure that their needs and priorities are taken into account when developing and monitoring landscape management plans. Additional detailed actions for achieving this objective are listed in the LMS (activity 2.1.7).

124. **This activity will be executed by FAO** in consultation with the Government of Kenya, including the National Treasury, Ministry of Agriculture, environment, and forestry ministries at national and county levels.

### Activity 2.1.3: Implementing and monitoring climate change (low-carbon) landscape management plans

125. 126. **Selection and prioritization of the sub-projects is conducted using pre-established inclusion and exclusion criteria as detailed in the G-FLOCCA operations manuals**, and requires for finance to be submitted to the National Treasury. These criteria include, but are not limited to: (i) land use; (ii) landscape management; (iii) forest landscape restoration; (iv) community-based sustainable forestry; (v) conservation of biodiversity and carbon sequestration; (vi) conversion to agro-forestry or pasture; (vii) construction of water harvesting structures; (viii) afforestation, erosion control, gully healing in extremely degraded areas; (ix) construction of small reservoirs for irrigation and other infrastructure; (x) wetland conservation, reconstruction, or rehabilitation; (xi) restoration of degraded land; (xii) construction/repair of community water retention ponds, wells, dikes; (xiii) setting up of agro-forestry demonstration plots; (xiv) smaller papyrus wetlands along LVB and other water bodies; (xv) removal of weeds, reduction of effluents and pollution

learned that the use of FFS is reinforced, and that practices and technologies the farmers adopt are suitable to address climate variability and climate risks as well as the other community specific conditions, such as availability of inputs.

The project will also provide a range of climate information services to select climate resilient and low carbon interventions, and to evaluate their productivity results against assumptions related to climate conditions, as well as against productivity and income results.

### 134. A key aspect of Farmer Field Schools is to ensure the sustainability of the approaches promoted in context as well as the capacity of farmers to adapt to changing climatic conditions. The project will therefore aim to contribute towards the development of more resilient farming systems. In this regard, the services provided by the programme will be designed and implemented with a view to ensuring that they are sustainable over time.

In the first year, FADs will undertake, along with activity 1.1.9 and the Resilience Survey (see Section 6.3), a survey of participating smallholder farmers to provide a detailed understanding of climate resilient, low-carbon farming systems in their communities. The survey will be conducted through focus group discussions at each site. At this time, a targeting and group formation exercise will be related, along with awareness raising and Free Prior Informed Consent (FPIC) processes for the selection of FFS facilitators. This will also include an understanding of their rights and responsibilities under the project (sub-activity 3.1.1.2).

### 135. Participating farmers will be selected in each site, with a clear emphasis on considering women as key hotspots, following activity 1.1.7 and the rapid CRA that will be completed with the collaboration of countries. For criteria and methods for selecting participating farmers, please refer to FFS Part C Section 5.3.

### 136. During the first year, FADs will also develop a manual on the selection process, including criteria, manuals, and procedures of the FFS for the selected value chains (sub-activity 3.1.1.3), in close collaboration with the county extension officers. The manual should include information about the selection process, the role of facilitators, scenarios, information on the recommended techniques (linked to activities 1.1.1 to 1.1.5) and how to implement them in a participatory manner. It will also include information on the roles and responsibilities of women, PLWD and people with the levels of literacy. Documentary support, visual illustrations, posters, video clips and other materials should be included.

### 137. FAO will train 3 Master Trainers (sub-activity 3.1.1.4) in the climate resilient, low-carbon sustainable agriculture techniques that are to be promoted through FFS. The training will be conducted using Business School Modules including leadership skills and entrepreneurship such as bookkeeping or contract farming. The training will also include a module on gender equality and women empowerment. The Master Trainers will train all facilitators in the skills and involve the role of women smallholders (and other marginalized groups) within the value chains they are selected to work with.

### 138. Each county will have at least one FFS facilitator, who is a Master Trainer or has completed the FFS training sessions and demonstration activities, managing the FFS facilitators, and reporting on a daily basis in the FFS logbook. The roles and responsibilities of the Master Trainers and facilitators, please refer to section 5.3 of the FFS.

### 139. FFS roll-out will be begun at year 2 (sub-activity 3.1.1.5). In the first year of the FFS training, farmers participate in field visits and demonstrations by Master Trainers and facilitators to learn about climate resilient low-carbon practices. The second year is dedicated to on-farm training and supervision visits by the Master Trainers and facilitators led by the facilitators. In the second year, the facilitators conduct supervisory checks and visits, and organize group discussions with farmers in their communities. The third year will be a period of consolidation using a participatory action research approach for the adoption of climate resilient and low-carbon practices. The FFS partners will also provide technical support to the facilitators during this time (i.e., from the second year until the implementation period). This project will support FFS for the value chains selected by each county over 5 years.

Each FFS group will consist of a minimum of five farmers, and a maximum of ten. The groups can be made up of only men or only women. FFS will be sequenced in three cohorts (2020 FFS). Each FFS group (25 people) will receive training on climate resilient low-carbon practices and technologies. These include the following: inputs, materials for the various climate resilient, low-carbon technologies. For second year of FFS, each FFS group will be provided with a set of tools and materials to support them in their work in a position to do so (to those who can pay membership dues) then will be supported to become members of mutual support groups.

Activity 3.1.2. Disseminate CRCSA technology, knowledge, and assets to cooperative members through peer-to-peer networks and exchanges.

141 Action 3.2 is designed to transfer technology to farmers through cooperatives and will be executed by Agriterra using GCF and Danish MFA funding (sub-activities 3.1.2.1 and 3.1.2.3). The process envisaged for this action is that the project will identify a group of pilot cooperatives, which will then receive multi-year support according to their needs and business plans through a milestone-based approach.

142 The project will begin by identifying and on-boarding participating cooperatives. For selection criteria please refer to section 5.3 of the FS. Following the initial screen and selection, each cooperative will undergo a comprehensive assessment (sub-activity 3.1.2.1) which will include an analysis of their business elements (sub-activity 3.1.2.1.1).

143 Comprehensive Group Assessment (CGA). The assessment includes an analysis of financial data, production, sales and marketing, governance data, transparency, benefit sharing, and will also include an analysis of processes that support the inclusion of women and other vulnerable groups in the cooperative group.

144 Climate Change Check (CCC). The CCC provides an assessment of climate risk on production and processing activities. It includes an analysis of the climate change challenges from both adaptation and mitigation perspectives along with a review of the cooperative’s capacity to address these challenges. Please refer to section 5.3 for detail on screening procedures.

145 Agriterra will use the results of the climate risk assessment conducted by countries under Activity 1.1.5, Challenges and the solutions applied by the cooperative are ranked and compared according to a point system. The impact of each solution is assessed against the criteria in section 5.3.2.1. This will allow Agriterra to determine the appropriateness of responses. Each cooperative receives a “mark” or score against the different criteria at the end of this process, which will be used as part of the selection and ranking process.

146 The project will then engage with each cooperative to discuss the solutions proposed in section 5.3.2.1 on detail from the list of adaptation and climate solutions proposed in each country under Activity 1.1.5.

147 Support received by cooperatives takes many forms:

* **Support for technology transfer:** This includes climate-resilient, low-carbon technologies, including hiring and training extension officers, consultants, extension agents, and technical backstopping (sub-activity 3.1.2.2).

* **Technical assistance:** This includes equipment, supplies (sub-activity 3.1.2.3). For example, the project may assist a cooperative in acquiring renewable energy technology or processing or packaging material; provided it will benefit from the project and is not already available to them.

* **Support for the development of climate-resilient, low-carbon business plans:** This includes support for the development of business plans that include adaptation measures (sub-activity 3.1.2.4) or upgrade of cooperative infrastructure such as water reservoirs, storage facilities, shelters, barns and technical equipment to improve resilience against climate change.

* **Support for the development of climate resilient, low-carbon business plans and business cases towards mobilization of capital and finance (output under 4.1):**

* **Support for the improvement of governance and management particularly for meeting climate-related needs:** This includes support in the areas of financial management, transparency, benefit sharing, gender inclusion (Activity 3.1.3).

148 The project will support up to 100 cooperatives in 150, onboarded in the first 3 years of the project. To support the implementation of multi-year support workloads under sub-activity 3.2.2, the project will deploy service providers on a rotational basis (sub-activity 3.1.2.4). The project will also provide technical assistance to cooperatives through AgriProlink.

These partners will be identified using the Agri-Prolink roster of experts or through open procurement. Screening criteria for selection are outlined in section 5.3 of the FS.

149 Because the demand for support is likely to exceed available resources of the project, and to ensure equitable access to project support among beneficiaries, the support workloads will include a measure of prioritization based on the level of need and vulnerability of each cooperative (sub-activity 3.2.2). The use of loans and finance services by participating financial institutions (as leveraged financing) to the more mature cooperatives under output 4.2.

Examples of such trainings and workshops that are being developed are the Women’s Leadership Youth leadership trainings developed by AgriFem as well as the Gender Action Learning System (GALS) trainings for women leaders in cooperatives. These trainings will be conducted on topics related to production, processing and value addition, and trade. For example, during Women’s leadership workshops (which are also structured around the GALS methodology), women will learn about their rights and responsibilities as farmers and action plans are developed to improve women's meaningful participation in the cooperatives' activities. Solutions for increasing women's access to credit, land tenure security, extension services, resources and support, ensuring enrollment of women extension workers and in non-traditional positions.

101. At the end of the project, the expected result of these efforts is that more women, youth and PLVND are more easily empowered to participate in decision-making processes at all levels of their cooperatives and communities through gender objectives through all activities, including through a gender impact assessment at end of project and post-project evaluation.

102. This proposal builds on the experience gained from previous projects implemented by AgriFem and AgriFFS related to the Gender Action Plan (above) for Kenya.

103. This project will be implemented in the context of the SCF fund, in cooperation with AgriFem and the Government of Kenya at national and county levels, and in particular the beneficiary cooperatives and farmer organizations.


### Output 4: Scaling up CRLCSA market and finance

Output 4.1 - Climate resilient agricultural products are sustainably financed and upscale by the adoption of new business models and practices among farmers and value chain actors.

Outcome 4.1.1 Increased access to markets and profitability of climate smart, low carbon sustainable agricultural products

Many smallholder farmers are producing using practices that are not well adapted to climate change, or that are not economically viable in a changing climate. This is particularly true for the production of maize under rainfed conditions. Under Outcome 3, the project supports smallholders in producing more (and better) without resorting to land expansion and monoculture. However, this alone will not be sufficient to ensure that farmers can continue to adapt to variability, climate change and climate shocks. However, at this stage the market incentives to sustain climate resilient agricultural practices are still weak for many farmers – that is, farmers do not see ways they can obtain price premiums for climate-resilient, low-carbon commodities.

**Area 4.1.2 Work with buyers and aggregators to increase demand and market opportunities for CRLCSA commodities**

The project will work on the demand side of the value chain to increase uptake of CRLCSA commodities produced by smallholder farmers in Kenya. This is a key strategy that will help ensure that the project is not only key to encouraging continued sustainability, replication, and upscaling. Therefore, the project will work with country government agencies, buyers and aggregators to create demand for climate-resilient agricultural products, particularly for climate-resilient, low-carbon products.

Demand creation in each of the 5 value chains has long been established, including through the targeting exercise undertaken at the start of project design (demand was a criteria for selection) and through the market research conducted by AgriFem. In all other value chains, there is a need to continue to create demand for tea and vegetables fetches a higher price on both local and international markets. B

## Activity 4.1.2: Increase access to various third-party certification and labeling schemes.

The purpose of this activity is to remove market access barriers and create a distinct market pathway for commodities that are certified as climate-resilient, low-carbon, or sustainably produced. This certification creates added value and ensures better prices are fetched for goods produced at a higher cost for the farmers and processors. The activity will also ensure that the costs of certification are included in the price-setting process.

### Sub-activity 4.1.2.A: Identify the most suitable certification scheme(s).

This sub-activity involves identifying the most suitable certification schemes, such as those recognized by international organizations like IFGAM, EcoCert, GAF, Rainforest Alliance, Fair Trade, and others, depending on the commodity and its market. The activity will also ensure that the costs of certification are included in the price-setting process.

### Sub-activity 4.1.2.B: Support cooperatives in accessing and complying with relevant certification schemes.

The activity will provide targeted technical assistance, training, and expert services to help cooperatives access and comply with relevant certification schemes on a voluntary basis. Third-party certification bodies will be engaged to assist the cooperatives in achieving compliance with these schemes. The costs of certification will also be included in the price-setting process for each of the 6 value chains (sub-activity 4.1.1.7). After the end of the project, this service may be extended by the GCF and/or other donors to ensure that it is sustainable.

### Sub-activity 4.1.2.C: Ensure compliance with certification schemes across the CA and CC assessments.

The activity will ensure that cooperatives comply with relevant certification schemes during the CA and CC assessments. The costs of certification will be included in the price-setting process for each of the 6 value chains (sub-activity 4.1.1.7). After the end of the project, this service may be extended by the GCF and/or other donors to ensure that it is sustainable.

### Sub-activity 4.1.2.D: Ensure the cost of certification is included in price-setting processes across the CA and CC assessments.

The activity will ensure that the costs of certification are included in the price-setting process for each of the 6 value chains (sub-activity 4.1.1.7). After the end of the project, this service may be extended by the GCF and/or other donors to ensure that it is sustainable.

#### B.4. Implementation arrangements (max: 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

##### Accredited Entity (AE)
- FAD will serve as the Accredited Entity (AE) for this project.
- FAD will be responsible for the overall management of the project including all aspects of project appraisal, i.e. administrative, financial and technical oversight and supervision throughout project implementation; ensuring funds are effectively managed to deliver results in line with the approved project document; ensuring that the project is implemented according to the quality of reporting to the GCF; and (c) project closure and evaluation.
- FAD will ensure these responsibilities in accordance with the terms of reference for the AE as outlined in the GCF General Procedural Manual, Section 10.2.3.1, and the Project Implementation Guidelines for Kenya (AFG-Kenya).

##### Role of AE
- The AE will be responsible for setting up a dedicated Project Task Force (PTF) to guide project cycle guidelines.
- The PTF will be composed by the Budget Holder (BH), the Lead Technical Officer (LTO), Funding Liaison Officer (FLO), HQ Technical Officer and other officers, as appropriate.

##### Co-financing and leveraged financing
- The co-financiers are responsible for reporting to the AE in accordance with the detailed provisions outlined in the funding agreement between the co-finance provider and FAD. This includes the terms of the funding agreement signed between the co-finance provider and its capacity of AE, on co-financing activities execution, and the monitoring and evaluation of the co-financing funds under the coordination of the Project Management Unit (PMU) through the Project Steering Committee (see Project execution section below).

## Project partners

### Project execution

#### Executive Entities (EE)

194. The project will be executed by FAO and Agrierna in a co-execution modality to deliver the project activities funded by GCF proceeds and Danish MFA (DMFA) funding. A description of each EE is indicated in section 7 of RFP 2018/03.

195. FAO will act as an EE and will ensure strong country-driven execution of project activities and will oversee the execution of the project activities funded by GCF proceeds and Danish MFA (DMFA) funding.

196. A FAO Project Execution Team (PET) will be set up in FAO-Kenya, comprising relevant FAO country personnel (Assistant FAO Representative Programme, Administrative and operational staff) and will be responsible for FAO EE functions.

#### Agrierna

Agrierna is a global farmer advisory organization that takes its roots in the agricultural sector and farmer advisory services. It has been operating in Kenya since 2013 as a member of the CGIAR Research Program on Climate Change, Agriculture and Food Security (CCAFS). Agrierna works with farmers to improve their livelihoods by implementing technology transfer programs in Kenya leveraging expertise of Dutch agriculture experts on climate resilient agricultural practices. Agrierna has been working closely with FAO in Kenya for several years in implementing technology transfer programs in Kenya leveraging expertise of Dutch agriculture experts on climate resilient agricultural practices.

197. The Government of Kenya, acting through National Treasury, will execute the activities co-financed by the Government of the Netherlands and GCF proceeds. The Government of Kenya will be responsible for executing activities 2.1.2. The National Treasury will retain decision making power and retain responsibility over the use of the funds allocated to these activities. The Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Irrigation (MLI) will have the final authority in selecting the sites and interventions and the implementation of the activities in accordance with G-FLUCCA procedures. The Government of Kenya will also administer GCF proceeds as EE.

198. The co-EEs will conduct their activities in accordance with the terms and conditions, guidelines, and rules.
