#### B

200\. A dedicated Project Management Unit (PMU) will be established and hosted by FAO in Nairobi, Kenya with a regional office located in Kitui. The PMU will be functional for the entire duration of this project. The PMU will coordinate directly with all relevant government departments, county administrations, external partners, service providers, NGOs, and other stakeholders and will provide overall management support to ensure delivery of day-to-day project activities.

201\. The PMU will be led by the assistant FAO Representative (program) and a National Project Coordinator (NPC) who will be recruited by FAO. The NPC will be responsible for overall project management and coordination with all relevant stakeholders, including government departments, county administrations, service providers, NGOs, and other stakeholders. The NPC will also manage the implementation of the project’s resources, legal and contracting agents. Regional project coordinators, program assistants, a gender and social inclusion focal point, and administrative support staff will be recruited by FAO.

202\. Please refer to Annex 2, F, Part V, Section B for more detail.

**Project Strategic Committee (PSC)**

A Project Strategic Committee (PSC) will be established to provide strategic guidance for the project. The PSC will be co-chaired by the PS of the Treasury (MOA) and the Principal Secretary of the MoA. As indicated in section B4, FAO is part of the PSC and as such is a party to the final decision-making process. Members will also include:

* The Principal Secretary (Representative) from the Ministry of Cooperative Development & Marketing (MoCDM)
* The Principal Secretary (Representative) from the Ministry of Environment
* The Chairperson of the MoA
* The Chair of the Climate Change Committee Chair of the CoG
* The Director General of the Kenya National Economic Board and
* The Representative of FAO in Kenya

204\. The role of the PSC will be to (i) provide overall guidance and direction to the project, ensuring it remains within agreed objectives, timeframes, and budget; (ii) monitor the progress of the national project coordinator; (iii) monitor project risks and the effectiveness of mitigation measures; and provide guidance on new project risks, and agree on prioritization of these risks. The PSC will also review the annual project implementation report, including the quality assessment report and any other relevant reports, to ensure that the agreed deliverables are produced satisfactorily according to the approved work plan and budget. The PSC will also provide guidance for the project’s implementation; (iv) approve the annual project implementation report, including the quality assessment report and any other relevant reports; (v) review and agree on the project’s annual budget; (vi) identify and address any shortcomings; and (vii) provide ad hoc direction and advice for exceptional situations when the project coordinator’s tolerance level is exceeded.

205\. The PSC will be expected to meet formally at least once every 12 months. Formal meetings will be scheduled and held in Nairobi, Kenya with a regional meeting being organized by FAO (dates and venue to be confirmed tentatively dates for the following meetings being agreed upon Any Other Business). Extraordinary meetings of the PSC can also be called as required.

The project will also be supported by a Project Technical Coordination Committee (PTCC). The PTCC will be chaired by FAO Kenya, and supported by representatives from the Ministry of Environment, Ministry of Agriculture, and the MoA. The PTCC will provide technical guidance to the PMU and NPC on all aspects of the project including in-person and online platforms and its function will be to conduct overall Monitoring, learning, and reporting on project progress and performance. The PTCC will also facilitate communication between the PMU and high-level PSC meetings. The PTCC will provide technical guidance to PMU and to the PSC.

## Figure 4: Flow of Funds

### B.5 Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

209. From 2015 to 2019, Kenya's economic growth averaged 5.7%, making it one of the fastest growing economies in Africa. However, despite this growth, the country still faces significant challenges, with poverty rates at around 43% as of the end of 2020. Half of public debt is external. This limits the Gov't's ability to finance projects and activities to deal with the climate change challenge. In addition, the lack of irrigation infrastructure in Kenya has created significant barriers; the Gov't cannot take adequate steps on its own. In addition, GCF investments enable existing efforts and translate them into a coherent strategy for adaptation. The project is at an early stage but will be ready to enter the stage for large scale autonomous adoption. The GCF has explored alternative modes of financing for this project but has not been able to secure funding from other sources. The irrigation infrastructure in Kenya was developed by the World Bank under the National irrigation program. These provide a solid baseline on using GCF resources to support this project.


---
**Page 44**
---

210. In 2019, the Kenyan government was able to disburse only T524 million USD in climate-related development expenditures. The same report indicates that international partners would need to sustain 87% of the NDC costs for the next five years. This is a significant challenge given the limited resources available and the urgent need for international investors to scale up their investments in adaptation sectors, which requires more investment than mitigation activities. The GCF supports smallholder farmers through the LREB, which includes the agriculture private sector, while taking pressure off the National Treasury in the face of competing priorities. The GCF supports smallholder farmers by providing them with access to finance and technical assistance for climate-smart communities and livelihoods in the LREB. Thus, GCF financing will contribute to faster economic growth and development in Kenya through the provision of financial resources to support approaches that enhance carbon sinks as defined in the NDC.

211. Smallholders are by definition, highly vulnerable on their own. There currently are limited mechanisms whereby farmers can pool together their resources to transform their agricultural practices into climate-smart ones. Access to land, low adaptive capacity, low soil, and aggregation among farmers is seen here as a way of sharing risk, reducing vulnerability, and increasing the resilience of smallholder farmers who are more vulnerable than other vulnerable smallholders. Some are reached through existing groupings that either exist or will need to be formed for them to access finance from the GCF. Others are reached by the LREB through its own outreach efforts in forming cooperatives. In terms of exposure and sensitivity to climate hazards, all at the same.

212. At the end of 2019, there were approximately 350,000 smallholder farmers in Kenya affected by climate change: small farms in size and number of animals, generally subsistence production or limited surpluses, low productivity. Little information is available on the extent to which these farmers are vulnerable to climate change. However, access to additional adaptation/reduction mechanisms (slightly higher incomes), the ability to access knowledge and market opportunities, and the ability to share resources with other members of a cooperative or with others, other co-op members are still variable. That is why we want to encourage non-members become members at the end of 2019.

213. Cooperatives vary in size, climate capacity and maturity. The number of members in a coop (coop size) is not directly related to its ability to provide services to its members. A large cooperative does not necessarily provide more climate services to its members; a small coop can be more mature than a larger one. Each cooperative has its own challenges and it particular challenges related to climate change adaptation and mitigation.

214. We defined a resilience index based on the Resilience Index Measurement and Assessment Framework (global standard) that measures the ability of a household or individual to cope with shocks at the level of an individual or household or individual-level. It will allow us to differentiate between members and non-members. By our simulation, using the GCF’s baseline scenario, we can see how much more resilient a cooperative is compared to its members. Non-membe rs rank in the 40% range, whereas membe rs rank in the less than 50% range. Because our target is to increase resilience of smallholder farmers and not just those who are already resilient, we focus on vulnerable farmers, and also understand attribution to various variables on which the project has acted.

215. GCF grants will be used for two types of activities: (i) direct support to beneficiaries; and (ii) indirect support directed at groups of beneficiaries. One group of beneficiaries will receive support through FFS and semi-formal organizations while the other will benefit from the LREB. The GCF will provide a total of KES 10 billion in grants over five years. These GCF grants are not used to develop the commercial capacity of coops but to increase their capacity to change the behavior of smallholder farmers, and to support them with access to finance for climate-smart agriculture (CSA), access to markets, acquisition of inputs, etc. According to the specific terms each coop faces in addressing climate risks.

216. While this project will mobilize grants from GCF and other partners, the concessionality provided to different beneficiaries will vary: individual farmer beneficiaries on the lower end of the resilience spectrum are expected to benefit most from the project. The LREB is a key mechanism for scaling up CSA in Kenya. It provides grant-funded entry into farms with no financial counterpart contribution required. Cooperatives on the relatively higher end of the resilience spectrum will be supported through FFS and semi-formal organizations. These cooperatives will leverage private finance from Banks and other donors. The exit strategy for this project entails “graduating” beneficiaries to access commercial credit facilities, including those provided by GCF. In addition, a revolving fund loans from financial institutions will be enabled to independently and continuously acquire and transfer climate-related services to smallholder farmers.

217. No other initiative targets cooperatives or integrates business models aimed at making the economic case for utilizing CSA in Kenya. The LREB is a key mechanism for scaling up CSA in Kenya. It provides grant-funded entry into farms with no financial counterpart contribution required. Cooperatives on the relatively higher end of the resilience spectrum will be supported through FFS and semi-formal organizations. # GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 41

B

## B. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The exit strategy of this project has multiple aspects: the first part is an "internal" exit strategy that develops the capacity of smallholder farmers and their organizations to continue to benefit from the added resilience provided by the project in its existing cooperatives so that they can also benefit from the added resilience and risk sharing mechanisms that it creates. This will be done through the development of new technologies and practices that will enable them to increase their resilience, increase their productivity and competitiveness, and access markets more effectively as a means of continuing accessing climate extension services and also increasing their market access.

The second part of the project's exit strategy is making sure that cooperatives themselves become self-sustaining. This will be done by ensuring that they have the capacity to continue to implement climate resilient, low-carbon practices in the business plan and financial management of the cooperative. The third part of the project's exit strategy is to ensure that the new technologies and practices sold by the cooperative will ensure that farmers do not face excessive risk in adapting to climate change. This will be done through the development of a new membership fee structure, which ensures that all members pay an equal amount for services provided.

The fourth part of the project's exit strategy is to ensure that the cooperatives are able to continue providing services and as an added benefit of membership. These new services would be covered by existing mechanisms and will not require any additional funding from the project (including licensing and certification). It ensures that farmers receive fair prices, and that market demand is met.

A third part of the project exit strategy lies in the link established with financial institutions who are ready to provide credit to smallholder farmers. This will be done by ensuring that the cooperatives themselves have the capacity to create a lasting, replicable, and easily scalable mechanism that can be applied to any cooperative. The fourth part of the project's exit strategy is to ensure that the cooperatives are able to continue providing to cooperatives to articulate better business plans and funding requests and on the institutional capacity strengthening provided by the project.

The fifth part of the project's exit strategy lies in ensuring that the cooperatives become more appropriate, accessible financial products for climate resilient, low-carbon agriculture (under outcome 4).

## Opportunities

The project strengthens capacity, disseminates knowledge and assets to smallholder farmers and their organizations, county governments and financial institutions. Some of the project activities create requirements for improved data collection, data and other support to farmers and cooperatives on the basis of improved capacity built by the project.

Under Component 1: The county governments will continue to deliver and disseminate climate information services, data and other support to farmers and cooperatives on the basis of improved capacity built by the project.

Under Component 2: The counties implement their Landscape Management Strategies using their own development funds. The total costs for landscape management activities are estimated at a maximum of 5% of the costs of initial implementation, annually (USD $25 per hectare per year). These include activities such as tree planting and maintenance, soil conservation, water harvesting, erosion control, as cleaning paths, creating the buffers, ensuring drainage and other regular land management activities. Total costs of maintaining these landscapes are estimated at a maximum of 1% of initial implementation annually (USD $5 per hectare per year).

Under Component 3: The project supports the delivery of technologies, knowledge, and assets to smallholder farmers through Farmer Field Schools and Cooperatives. Some maintenance costs may be incurred by farmer groups and farmer organizations for the project-supported assets that may include greenhouses, irrigation kits.

## Resilience to gender and social safeguards to reduce climate risks for the most vulnerable; as well as risks to women's health, wellbeing, and livelihoods from increased engagement in economic activities.

### 231:
* **Resilience:** In this project is a direct result of adaptive capacity and sensitivity. To measure progress in increasing resilience, we will use the Resilience Improvement Matrix (RIM) which draws heavily on the Resilience Index that draws heavily on the Resilience Index Methodology and Analysis Framework (RIMA), which was developed by the World Bank. The RIMA is a tool for assessing climate risk and vulnerability to food-related shocks, the RIMA it contains sub-indicators that can also inform climate resilience. The RIMA uses three categories of indicators: 1) those that are directly influenced by climate change; 2) those that are indirectly influenced by climate change; and 3) those that are not influenced by climate change. The RIMA includes sub-indicators for each category, which are defined as follows:

    * **Access to Basic Services (ABS):** This includes access to water/irrigation, energy, markets and roads, health services. The reason these indicators are included is because they facilitate or hinder access to resources and services that can be used to adapt to climate change. They also provide a buffer in the face of climate change; they also facilitate or hinder access to public services and aid in the case of extreme weather events.

    * **Access to Assets (ATS):** This includes assets directly related to the production of income from agriculture, livestock, fisheries, and forestry. These assets are included because they provide a buffer against climate impacts. They can be used as buffers to climate impacts for example, livestock that can serve as buffers to climate impacts. For this project we have retained the following productive assets: land, livestock, trees, fish, seeds, tools, and equipment. The RIMA includes indicators related to access to finance and access to processing and value addition; these are not included in the RIM because they do not provide a buffer against climate change.

    * **Adaptation Capacity (AC):** This category also includes sub-indicators that will be directly influenced by the project and are limited to adaptive capacity in relation to climate change. Though all of the indicators listed above can contribute to adaptation, this category is limited to those that relate specifically to adaptation. These include: access to information and communication technology; access to education and training; and access to climate information and early warning.

    * **Access to Social Safety Nets (SSN):** In this category we included only one indicator related to social safety nets, which are defined as a buffer against the impacts of climate change. Incontinuity in the provision of social safety nets is considered a risk factor for food insecurity and malnutrition, and the dependence on rainfall agriculture, given that precipitation is very important for crop production.

# Reducing Emissions from Forest and Land use (MRS4)

The calculation of mitigation benefits for this project was made using two different methodologies: The FAD model was used to calculate emissions reductions from AFOLU activities in all value chains except dairy and poultry, and the GLEAM model was used to calculate emissions in the livestock value chain. The project achieves significant reductions in emissions intensity per unit of output. Globally, the project achieves 10 million tons CO2e.

## Mitigation benefits are expected from the following activities and technologies:

- Reforestation, rehabilitation, and conservation of land under the Landscape Management Plan (Output 3.1)
- Integration of agro-forestry in existing value chains (Output 3.1)
- Improved livestock management practices (Output 3.1)
- Improvements in water and energy use efficiency (Output 3.1)
- Increased crop yields per hectare (Output 3.1)
- Improvements in emission reduction per unit of Mg, kg of meat and kg of eggs through improved production systems (Output 3.1)
- Reductions in emissions from food loss and wastage (Output 3.1)

## Key assumptions for the calculation of mitigation for each model are included in FS, Part C, section 6.4 and Annex 22.

### D.2. Plans shift potential (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

The fundamental paradigm shift that this project fosters is the translation of value chains from low productivity to high productivity through climate-smart, climate-resilient, low-carbon pathways through localized approach. The project leverages cooperatives as a climate risk mitigation mechanism and as an enabling institution for farmers in negotiating power on the market. This is done by strengthening the ability of farmer organizations and cooperatives to access markets and technology, while also providing them with the necessary tools and mitigation technologies to enhance proactive climate resilience in production and business. Ensuring that these institutions are well positioned to provide services to their members will ensure that farmers will implement climate solutions that make both climate and financial sense to them.

However, there is a need for policy makers to develop and adopt national plans that ensure that climate costs are integrated as part of the regular cost of farming. Prices of commodities will then reflect the true cost of production, which will be higher than current prices. This will be achieved by individual farmers in adapting to climate change. The increased market opportunities fostered under Outcome 1 will also provide a strong incentive for farmers to adopt climate-smart practices that fetch better prices, stronger bargaining positions and increased market shares, because both production and quality of products are improved.

This project dovetails with local governance and development processes, recognizing that while climate capacity is stronger at national level, it is imperative to strengthen it locally. The project generates a paradigm shift in the way that governments interact with farmers by building the capacity of the family farm and smallholder farmer to better manage their resources through the use of improved technologies and practices. This will also build the capacity of county governments to exercise normative influence on markets and producers by using market-based instruments, such as carbon credits, to incentivize climate-smart production systems including ecological services that underpin agriculture. Coventional extension services will therefore be complemented with innovative methods for knowledge sharing and exchange between farmers and other cooperatives – as climate related services that cooperatives offer their members to sustain climate resilience are increasingly recognized at the same time.

### Potential for scaling up and replication

Climate change adaptation measures introduced in this project will support resilient, low carbon, sustainable agricultural systems. These systems can be scaled-up through forms that can create a model for further updating to other beneficiaries in the same value chains or extended to other value chains and other sectors within the country. The project also provides opportunities for knowledge sharing and knowledge exchanges. Under Outcome 1, innovative extension methods and climate information

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 49

The services developed and delivered by county governments can readily be made accessible to other counties through the action of the Council of Governors or the Maafila center. The decentralized carbon accounting method will also be extended to other counties under Outcome 2, while the climate risk management tools promoted under Outcome 2 can also be extended to other counties. Under Outcome 2, the integrated climate risk management tool will be developed and disseminated in a way that ensures that lessons are drawn regarding the impact of such agricultural landscapes and agricultural production systems on carbon sequestration and emission reduction. The technologies promoted under Outcome 3 – although they may not be replicable or scalable at this stage – will be made available to other counties through the Council of Governors and Maafila center, once the lessons learned from pilot participants are seen and will see and document the economic benefits of applying these technologies as well as the FFS and/or cooperatives. Farmers in the same value chains who are not participating in the project will be able to see – through the research activities of the project – the benefits of climate-resilient, low-carbon technologies because the technology transfer mechanisms created under Outcome 3 will continue to be available for other counties and will be made available at a very low cost. The same applies to knowledge and information independently (at a price).

All the technologies promoted under Outcome 3 – whether they are FFS or cooperatives – are replicable and can be scaled up. It is expected that this project is that participating farmers will see and document the economic benefits of applying these technologies as well as the FFS and/or cooperatives. Farmers in the same or different value chains who are not participating in the project will be able to see through the research activities of the project – the benefits of climate-resilient, low-carbon technologies because the technology transfer mechanisms created under Outcome 3 will continue to be available for other counties and will be made available at a very low cost. The same applies to knowledge and information independently (at a price).

The project also creates an internal scaling process whereby smallholder farmers can aggregate into more mature farmer organizations, culminating in high-performing climate-resilient, low-carbon cooperatives and farmer organizations that are able to access the technologies promoted under Outcome 3. This will create an attraction effect which combined with project efforts to remove barriers to aggregation, will support the scaling up of these technologies through the creation of a network of climate-smart smallholder cooperatives and farmer organizations who will be able to access the technologies and knowledge independently (at a price).

Under Outcome 2, financial institutions are naturally scalable and replicable. The project creates capacity in financial institutions to deliver and develop concessional finance for farmers designed specifically for climate-resilient, low-carbon agricultural landscapes. This will enable the financial institutions to offer concessional finance to eligible beneficiary cooperatives (whose capacity to develop bankable proposals will also be developed) that are scalable and replicable by financial institutions to other areas and other value chains easily and at very low cost.

## Potential for knowledge sharing and learning

The project is designed around four fundamental elements of its project strategy. Under Outcome 1, county administrations, particularly those departments concerned with climate change, agriculture, and rural development, will be capacitated in the delivery of climate-resilient services with the participation of farmers and farmer organizations – including climate information services and climate risk management tools. The project will also create a network of farmer organizations as vehicles of climate knowledge dissemination or in traditional modes of on-demand, demand-driven extension services. Under Outcome 2, the project will capacitate county administrations to learn about the benefits of climate-resilient, low-carbon landscape management in support of agricultural production systems and the use of climate information services through the REEB platform and other mechanisms to promote knowledge sharing and learning.

The project is designed around four fundamental elements of its project strategy. Under Outcome 1, county administrations, particularly those departments concerned with climate change, agriculture, and rural development, will be capacitated in the delivery of climate-resilient services with the participation of farmers and farmer organizations – including climate information services and climate risk management tools. The project will also create a network of farmer organizations as vehicles of climate knowledge dissemination or in traditional modes of on-demand, demand-driven extension services. Under Outcome 2, the project will capacitate county administrations to learn about the benefits of climate-resilient, low-carbon landscape management in support of agricultural production systems and the use of climate information services through the REEB platform and other mechanisms to promote knowledge sharing and learning.

245. The project also generates knowledge and learning for other value chain actors, and particularly buyers, aggregators and finance institutions. 246. Finally, the project includes sub-activities related to the evaluation and documentation of the effectiveness of the project’s interventions. ## Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

### The project supports the improvement of the enabling environment for farmers and cooperatives. First, by improving access to finance that supports climate resilient, low-carbon value chains, by improving the supply chain infrastructure, and by providing technical assistance and training on how to develop bankable business cases. This portion of the project, under outcomes 3 and 4, is a significant part of the project’s contribution to the enabling environment for farmers and cooperatives.

### The approaches taken for transferring technology under Outcome 2 are intended to reduce the costs and risks faced by farmers when attempting to adapt or adopt low carbon practices. First, all practices have been designed with the aim of reducing costs and increasing returns. Second, by providing training to farmer extension and networking reduce the costs of disseminating knowledge and practices, while ensuring that they are tailored to local conditions. Third, by providing technical assistance and support fully considered in farmers’ business cases when proposed, farmers will not face any surprises or unanticipated costs. Dissemination of best practices and lessons learned from the project will further reduce costs and risks.

### The project also supports the improvement of the enabling environment for farmers and their organizations to access finance that supports climate resilient, low-carbon value chains, by improving the supply chain infrastructure, and by providing technical assistance and training on how to develop bankable business cases. This portion of the project, under Outcomes 3 and 4, is a significant part of the project’s contribution to the enabling environment for farmers and cooperatives.

### Carbon technologies will ensure long-term sustainability of project outcomes. By also working with buyers, the project will help to create new markets made for the commodities produced under this project.

## Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies

251. The project contributes to improving regulatory frameworks and policies at county level in each country where it is implemented. This includes the development of a climate change action plan consistent with the country’s CDP and investment plans (e.g., the landscape management strategies) and Council Assembly resolutions, as well as the integration of climate change into national policies. The project supports the county’s climate change acts and action plans (refer to FS Part B, section 3.3). Through the Council of Governors’ resolution, the project will ensure that the principles of the CDP are reflected in the regulatory legislation will also be extended to other counties and cascaded to national level policy making. The project will also support the development of county-level policies on biodiversity conservation and regulations related to biodiversity, water, energy and pollution if relevant at country policy levels.

253. For further details on how the project contributes to national policies, strategies and plans, please refer to section D.6 and section 3 of the feasibility study.

## Index is composed and measured. Project activities will have direct linkage to SDGs 1, 2, 3, 5, 8, 13 and 15.

### The measureable benefit of the project is job creation in the six value chains, while indirect benefits include increased agricultural productivity in the supply chain for agriculture, improved food value chains, increased employment in the transporting and banking aspects of the value chain. In particular, the project expects to create jobs in the agro-processing industries.

### Asterisks (*) indicate specific experts to promote benefits to the broader population of the TREG through the implementation of climate-sensitive landscape management strategies, and the overall improvement of the environment.

#### Other indirect socio-economic benefits include:
- Improved access to education for children and especially girls (whose working tasks may be reduced)
- Improved health through access to increased, more diverse and better quality food (other produced or purchased)
- Improved access to health services (increased ability to pay)
- Increased income
- Reduced rates of gender-based violence and child labour
- Increased opportunities for meaningful employment for youth, women, persons with disabilities

### Environmental Benefits:
At the farm level, environmental benefits arise from the implementation of sustainable land management practices such as crop rotation, intercropping, the use of leguminous or crops in rotation, as well as the judicious application of fertilizer, reduction in use of noxious agro-chemicals and herbicides, which results in:
- Improved land productivity and increased biomass production.
- Improved soil fertility
- Reduction in pests and microorganism content.
- Increase in agro-biodiversity at farm and landscape levels.

At the landscape level the project also expects to deliver through the implementation of the landscape management strategies, including but not limited to: reduced Interventions, improved forest cover and forest density, reductions in rates of runoff and erosion, improvements in biodiversity status, and improved water quality.

### D.4 Needs of recipient (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### The vulnerability in LRE is due to agriculture primarily being rainfed and small-scale production systems that are highly dependent on rainfall and precipitation changes; cropping crop failures in the short-term and production declines in the long-term.* Most smallholder farmers are particularly vulnerable because they lack access to resources such as land, water, finance, extension services, and inputs. These factors have led to declining yields over time due to climate trends, resulting in unstable and unreliable income. For example, a survey of smallholder farmers in Kalambo District found that 80% of the respondents had experienced crop failures in the past five years.* They do not have access to stable financial resources and credits from public or private supporting institutions* Overall, they are unable to cope with climate change impacts such as droughts, floods, pests, and chronic food insecurity, requiring short-term emergency food relief and long-term development programs.*

#### Economic vulnerability is also a major concern. Smallholder farmers in LRE have limited options for their actions to respond to climate hazard and require the project supports to reduce costs of climate-related shocks and stresses.* The project will support smallholder farmers by providing them with tools to deliver climate services and easing access to market. Smallholders working small plots (0.2 ha or an average, double plot) are particularly vulnerable due to lack of productivity.

https://documents.worldbank.org/curated/en/687941468221707248/Lake-Kariba-Landscape-Strategies-and-Action-Plan.pdf

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 52

## Access to climate services (e.g., sanitation, education, health, markets). They also have limited access to climate-proof inputs (e.g., seeds, varieties, breeds, etc.). Approximately 75% of their income depends on a single crop.

### In the LURB, yields are symptomatic of low productivity aligned to climate variability, high levels of loss and wastage in the post-harvest and processing cycles. Many farmers are producing with limited technical means. The majority of smallholder farmers are not able to access inputs or services that would allow them to move away from unsustainable mono-cropping land use and water use, although many farmers are becoming aware of land degradation and its impacts.

### Smallholders who produce surpluses (i.e., above household consumption) can post production and access markets. However, the majority of smallholder farmers in Kenya do not have access to markets or inputs. Individual farmers have very little access to financing on their own. Cooperatives and farmers organizations generally capture the benefits of market access and are able to provide services to members. The project will build capacity in articulating business propositions for financial institutions. Women, youth and persons living with disabilities (PWD) will be prioritized as they have limited access to land, income finance and technical support. Please refer to the Gender Assessment for further detail.

### This project therefore creates a system where governments and private sector come together to remedy this situation. The project is designed in such a way that it can be scaled up across the spectrum. Vulnerable smallholders are trained and capacitated as the best available climate resilient, low-carbon crop production systems. They will also have access to inputs and services that they need and also have the opportunity to further reduce their costs through joining cooperatives. At the other end of the project spectrum, there is a strong focus on building the capacity of public institutions so that they are able to include in the regular cost of agribusiness the provision of climate services to their clients. This will be done by providing training and technical assistance at the national level and additional capacity to access private finance, and thus perpetuate the model autonomously.

### County budgets are limited and even though counties are legally mandated to dedicate 2% of their budget towards agriculture, this is not always the case in practice. The project seeks to provide a mechanism to direct public resources to cooperatives. This is why the project designed to strengthen the capacity of county agricultural extension services to provide climate change adaptation information, enabling, normative manner, by providing guidelines, regulations, climatic information, and advisory.

### D. Country ownership (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

### The project supports the implementation of Kenya's national climate change policies, strategies and plans as follows:

#### 1. The National Climate Change Act of 2016 - Paragraph 2 of Section 3 which lays out specificities how to implement the Act. This project will support the Act by providing the formulation of programs and projects to enhance resilience that are intergenerational, gender equity mainstreamed and compatible with Kenya's national development plans. The project will also provide technical assistance needed to promote low carbon technologies. All points are supported by the project's activities. The Act of 2016 is implemented through the Ministry of Environment and Forestry (MoEF) and its Directorates, which coordinates and facilitates climate change adaptation policies and action plans government-wide. The MoEF has a dedicated Climate Change Unit that is responsible for all matters supported by the project.

#### 2. Kenya's National Adaptation Plan - Kenya's 2020 Updated Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) sets out the projected target of 143 MtCO2e by 2030. The project will support the achievement of this target and the projected emissions are expected to be reduced to approximately 143 MtCO2e by 2030, which the NDC states is a reduction from current levels of 185 MtCO2e. Over the course of the project's lifespan, the project will tentatively mitigate a projected 286MtCO2e.

#### 3. Kenya's National Climate Plan - 2015 to 2030: The project supports national level as well as county-level climate change adaptation at the national level while also contributing to economic development to build resilience. Outcome 1 of the project supports the mainstreaming of climate

## National Climate Change Action Plan 2018–2022

The National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP) is a key component of Kenya’s NAP, which aims to link climate change adaptation with development and improve water conservation by revising main sectoral policies. The NCCAP will contribute towards the achievement of Sustainable Development Outcomes 1, 2 and 3 and will contribute to implementing the NCCAP. Specifically, the transfer of technology and knowledge (including through capacity building) and the dissemination of climate information services (CIS), will support the NCCAP initiatives.

### 264. For further information on the National Climate Change Action Plan (energy, water, environment, forests and others) please refer to section 3 of the FINA Part B.

#### The National Designated Authority (NDA)

The NDA has been closely involved in the development of this proposal. The NDA will be responsible for ensuring that the project is implemented as proposed and will ensure that the proposals are consistent with country needs, priorities, and plans. The NDA will play a key role in contributing to the dialogue between county governments, linking with the NCCAP.

#### 265. This project is the culmination of a long consultative process that involved the active participation of many stakeholders including representatives from government ministries, central government agencies and development partners to ensure consistent intervention with national priorities and plans. The project has been developed in close collaboration with the FINA program. The lessons from past projects and cooperation with other ongoing or piloted initiative have been taken into account while developing this proposal. Please refer to section 3 of the FINA Part B for more details.

#### 270. At local level consultations also took place with smallholder farmers, men and women. Individual farmers were contacted and interviewed regarding application of climate resilient, low-carbon technologies or practices in their farming activities. The survey was conducted among a sample of 150 smallholder farmers who were members of cooperatives and others who were not (included). Gender-specific quantitative data on the use of climate-resilient agricultural technologies and practices was collected.

#### D.B. Efficiency and effectiveness

The project structure proposed for this project seeks grants to support technical assistance, technology transfer, capacity building for vulnerable smallholders along with nationally-funded targeted investments in climate change adaptation and mitigation (e.g., water harvesting structures, irrigation systems, and climate information services). Considering the high level of needs for climate finance in Kenya (Refer to section BII and CII), this project is proposed as a pilot initiative. The project will be implemented in the LREB region, this proposed initiative makes optimal use of the GCF contribution to generate a public private partnership model that can be scaled up across other regions in Kenya.

The project for the LREB and the national economy justify the level of financing requested. The project aims to support 100,000 smallholder farmers with climate-resilient agricultural technologies. Total cost comprises of a GCF grant of USD 29.215 million (64% of total project cost). Government of Kenya

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 54

Through the Ministry of Treasury of Kenya contributing USD 14.0 million (28%), a Grant of USD 57.9 million (11.6%) from the Ministry of Foreign Affairs of the Government of Denmark (DMFA), and USD 95.81 million (19%) from the World Bank, the EFA has explored the financial and economic performance of the activities considered in the Funding Proposal of the project "with" and "without" GCF support.

The project focuses on the top 7 priority value chain products: maize, beans, wheat, rice, sorghum, groundnuts, and cowpeas. These are the most important crops for more than a household food security orientation (African legume vegetables, poultry and fruit tree). The total project area is estimated at 105,324 ha of which 78% will be under GCF support.

The project assumes that out of the total beneficiaries, 69.5% will have access to one or more of the benefits. Out of the total beneficiaries the project assumes that: 35,750 will have tea; another 35,750 coffee; and 28,800 will have dairy and poultry. The total number of beneficiaries is estimated at 106,490. In addition, 42,400 and 28,800 beneficiaries will have dairy and poultry respectively. These beneficiaries will also have access to the other benefits.

The incremental production of the targeted value chain products provides the benefits for the EFA. The project assumes that the incremental production is achieved through the use of climate smart practices (in case) and estimated increased productivity under climate sensitive and low carbon technologies (with project). The economic performance was evaluated at both household level and project level. At the household level include the Net Present Value (NPV, economic and financial), the Internal Rate of Return (IRR, economic and financial), and the Benefit Cost Ratio (BCR) for each of the value chain products. At the project level labor (for the financial analysis). Using the aggregated cost and benefits flows of the VC product models and the assumptions on the number of beneficiaries, the incremental production was estimated at both household and project levels.

The economic viabilities were estimated for two climate change scenarios - RCP 2.6 and RCP 8.5 - for both WGP and WP cases. The results are presented in the following figures. The first figure shows the stream of benefits from climate change adaptation practices, involving agricultural and livestock production practices.

On the basis of the yield levels and cost of production under WGP and WP cases, the profitability indicators were estimated. Considering the Financial Internal Rate of Return (IRR) for 20-year period as a summary measure of project profitability, the IRR was found to be positive in all cases. The first figure shows the second bar chart below compares the IRRs of WGP and WP cases for all VC models under RCP 8.5 scenario. The same trend of results was obtained for the RCP 2.6 scenario as well.

### As the bar chart shows, the farmers who participate in these VCs can generate enhanced profits starting from year 4 onwards compared to their baseline situation. The project intervention will allow them to increase their profitability from cultivating the same area of land and also adopt more carbon-intensive low-carbon technologies under the WP scenario which also contributes to reducing the carbon emission.

276. Based on individual VC commodity model analyses, the project level financial and economic profitability is 10% for RCP4.5 and 8.5 scenarios with benefit cost ratio of 1:1.1 and 1:1.3 respectively under RCP4.5 and RCP2.6 scenarios for a 20-year period at 10% discount rate (USD 9.7 million) and 20 years at the same discount rate for 25 years under RCP 4.5 (USD 12.9 for RCP 2.6). The project can break-even the project cost within a short time period of about 3-4 years from the commencement of the project and commence operation in both climate change scenarios.

The project financial internal rate of return is 21% with benefit cost ratio of 1:24, and 22% and 1.25 respectively for RCP 8.5 and RCP 2.6 scenarios under 10% economic discount rate. The project is also financially viable in terms of the net present value (NPV) which is positive at a significant level with net social present value of USD 113 million and USD 116 million for RCP8.5 and RCP2.6 scenarios. The project therefore is suitable for receiving public funds for investment.

Further details on the economic and financial analysis undertaken for the project can be found in Annex 3.

## Replicability

The project's strategy for replication builds on various components:

- Building climate change responses into cooperatives' business models and demonstrating their profitability will ensure that the most vulnerable farmers have permanent access to the best available technologies, information and services.
- Ensuring the country government’s participation in the project to identify and disseminate lessons learned beyond project beneficiaries, after project completion.
- Deploying an active learning and knowledge sharing strategy that builds on experiences of local Governments, Climate change Multilateral partners and other stakeholders.

The technology transfer mechanisms set up by the project can continue to be available to farmers after project completion.

## Sustainability

There is a good level of commitment among county governments towards climate change. However, the conventional approach to delivering climate services to farmers (e.g., distribution of inputs) has not been sustainable for long-term use and does not reach all last mile users and beneficiaries.
- The project transforms the ways in which government support farmers and their communities through providing guidance, enabling investment, normative frameworks, capacity building, etc. It can partner with farmer organizations and cooperatives to scale up climate-smart agriculture in the region. As a result of this, the economic, social, environmental and climate benefits of technologies proposed will be less risky in adopting them.

The project will support the creation of technology transfer mechanisms, knowledge sharing platforms that will outlast the duration of the project. The project will identify priority leverage strategies and plans are developed to ensure that low-carbon technology becomes a sustainable part of local business models, all farmers entering into cooperatives will have access to knowledge and will benefit from climate services provided directly by cooperatives.

## Activity 1.1.3: Develop and test methodologies for provincial carbon accounting

Under this activity, county governments will acquire technical capacity to conduct carbon accounting at the provincial level through training as well as through learning by doing in linkages to the development of landscape scale carbon accounting methods used for delivering this activity. This includes acquisition of resources and institutional capacity, acquisition or development of software tools, and hiring of technical expertise to backstop emissions reporting in each county.

### 1.1.3.1 Cluster-level workshop bringing together county-level representatives from agriculture, environment departments and climate change units

- **1.1.3.1.1 Conduct a participatory design session to select and develop a LREB-specific decentralized carbon accounting methodology**
- **1.1.3.1.2 Set up the process for data collection and analysis in each county**
- **1.1.3.1.3 Develop operating manuals for data collection, analysis and reporting**

### 1.1.3.4 Conduct learning by doing through training of county-level staff to prepare emissions reports

#### 1.1.3.4.1 Conduct systems needs assessment in each county
- **1.1.3.4.2 Develop a database for data collection in each county**
- **1.1.3.4.3 Collect and analyze crop, livestock and economic data through annual surveys and extension services**

### 1.1.3.5 One emissions report per year at level 2, and one at level 5

#### 1.1.4.1 One step take and gap analysis report on agricultural databases
- **1.1.4.2 Upgrade databases**
- **1.1.4.3 Value chain level reporting system to monitor trends**

### Activity 1.1.4: Upgrade and update agriculture databases, crop and productivity data

Under this activity the project supports county governments in developing more robust agricultural databases that can serve cooperatives, business and government agencies by supporting the documentation of climate change adaptation and mitigation for low-carbon technologies. This activity builds on existing methodologies agriculture extension and services.

### Activity 1.1.5: Assess and select climate solutions for the agriculture sector in relation to climate impacts

In this activity, county governments build their capacity to understand local climate risk to agricultural production and to select appropriate climate solutions such as carbon technologies in which to invest. This can be done through a training or a menu from which cooperatives and farmers can make informed decisions about investment and lending.

#### 1.1.5.1 Training for county climate change units, agriculture departments and agriculture extension services on climate solutions

- **1.1.5.2 Conduct a participatory screening and pre-selection of climate technologies for the 6 Value Chains**

#### 1.1.5.3 Cluster based training in each county to identify appropriate carbon solutions and climate solutions identification.

## Activity 2.1.2 Implement and monitor climate-resilient and low-carbon landscape management plans.

This activity is financed with a combination of GCF funding and co-financing from the TAD/GEF through county governments, leveraging local resources (e.g., FLOCCAs) and the GEF-7 Mt Elgon Project EGP. Local governments will develop a landscape plan to ensure local community buy-in for the landscape management plan. Each county will implement selected climate-resilient and low-carbon land use practices such as reforestation, afforestation, watershed management, agroforestry, and soil conservation in improved land management actions on 10% of agricultural landscapes targeted by initiatives over the 6 years of project implementation.

#### Output 4.2 Vulnerable smallholders and their organizations have increased access to gender-responsive and socially inclusive financial products that support climate resilient, low carbon growth.

The project will support the development of capacity within financial institutions in the region to invest in value chains with financial institutions in the region. The project will support financial literacy and financial management training for 200 cooperatives in the region. This activity will help strengthen and expand the range of financial products available to support smallholder farmers, particularly women, in the region. The project will support financial institutions in the development and roll out of new financial products and services to promote the uptake of CRLCSA value chains.

The project will support farmer cooperatives in accessing the financial services offered by the partner financial institution. This assistance is to ensure that cooperatives have the capacity to mobilize their own financing, including through the use of their own business plan, and through the submission of proposals. This will bridge the gap or the need for climate resilient, low-carbon finance in the 6 value chains.

The project will assist select cooperatives in each of the six value chains in developing a business plan to invest in agroecosystem services, biodiversity, or other ecosystem services.

## E. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words)

### 297. MELs will be undertaken in compliance with FAO and GCF policies. As AE, FAO will ensure that all EE are making use of a common monitoring and measurement system to analyze and quantify the causal pathways and attributable changes, the contribution of project activities and the overall results of the project.

#### Monitoring System

* 298. The implementation, supervision, and day-to-day monitoring will be carried out by the Project Management Unit (PMU), with the support of FAO Kenya and information flows from each EE. Monitoring will ensure that:
    * (i) project deliverables and outputs are produced in accordance with the project results framework.
    * (ii) all identified risks are managed effectively, including those related to climate change.
    * (iii) any newly identified and appropriate mitigation strategies are applied; and
    * (iv) agreed project climate-related benefits are being delivered. The data collected will be used for reporting purposes.

* 291. Project outputs will be monitored separately as well in relation to the achievement of higher level project results and overall GCF goals. Reporting on results will be required as follows:

#### Project Progress Reports

* 293. Semi Annual Progress Reports: The PMU will submit semi-annual progress reports which will be sent to the Budget Holder. The progress reports will include details of achievements against targets and milestones. Executing Entities also will report their financial and operational progress on a six-month basis.

#### Annual Performance Reports

* 294. Annual Performance Reports: The MEL Officer and EE will provide inputs to the Annual Report for each year of implementation. The MEL Officer will ensure that the indicators in the results framework are monitored throughout through the online tools and other procedures. The annual performance reports will be due


---
**Page 81**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V1.0 | PAGE 77

At GCF 60 days after the end of each calendar year. The final project annual report and the terminal evaluation report will serve as the final project report.

## Technical Reports
285. Technical Reports will be prepared by consultants as part of project outputs (deliverables) and to document and share project outcomes and lessons learned.
The drafts of any technical reports will be submitted by the PMI. The Lead Technical Officer (LTO) will be responsible for ensuring appropriate technical reviewers are identified and engaged in the review process.

## Project Annual Report (Final APR)
286. Annual Financial and Co-financing Reports: containing information compiled from all EEs on expenditures against targets, including expenditures from all sources of co-financing.
287. Project Annual Report (Final APR): A final APR report will be submitted by the AE with input from EE to the GCF not later than 6 months after technical completion of activities.

## Field-level Activities
288. For field-level activities, the project will make use of georeferenced monitoring.
The project places particular attention on ensuring the monitoring of different groups of people in the project area, especially those regions/activities relevant to women and/or vulnerable populations.

### Monitoring of Core GCF Results - GHG emissions reductions and Adaptation Benefits
289. The project will conduct the CDM and EACN simulation exercises in the final year of the project, leveraging updated data on project execution and output delivery regarding specifically:
- Number of heads of cattle and productivity thereof by end of project.
- Changes in land use (e.g., deforestation, reforestation, afforestation and conservation).
Using these simulations the project's final evaluation will compare projected achieved emissions reductions with anticipated reductions at inception. Please refer to Annex C2 for details on simulation methodology.

### Resilience Surveys
290. As indicated in its P. Part 6.3., a resilience survey may be requested by the GCF as part of the project's final evaluation.
The survey will be designed using indicators and questions that are relevant to all sub-objectives contained in the revised result index.
It will be tailored to include additional questions to minimize the reporting burden on farmers and the project and to maximize data collection. The measurement will be made at household level or farm level.

### Cooperative Census
291. The cooperative census, which was crafted with climate, gender equality and social inclusion intent will be carried out by countries on a biennial basis; assessment evolution in membership and participation, social inclusion, sales and income, environmental and climatic challenges and access to assets (land, water, energy).

## Mid-Term and Final Evaluation
292. To provide an external viewpoint on the progress of the project and the achievement of its objectives, and in line with the AMA signed with the GCF two independent evaluations will be conducted by FAD - mid-term and final evaluation.
In line with the GCF policy on evaluations, the interim reviews and final evaluations may also be managed by FAD Office of Evaluation or decentralized (under the responsibility of decentralized offices).
In both cases, the project management team will be responsible for ensuring that the evaluation process is carried out in accordance with the GCF's policies.
The project mid-term review will follow the GCF's Integrated Results Management Framework, normally containing an assessment of the programme's potential and its evolving environment. The projects beneficiary surveys will constitute important inputs for the interim and final evaluations.
Please refer to Annex 11 for the detailed M&E plan.

# F: Risk Assessment and Management

## 1. Risk factors and mitigation measures (max. 3 pages)

### 293. The project will be subject to risks for this program, including an evaluation of financial, technical, operational and institutional risks that may prevent the project from achieving its objectives. An environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA) has been conducted on all project components. A detailed legal opinion by AE has conducted due diligence assessment on all joint partners and executing entities. FAO confirms that the project will not be subject to any of the listed sanctions under the United Nations Security Council Resolutions. All partners are subject to or impacted by any United Nations Security Council Sanctions. No entity or individual in the project area is subject to any of the listed sanctions under the United States Executive Order 13224 and Consolidated Sanctions list. This will be reviewed annually on the basis of updated lists of sanctioned entities during the implementation period.

### 294. There is a low risk of reversal and no risk of leakage (the carbon sink creating activities of this project). All activities designed to reduce GHG emissions from agricultural production and processing are also designed to ensure that the land use change does not result in an increase in GHG emissions, nor any other potential for continued agricultural production in the area. Regarding the risk of reversal, this applies to the emission reduction activities under Component 1 (land tenure and land conflict) and Component 2 (land governance). To mitigate this risk, the project will ensure that all land use changes are consistent with the land use plan and to agroforestry or any other increases in land cover achieved by the project. The risk of reversal arises from unforeseen circumstances such as climate change, population growth, conflict, droughts, floods, fires, etc., and to sudden population changes (e.g. influx of migrants or refugees, pandemics). Measures to prevent reversal are included under Component 1 (land tenure and land conflict) and Component 2 (land governance), including by farmers, by ensuring that the economic benefits of improved landscape management are documented, seen and shared with all stakeholders in a transparent manner. The project will also ensure that any land use changes under climate resilient, low-carbon practices fetch fair prices and attract more financing than others. The document provides an overview of the risks associated with this project. It is important to note that projects under climate change adaptation are subject to some level of risk. This document will also serve as a means to assess any risk of reversal or leakage during project implementation because it is not possible to predict all unforeseen circumstances.

### 295. Main risks identified for this project are listed in the table below.

| Selected Risk Factor | Land tenure, land ownership or land conflict |
| --- | --- |
| Category | Governance |
| Probability | Medium |
| Impact | Medium |

#### Description

Ousted land regimes, limited access to land registries and information, poor dispute resolution, poor enforcement of laws and regulations, lack of awareness on the rights and responsibilities associated with land governance in Kenya. Weak land use planning and insecure land tenure have prevented farmers from investing in long-term land management practices (e.g., agroforestry or soil conservation) that can reduce vulnerability to climate change. It also discourages long-term planning in favour of short-term gains, which is a major barrier for the adoption of low-carbon agricultural practices.

#### Mitigation Measures

1. Identification of customary tenure rights and right holders, including the identification of customary use of land and resources (e.g., access, cultivation or sustainably managed natural or customary use of land and natural resources (Component 2). In case the project confirms the presence of Indigenous Peoples in the project target area, the project will ensure that the relevant laws and related policies will be applied to these Indigenous Peoples who might be impacted by the project intervention.

# Selected Risk Factor 2 Market reorientation and supply chain constraints in selected value chains

## Category: Technical and operational
### Probability: Low
### Impact: Medium

**Description**

There is a very low risk that the national food market would see a change in demand in the commodities targeted by this project. For coffee and tea, demand projections based on current global trends have shown steadily increasing urbanization, even despite market shocks and the pandemic. However, supply chains may be constrained due to uncertainties related to climate change (e.g., droughts, floods) and other factors such as transport costs and output barriers (duties to acquisition of inputs, and barriers to delivery of outputs). This is highly unlikely, as most of these commodities are produced in areas with relatively stable weather patterns.

## Mitigation Measures

Measures to mitigate the risk embedded within this scenario include: (a) the project will work well and for farmers to demonstrate the quality and economic value of outputs produced using climate resilient, low-carbon technologies. This will be done through the development of the integrated landscape management plan (ILMP), which will provide appropriate price-setting as part of activity 4.2.1; (b) this will also assist cooperatives in understanding and assessing the market demand for their products and services, including coffee and tea. The project will also support the creation of values for brands as a service given to farmers and cooperatives under activity 1.1.4. This will serve the dual purpose of increasing the quality of the product and improving its value; (c) the project will provide training on informing county-level trade and development policy to better support farmers in their responses to market shocks.

# Selected Risk Factor 3 Decreasing national budgets for management of natural resources

## Category: Technical and operational
### Probability: Low
### Impact: Medium

**Description**

There is a small risk, given the increasing demands on national budgets and given that some of the sectors such as Forestry have not yet undergone full devolution in Kenya, that national and decentralized budgets for the project will be reduced. This would result in less funding being available to support the project activities. County governments would progressively divest themselves from the conservation and management of natural resources (e.g., forests) due to the high level of competition with other sectors such as agriculture and livestock production, contrary to the project's objectives and the country's best interest. This risk is very low - given the historical high levels of funding for these activities in Kenya (e.g., forest conservation and management, wildlife protection and orientation), the presence of a solid corpus of national laws related to conservation and natural resources management, and the fact that the project will be implemented through county governments with their own revenue streams. National and county budgets and priorities are set annually and enshrined in the Budget Policy Statement (BPS) for each fiscal year. The BPS is prepared by the Ministry of Finance and Economic Development in the medium term, including macro-economic forecasts; (b) the financial outlook with respect to Government revenues and expenditures is reviewed on a quarterly basis as part of the National Treasury's Financial Management System; (c) expenditure limits for the national government, and indicative transfers to county governments: (d) the fiscal revenue and financial objectives are set annually in the BPS including limits to total annual debt; (e) fiscal risk statement".

## Mitigation Measures

The project includes measures to build the country's capacity to understand the risks to its natural resources posed by climate change, and to develop strategies for adaptation. This will be done through the development of the integrated landscape management plan (ILMP), which will provide appropriate price-setting as part of activity 4.2.1; (b) this will also assist cooperatives in understanding and assessing the market demand for their products and services, including coffee and tea. The project will also support the creation of values for brands as a service given to farmers and cooperatives under activity 1.1.4. This will serve the dual purpose of increasing the quality of the product and improving its value; (c) the project will provide training on informing county-level trade and development policy to better support farmers in their responses to market shocks.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 89

## Technical and operational

| Description | Low | Low |
| --- | --- | --- |

There is a possibility that changes in monetary policy may impact financial institutions' ability to lend to project beneficiaries, which will make finance more difficult to access or could constrain the availability of supply for goods and services. The probability of this occurring is low (10%). The proportion of banks required by law to keep a specified proportion of their total deposits at the Central Bank (5.25%). The Central Bank's ability to influence interest rates through open market operations, as well as its capacity to provide liquidity in Kenya in March 2023 is also high, at 9.6%. Changes in these conditions will impact the project's banks' and financial institutions' ability to lend to project beneficiaries. Monetary policy decisions are made by a monetary policy committee (MPC). The MPC meets at least once every three months. The MPC has the authority to change interest rates or other monetary policy instruments, subject to the principle for the Central Bank to maintain annual price stability. Monetary policy is guided by a monetary policy framework that sets out the objectives and principles of monetary policy.

### Selected Risk Factor 5: Factors (collaboration between Government stakeholders)

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

#### Description

Limited collaboration between stakeholders from different ministries (e.g. Ministry of Agriculture - MoA, Ministry of Environment and the National Treasury as well as the county governments) on land restoration activities and the coordination of their respective policies and programs will result in limited information and knowledge-sharing on climate resilience practices and technologies, and ultimately lower the probability of risk.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)

All actors will be engaged in the Project Implementation Committee (PIC) and Project Technical Coordination Committee (PTCC) to ensure that government organizations work hand-in-hand. At local (county level), all actors will be involved in the County Climate Change Action Plan (CCCAP). This will enhance collaboration between all government stakeholders. These actions will lower the probability of risk.

### Selected Risk Factor 6: Resistance over women's participation and empowerment

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Low | Description |

The project may encounter passive or active resistance to women's participation as well as when working to achieve gender equality in the project. This will lower the probability of risk.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)

The project targeting mechanisms including group-based and self-targeting mechanisms will promote women's participation including vulnerable groups such as female head of households, female youth and girls, and pregnant women. The project will also support women's empowerment through capacity building and gender transformation activities. Women's Leadership Youth leadership trainings developed by AgriNet as well as the Gender Equality Committee (GEC) will be used to promote women's participation in the project. This includes the inclusion of women vulnerable groups in production, processing and value addition, and trade. During the Women's Empowerment Action Plan (WEAP), women will be involved in all aspects of the project design and together with male and female farmers an action plan is developed to improve the participation of women in the project. The WEAP will also include a monitoring and evaluation framework that includes a gender analysis process to monitor the progress made on GEVE and take necessary corrective actions as appropriate.

G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

296. The project is aligned with the FAD Environmental and Social Standards (2022) and GCF policies including the GCF Policy on the Prevention and Protection from Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (PPS), the GCF Project Preparation Policy (2018), the GCF Policy on the Prevention and Protection from Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (PPS), the GCF Information Disclosure Policy (2019), GCF Policy on the Prevention and Protection from Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (PPS), and the GCF Project Preparation Policy (2018). The project is planned at the community level in the project area through period 2021-2023, considering GCF (2022) guidance on development of environmental and social safeguard policies.

297. The project is expected to predominantly generate substantial positive environmental and social benefits, including improved livelihoods for smallholder farmers, increased food security, reduced vulnerability to climate change, reduce GHG emissions from Agriculture, Forestry and Land Use sector (AFOLU), strengthen land use planning and management practices in the project area to generate adverse social and environmental impacts that need to be carefully managed and monitored.

298. The FAO Project Environmental and Social Screening Checklist was prepared, and the project was categorized as a Medium Risk category.

299. There are identified potential adverse environmental and social impacts requiring the need for environmental and social safeguards. These include: (i) increased use of agrochemicals in the project area; (ii) increased waste from agro-industrial activities; indirect trigger for increased pesticides use (even when IPM is being promoted); and (iii) increased land use change due to agricultural expansion. The Environmental and Social Safeguard Policy 2, will trigger the following Environmental and Social Safeguard Policies: ESS1, ESS2, ESS4, ESS5, ESS7, ESS8, ESS9.

300. Potential impacts are limited to the project’s footprint, not unprecedented in the project area, never irreversible nor cumulative with other projects or activities. The project will implement best practices and good land use abatement practices that include FAO guidelines as well as World Bank Group Environmental Health and Safety Guidelines (EHS) and ILO.

301. Project activities are likely to be subject to project screening process (using FAO Screening checklist) to identify potential EAS risks and impacts. Depending on the classification and level of risk identified, Environmental and Social Safeguards will be implemented as per the GCF Policy on the Prevention and Protection from Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (PPS). It is expected that only some of the project activities will require ESM following the screening phase. Additionally, all projects are subject to a detailed environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA) which includes necessary construction and/or operation permits and licenses.

302. The country has ratified the United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples and has yet to ratify Convention 169 of the International Labor Organization; the country is yet to ratify the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights (ICCPR) and the International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights (ICESCR). The country has ratified the (i) International Convention on the Elimination Of All Forms of Racial Discrimination (ICERD); (ii) the Convention Against Torture and Other Cruel, Inhuman or Degrading Treatment or Punishment (CAT); (iii) the Convention on the Rights of the Child (CRC). Kenya does not have specific legislation on Indigenous Peoples, and does not identify with any particular group. The country has a policy that recognizes the rights of indigenous communities to their traditional customs and belief systems and way of life, as marginalized (these communities include mostly hunter-gatherers).

303. Participants to the project will be invited to self-identify as members of a vulnerable, indigenous, ethnic or religious minority and specific measures are included to support meaningful participation of Indigenous People in the project. The project will implement the Free Prior Informed Consent (FPIC) process for Indigenous Peoples, a Free, Prior and Informed Consent (FPIC) process will be implemented following FAO guidelines; to ensure an effective FPIC process, the FAO has developed a tool that will be used by the project team. The Indigenous People’s Planning Framework (IPPF) has been included within the project Environmental and Social Management Framework.

304. The overall ESM budget (Annex E is $697,356 for a blend of human resources to respond to ad-hoc requests from the project team and to provide technical assistance in the implementation of environmental activities budget items. ESM compliance and Gap monitoring are also included in the baseline, mid- and end-line surveys.
