# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 84

## Stakeholder Engagement

### Identification of Stakeholders

The identification of stakeholders was done using a collaborative approach, mobilizing Kenyan government authorities and other relevant stakeholders to identify the project’s target audience and understand each stakeholder's potential influence on benefits from, and contribution to the project.

### Initial Engagement

The initial engagement with stakeholders was done through exchanges between The National Treasury who is the NDCU, and F4C based on the basis of the project's proposed regional and thematic scope. Subsequent discussion expanded this list by including other relevant stakeholders such as county governments, local government authorities, national-level women have helped in refining the concept note and identifying the inclusion of relevant actors.

### Key Actors

- **Farmers:** Individual smallholder farmers involved in the six value chains namely coffee, tea, dairy, African honey bees, livestock, and horticulture. The project also identified vulnerable groups who participate in project activities will be the object of particular attention.
- **Farmers' Groups:** This includes informal self-help groups, business units, farming groups, community forest associations, farmer field schools and cooperatives in the six value chains, as well as value chain actors across all sectors. The project also identified vulnerable groups who participate in project activities.
- **Farmer Organizations:** Farmers are also direct and indirect beneficiaries of the project.

### Governance

The project will be implemented through a decentralized approach and public administration in all the devolved sectors. This includes ward-level decentralized administration and services. Counties are directly responsible for implementing the project at the local level.

### Regional (LREB) Institutions

- LREB secretariat
- Relevant caucuses such as agriculture, water resources, environment, livestock, fisheries, and wildlife.
- Water Service Boards: Lake Victoria North and South Water Service Boards, as well as knowledge sharing platforms on various topics.
- National Government Departments:
  - The National Treasury (NT)
  - State Department of Agriculture
  - State Department of Environment
  - State Department of Livestock
  - State Department of Fisheries
  - State Department of Natural Resources and Wildlife
  - Kenya Agriculture and Livestock Research Organization (KALRO)
  - Department of Resource Survey and Management

### Non-State Actors

- Non-Government Organizations, Faith Based Organizations, community workers, and other stakeholders.

### Financial Institutions

This includes community based credit and savings cooperatives, savings groups, as well as financial institutions such as banks, microfinance institutions, and insurance companies.

## Process for Designing the Project

A participatory process for the design of this project was conducted starting in late 2021, culminating in April 2023. This process involved gathering ideas, suggestions, inputs, and contributions from stakeholders identified through the stakeholder identification process. The results of these consultations and feedback received are included in Annex 7.

### Key Components

- Assessing the project's technical components to meet the needs and gaps.
- Understanding and defining the technical components of the project were also deployed.
- These include in-person and virtual surveys, interviews with key stakeholders, and focus group discussions.

## Engagement During Implementation

During implementation, the project will make every effort to provide relevant information to stakeholders in appropriate and timely manner.

### Communication Methods

Communication will be done using a variety of media such as print (newspapers, posters), radios, public meetings and workshops to ensure maximum reach to stakeholders. Particular attention will be paid to the inclusion of vulnerable groups who participate in project activities through targeted communication and traditionally excluded groups, including women, elderly people, indigenous/ethnic groups and persons living with disabilities.

### Grievance Redress Mechanism

The project will also include a Grievance Redress Mechanism and for the use of portrait or image-based communication in public spaces.

## Engagement Details

Details of how each stakeholder and stakeholder group is to be engaged are included in Annex 7.

### G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### 314. The Gender Assessment documented the current state of women in Kenya compared to men, as well as how gender intersects with other dimensions such as age, youth, disability status, and household headship status. It also highlighted the barriers that prevent women from engaging in agricultural value chains in the context of a changing climate.

The assessment revealed that women are more vulnerable than men due to their lower levels of education, limited access to productive resources (e.g., land), and under-represented and vulnerable groups in CVA value chains. Based on how their gender interacts with other social dimensions such as age, youth, disability status, and household headship status, the assessment identified a need for an effort to build intersectional-equity principles into its targeting metrics.

To ensure that female youth, female-headed households, women living with disabilities, and women in rural areas are not left behind, the Gender Action Plan (GAP) targets gender inequalities across several priority areas in climate-resilient agriculture. These include: increasing access to productive resources; enhancing access, agency, women's leadership, and voice in climate-smart agriculture.

#### 317. The Gender Action Plan (GAP) targets gender inequalities across several priority areas in climate-resilient agriculture. These include: increasing access to productive resources; enhancing access, agency, women's leadership, and voice in climate-smart agriculture).

The plan aims to mainstream gender equality into all project activities by ensuring that women living with disabilities have all specific quotas that are required for each activity. Mainstreaming in terms of participation is expected to increase the number of women participating in agricultural value chains from 10% (relative to the relative population sizes at the national level: 50%, 18.69%), FHT for women (LWD, and 27.32%) to 40% (relative to the relative population sizes at the national level: 50%, 18.69%). The quota is expected to increase from 25% (total project population) and 1% for men (LWD of total project population). Mainstreaming in terms of leadership is expected to increase women's representation on boards and committees, and gender-transformative concepts into 100% of training materials. These training materials include the "gender- and youth-sensitive agricultural extension services" and "women's empowerment and youth leadership," as well as more general training materials on agricultural production, markets, finance, etc.

## Section 321: Financial Management and Procurement of Goods, Services using GCF resources adheres to relevant FAQ notes and regulations, as well as relevant provisions in the Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) between the Green Climate Fund (GCF) Secretariat and the Accredited Entity.

### Subsection 321.1: Financial Management

- **FAD has deployed an Oracle-based Enterprise Resource Planning (ERP) system**, the Global Resources Management System (GRMS), to manage financial resources, procurement, and finance functionalities.
- GRMS improves the flow of financial information, supporting the GCF’s requirement for transparency in financial reporting.

### Subsection 321.2: Procurement

- Direct contracts are awarded by the Accredited Entity using Letters of Agreement (LoA) or Letters of Intent (LOI).
- Services and Works are contracted through Letters of Agreement.
- The Global Procurement Service, which operates under the GCF Procurement Team, provides operational and advisory support to ensure that the Organization procures goods, services, works, and other items in accordance with agreed standards and practices.

### Subsection 321.3: Financial Management and Procurement

- Financial management and procurement executed by an Accredited Entity acting as Executive Agent for GCF projects will be overseen and supervised by the FAD-GCF project team.
- The FAD-GCF project team supports underwriting, financial planning, and reporting activities to ensure that the project performs frequent spot checks and audits on financial and procurement activities.

### Subsection 321.4: Controls

- Controls against money laundering, terrorist financing, and prohibited practices will be fully exercised by FAQ auditors in accordance with relevant laws and other Conformity Practices.
- The FAD office of the Inspector General handles complaints related to:
    - Unauthorized access to FAQ activities (e.g., bribery, embezzlement, solicitation/acceptance of bribes or kickbacks).
    - Misrepresentation, forgery, or false certification in connection with any business.
    - Fraudulent claims for reimbursement, including but not limited to fraudulent claims for rental subsidy, education grant, travel, medical insurance.
    - False statements on the GCF application form, financial information, or misconduct related to contract bids, performance of contract obligations or evaluation.

### Subsection 321.5: Staff Violations

- Violations by staff will be subject to disciplinary action in accordance with the Organization’s Disciplinary Policy.
- Staff violations of the Standards of Conduct for International Civil Service.
- Complaints regarding sexual harassment and abuse of authority.

(b) The project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with the national priorities, strategies and plans of Kenya;

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP255

## Proposal name: Transforming Livelihoods through Climate Resilient, Low Carbon, Sustainable Agricultural Value Chains in the Lake Region Economic Bloc, Kenya

### Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations

### Country: Kenya

### Project/programme size: Small

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. **The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:**


| Strengths | Points of caution |
|-----------|-------------------|
| This cross-cutting project is building on demonstrated approaches of working with cooperatives and smallholder farmers in the Kenyan agricultural sector, while also supporting the development of new partners to build resilience and organize better. |  |

| The project proposes a value-chain approach for multiple crops including staple crops and cash crops - providing end-to-end support to climate-affected producers and value-chain actors. |  |

2. **The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal in accordance with the term sheet agreed between the Secretariat and the accredited entity (AE) and, if considered appropriate, subject to the conditions set out therein. It is document GGE/19/2.**


## II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### 2.1 Project background

3. **This project, "Transforming Livelihoods through Climate Resilient, Low Carbon, Sustainable Agricultural Value Chains in the Lake Region Economic Bloc, Kenya (CRLCS)," aims to transform livelihoods and increase food security for smallholder farmers in the Lake Region Economic Bloc by 2025. The project will be implemented using a locally-led approach. It leverages the private sector, smallerholder farming networks, and the cooperative movement’s strengths in Kenya to establish a framework for technology transfer and capacity building of smallholder farmers through the development of climate-resilient farming networks. With a focus on six priority value chains (dairy, poultry, coffee, tea, fruit trees, and vegetables), it will provide access to climate-smart adaptation and mitigation technologies at every stage, from production and harvesting to processing and marketing.**


---
**Page 99**
---

The project is organized around four main outcomes/components. Outcome 1 focuses on strengthening county-level government services to support the expansion of climate-resilient, low-carbon and sustainable value chains, emphasizing how governments can assist cooperatives and smallholder farmers in accessing finance for investments in resilient agriculture. The project will focus on transferring production, harvest and processing technologies directly to farmers and their organizations to reduce emissions, enhance resilience and optimize natural resource management (activity 1.2).

Under Outcome 4 the project aims to improve farmers' market access by partnering with value chain actors such as Kenya Coffee Producers’ Association (KCPC), Kenya Tea Development Agency (KTDA) and Kenya Sugar Board (KSB). The project will also provide credit and bridging risk mitigation through Kenya Commercial Bank (a direct access entity in Kenya), Co-operative Bank of Kenya, and Equity Bank. Additional grant co-financing is provided by the Government of Denmark, the European Union, and the Global Environment Facility (GEF) Trust Fund for Climate Change Adaptation of Kenya. The project will be implemented through FAO at the AE, with FAO and Agrifrica serving as implementing partners to ensure that the project complements existing activities it co-finances. The project aims to directly benefit 572,000 individuals, primarily smallholder farmers, including both men and women, and will also indirectly benefit 2.1 million individuals.

### Component-by-component analysis

#### Component 1: Enabling public support of adaptation and mitigation (total cost: USD 2.44 million; GCF cost: USD 1.97 million)

- **Component 1** of this project focuses on enhancing climate-resilient and socially inclusive agricultural practices by developing innovative, gender-responsive extension methods to disseminate climate-smart agriculture technologies and services for underserved communities, including women, youth and persons with disabilities, through existing service delivery mechanisms (activity 1.2.3).
- Additionally, the project will develop decentralized carbon accounting methodologies, update agricultural databases, and assess climate solutions tailored for the agriculture sector (activities 1.2.4). The development of a national database and mapping existing platforms will also support broader adaptation and learning (activity 1.1.6).

#### Component 2: Sustainable resilient agricultural landscapes (total cost: USD 13.61 million; GCF cost: USD 9.75 million)

- Under **Component 2**, the project aims to create county and regional strategies for climate-resilient, low-carbon agricultural landscape management, focusing on enhanced water harvesting and soil conservation practices in semi-arid areas (activity 2.1.3). These strategies will be implemented and monitored to ensure effective climate adaptation and mitigation actions are taken at scale.

#### Component 3: Resilient livelihoods (total cost: USD 25.64 million; GCF cost: USD 22.09 million)

- **Component 3** will provide CRUSA production and processing assets, along with training and capacity building for the development of climate-resilient and low-carbon resilience (activity 3.1.1). It aims to spread CRUSA technology and knowledge through cooperative networks and peer exchanges, encouraging smallholder farmers to form cooperatives as a means to increase their bargaining power in the market place (activity 3.2.1). The project also emphasizes improving social inclusion and fostering women’s active participation within climate-smart agriculture value chains.

#### Component 4: Scaling through CBLCSA market and finance (total cost: USD 4.16 million; GCF cost: USD 1.97 million)


---
**Page 100**
---

## Assessment of performance against investment criteria

### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: N/A

The project will support 72,000 vulnerable smallholder farmers whose livelihoods rely on one of the six most value chains in the region. This includes an expected reach of at least 80,000 cooperative members (project participants), benefiting approximately 325,000 individuals through direct and indirect benefits. The project will also include non-participating cooperatives within the six value chains (1,342,160 individuals), with additional benefits reaching extended family members of participating farmers. This brings the total immediate indirect beneficiaries to 2,089,161, with women representing nearly 57 percent of this group—nearly 15 per cent of Lake Region Block (LRB) households. The project will also reach smallholder farmers who have been across the lake region are anticipated, supported by an appealing strategy and the willingness of key stakeholders to support the project.

The project also aims to enhance health, well-being, and food and water security for the most vulnerable communities by improving adaptive capacities through climate-resilient and low-carbon agricultural practices. It will promote sustainable land management and conservation mechanisms within organized farmer groups and cooperatives. It will boost food and nutrition security in the region by increasing access to markets, reducing post-harvest losses, and promoting value addition and sale, thereby improving income through increased productivity, value addition, and market linkages.

The project also seeks to reduce exposure to climate risks like droughts and floods through watershed management, soil conservation and increased access to climate information


---
**Page 101**
---

## Paradigm shift potential

**Scale: N/A**

The project promotes a fundamental paradigm shift by transforming value chains from low productivity and resilience to high-performing, financially sustainable and climate-resilient low-carbon livestock systems. This is achieved through strengthening farmer organizations and cooperatives by empowering farmer organizations and cooperatives to identify climate change solutions and while facilitating the adoption of new technologies and practices that are economically enhancing climate resilience in their production and business practices. By ensuring that the promoted technologies and practices align with the business models of cooperatives, farmers adopt them at a rate that makes both the technology and business model viable.

Overall, the project has significant potential for scaling up and optimization, explaining knowledge-sharing and learning through training sessions, demonstrations, community meetings and field visits. It also aims to create an enabling environment for farmer organizations and cooperatives by strengthening their capacity and administrative delivery of climate-related market access services. This support will empower farmers to produce more and higher-quality products that are in demand on the global market. The project contributes to this enabling environment include improved climate information services and warnings, agricultural extension services, training and advisory services, adoption of new technologies, enhanced extension services, as well as market studies, information and sales data.

## Sustainable development potential

**Scale: N/A**

The project activities will have direct linkages to the following Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs): SDG 1: No poverty; SDG 2: Zero hunger; SDG 3: good health and well-being; SDG 5: gender equality; SDG 8: decent work and economic growth; SDG 13: climate action; and SDG 15: life on land. These goals will guide the project’s implementation and ensure that it contributes effectively to poverty reduction, food security, improved health outcomes, gender equality, health equity, sustainable economic growth, climate change and biodiversity conservation.

The project has a measurable co-benefit in job creation across the six value chains, while indirect benefits include increased economic activity in the supply chains for agricultural inputs and feed. The project also aims to provide meaningful employment opportunities for women, youth and persons living with disabilities in decision-making roles within cooperative societies and other relevant organizations.


---
**Page 102**
---

22. Additional indirect socioeconomic benefits encompass improved access to education for children, particularly for girls, whose workloads may be reduced or whose families may have greater financial resources to invest in their education.

23. The LERB also provides opportunities for women and youth to participate more fully in the economy through increased income generation from agroforestry systems, which can help to promote greater gender equality, decrease rates of gender-based violence and child labour, and enhance food security by employing women as key decision-makers on farms.

24. At the farm level, environmental benefits arise from implementing sustainable land management practices, anti-erosion initiatives, increased soil cover, crop diversification or rotation, reduced use of synthetic fertilizers and pesticides, and decreased use of fungicides, herbicides. These efforts lead to improved land productivity, increased biomass production, enhanced soil fertility, better soil moisture retention and microorganism content, and increased agrobiodiversity at both farm and landscape levels.

3.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: N/A

Smallerholder farmers, in particular in the LERB faces significant challenges due to interannual fluctuations in rainfall and increasing variability in rainfall, leading to reduced yields and unstable or unreliable incomes. The increasing variability in rainfall has exacerbated these issues, creating a situation where many smallholder farmers are unable to meet their food needs.

Furthermore, in the LERB, agricultural yields reflect low productivity due to climate variability and limited access to inputs. Many farmers operate with minimal technical support, low mechanization levels, and limited access to necessary inputs. Current practices largely rely on manual labour, which is time-consuming and water uses impacts.

On the other hand, smallholder farmers have very limited financial capacity to invest in their crop production. Cooperatives and farmer organizations typically face limited access to financing due to high eligibility requirements, elevated lending costs and challenges in presenting collateral or security for loans.

In order to overcome the challenges of climate vulnerability and limited financial availability, this project establishes a system where governments and the private sector work together with smallholder farmers to provide them with the necessary resources. This includes vulnerable smallholders receive training and resources for adopting the best climate-resilient, low-carbon agricultural practices that will increase their productivity in the short term while providing long-term benefits for adapting to climate changes, while also allowing smallholders to further reduce their vulnerability to climate change.

Cooperatives exit the project with members who all implement climate-resilient, low-carbon agriculture, have access to better assets, increase their market presence, and can integrate climate-smart practices into their business models. This will enable them to remain competitive and gain additional capacity to access private financing, supporting the model's long-term sustainability.


---
**Page 103**
---

3.5 Country ownership

The country ownership of this project is very high for several reasons: (i) it strongly supports the implementation of national climate change policies and strategies such as the National Climate Change Action Plan (2014-2019), the National Adaptation Programme of Action, and the National Adaptation Plan (2015–2030) of Kenya; (ii) the national designated authority (NDA) is involved in the project from the inception and project design stage. The NDA participated actively throughout the project cycle to ensure that all project inputs align with the project proposal with government priorities. Once the project is approved, the NDA will play a crucial role in ensuring its implementation; (iii) the Government of Kenya has also one of the EEs and through the National Treasury of Kenya has co-financed the project; (iv) involvement of several partners at the regional and national level throughout the project cycle; (v) the project is implemented by the NDA, which ensures that all stakeholders think place on multiple occasions, starting with the initial consultative meeting which brought together representatives from the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock, Ministry of Environment, agriculture and climate departments from 14 counties; and (v) numerous consultations were held at the local level with smallholder farmers including climate change surveys, individual interviews with regard to applications for the climate resilient and low-carbon technologies.

3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

The project proposal demonstrates the project's efficiency and effectiveness through a comprehensive economic and financial analysis. Both analyses have been conducted for its priority value chains – coffee, dairy, fruit trees, vegetables, poultry, and tea – under two climate scenarios: (i) a high-emission scenario; and (ii) a low-emission scenario.

In the context of the macroeconomic environment in Kenya, inflation has risen steadily, fluctuating moderately between 5 and 7 per cent over the past five years—a relatively stable trend compared to other countries in East Africa. The country is also experiencing droughts impacting agricultural productivity. The AE identifies access to finance as a key barrier preventing smallholder farmers from adopting climate-resilient technologies.

Economic viability in the long run is substantiated by the positive results of economic indicators across the six value chains. Economic benefits were valued based on gross revenue for each value chain, adjusted to economic prices using calculated conversion factors, along with reduced costs and avoided losses. The project has a net present value (NPV) of USD 120 million and an internal rate of return (IRR) of 40 per cent over the 20-year period. The project also achieves a social discount rate of 8 per cent over a 20-year period. Additional economic benefits were calculated for each value chain, including additional revenues (e.g., soil quality), which were not included in the model due to data limitations.

Financial viability over the long term is supported by the financial indicators showing that the same revenue stream as above would generate an NPV of USD 18.7 million with a financial IRR of 23.4 per cent and a 12-year payback period under RCP2.6. Under RCP2.6, the project has a net present value (NPV) of USD 50 million over the same time period, indicating the project's financial feasibility using an 18 per cent financial discount rate over 20 years.


---
**Page 104**
---

30. The financial model projects an average income increase of 20 per cent for farmers, with a conservative target of 15 per cent incorporated into the logical framework under Outcome 4. The model also indicates the financial viability of leveraged financing from Equity Bank and Cooperative Bank. These loans are repayable over a period of up to five years at an interest rate of 7.5 per cent, and no grace period for these agricultural loans; the investments are shown to be marginally profitable with a debt service coverage ratio exceeding 1.25 and a debt service coverage ratio exceeding 3, ensuring sufficient cash flows for debt servicing post-project completion.

30. The adaptation cost per direct beneficiary is USD 650, and USD 138 per total beneficiary, which are moderate within the GCF agricultural and adaptation portfolio. The mitigation cost per direct beneficiary is USD 247, and USD 51 per total beneficiary, which are also moderate within the GCF agricultural and adaptation portfolio. The co-financing ratio stands at 0.71. The proposal addresses the limited dissemination of best practices within the LERB and underlines the project's potential to expand and reinforce best practices in low emissions, climate-resilient agriculture among farmers in the region.

### IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

30. **Environmental and social risk category**: The AE has assigned the project an environmental and social (E&S) risk category of Category B based on its screening of the proposed project activities against the E&S criteria in the GCF’s E&S Framework. This assignment confirms that the risk category is within the AE's E&S risk accreditation level.

30. **Performance Standard PS 1**: Assessment and management of environmental and social risks and impacts. The project is expected to predominantly generate substantial positive E&S benefits through the adoption of climate-resilient, low-carbon sustainable agricultural practices and technologies. Some of these main benefits include strengthening farmers' resilience against adverse effects of climate change (mitigating climate risks and simultaneously improving their adaptive and coping capacity); and reducing GHG emissions from agriculture (e.g., by increasing soil carbon sequestration through agroforestry, reforestation, watershed management). Besides all positive impacts, the project may also generate some adverse social and environmental impacts that need to be carefully managed and monitored.

# PS3: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention

The project will promote practices and technologies that are aimed at improving efficiency in the use of natural resources (water, land, energy). The target groups for these activities include women, men, youth, elders, persons with disabilities and Indigenous Peoples. The project will incorporate gender equality principles into all its components, including awareness raising measures, restoration of water catchment areas and watershed management, conservation and restoration of wetlands, promotion of efficient use of natural resources (e.g., reducing the number of livestock per one large system; promoting water-saving irrigation techniques (drop irrigation, water-saving equipment), rainwater harvesting structures, and energy-efficient machinery and equipment (e.g. tractors, ventilation systems, drying and storage systems).

# PS4: Community health, safety and security

Community health and safety risks are likely to arise from exposure to agro-chemicals, water-borne diseases, domestic diseases, vector-borne diseases, and other environmental hazards. The project will address the marginalization of women, youth and persons with disabilities. Proposed mitigation measures include awareness raising on hygiene practices, provision of sanitation facilities, promotion of social distancing for potential diseases, adoption of good industry practices and waste management techniques, and establishment of a dedicated budget line for women, youth and vulnerable groups in selected value chains for each training, grant assistance or other activity. The project will also implement a participatory and demand-driven approach to assess community needs and develop support packages tailored to attend their needs, challenges and priorities. Education and awareness-raising with the local user communities on water quality monitoring techniques, including detection of water-related diseases, as well as intermittent water quality monitoring of the main water reservoirs, will also be embarked upon.

# PS5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement

Based on the Secretariat assessment of the A3's submission, project activities are unlikely to lead to involuntary resettlement. The land that is proposed for acquisition under this project will not be used for land acquisition and is thus further reinforced by an exclusion list. Proposed activities will imply the need for land acquisition only in exceptional cases.

The country reports historic difficulties related to land access and tenure security rights that could compromise the success of the project, particularly with regard to the conservation of natural resources among marginalized communities. Existing land uses and land ownership are considered important factors when selecting areas for conservation projects. Landowners who adopt technologies and practices, for example private landowners are more likely to conserve land as they are assured of retaining the long-term gains from investments in conservation. Security of tenure may also be a factor that influences the willingness of communities to participate in project activities, and can influence socioeconomic activities such as a change in the range of income generating activities.

# PS6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources

The activities promoted by the project are expected to generate substantial positive environmental impacts. These include the restoration of degraded ecosystems which not only the resilience of ecosystems and local communities to climate change, but also reducing land degradation and desertification. The project will achieve these objectives through an ecosystem-based approach. Considering that some agricultural and forestry activities could take place near or in the buffer zones of critical habitat or natural protected areas, there are


---
**Page 106**
---

### Gender policy

The AE has provided a gender analysis and action plan and therefore complies with the GCF gender policy. The gender assessment undertaken reveals key challenges faced by women in the EREPs (Economic Resource Enhancement Projects). Women are underrepresented within the agricultural labour force but face substantial barriers in accessing land, finance and agricultural

**Page 107**

## Risks

### 4.3.1 Overall project assessment (medium risk)

The proposed cross-cutting project will help address climate change impacts in 14 countries in Kenya (the LREE) by investing in climate-resilient low-carbon, sustainable agriculture and agroforestry systems that can increase the resilience of smallholder value chain participants in agriculture. Target GCF result areas are: forestry and land use; most vulnerable groups; and gender equality. The project is estimated to cost USD 50 million. GCF is requested to provide a grant of USD 29.2 million. Together with grant contributions of USD 14.8 million from the LREE, USD 3.6 million from the Kenyan government, USD 7.5 million from FAO, and USD 0.91 million from FMG, the total project cost is USD 50 million. The co-financing ratio is 1:3.01 and will account for 48 per cent of total financing.

### 4.3.2 Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current project (medium risk)

FAO and Agriterra will jointly share project implementation responsibility for this funding proposal. In addition to overall project management, FAO will serve as EE and handle the mobilization of co-financing resources from other donors. This includes identifying market opportunities and inclusive finance. On the other hand, Agriterra will be responsible for disseminating information on climate change adaptation strategies to farmers; business plan development and access to financial incentives for farmers.

FAO was accredited by GCF as an EE in 2016. FAO is a specialized agency established to fight hunger worldwide through its member countries' voluntary contributions. Agriterra is a non-profit agricultural agency that works on farmer organization and cooperative development. Agriterra provides training and sharing of expertise to farmers’ cooperatives by experts to strengthen governance, leadership, operational management and sustainable


---
**Page 108**
---

# Project-specific execution risks (medium risk)

## The proposed project aims to reduce climate emissions via conservation of the ecosystem, dissemination of technologies and assets, and reorientation of business practices towards more sustainable agricultural production.

### Financial risks

The Government reported larger budget deficits compared to that originally planned due to higher spending on social services and lower revenues from taxes. These revenue shortfalls put pressure on the national budget to support publicly financed projects. This concern is further compounded by the fact that the project will be co-financed with natural resources conservation, good access to concessional external financing, and no immediate external liquidity pressures; 4 per cent of total external debt is due in the next three years (with longer-term maturities). In addition, the US dollar has been depreciating against the United States dollar in the last five years and is projected to further depreciate by 4 per cent over the next two years. During this period, the project will be exposed to foreign currency depreciation and inflation risk which can lead to cost increases.

## Project viability and concessionality

### The proposed project has outlined numerous activities for a sustainable agricultural landscape and resilient livelihoods. These include activities in communities to strengthen data management, manage community land and agricultural land, improve soil conservation and technology adoption, and strengthen the capacity of local institutions. Both FAO as AE and EFC and Agriterra as an EE have extensive experience implementing these types of activities and should raise no concerns regarding their ability to implement them successfully.

### Compliance risk (low risk)

The GCF and Agriterra, a Netherlands-based cooperative development focused international non-governmental organization, will be the EFs for the activities funded by GCF proceeds, as well as those co-financed by FAO and the Government of Denmark. The Government of Ethiopia has committed to provide 10 per cent of total project costs that it co-finances (activity 2.1.2) and will not administer GCF proceeds as an AE. The proposed project activities generally present no risk for the loss of land tenure and terrestrial grazing (ML/TF).

### ML/TF risk

The AE has assessed that the ML/TF risk lies in the arrangements to be put in place such as agreements/governing the financing of services (AML/GFT) clauses in project subsidiary agreements, as well as its own AML/GFT controls. The AE also highlights that close monitoring and reporting will be required for any potential ML/TF risks through its Eastern Africa sub-regional office in Ethiopia and country representation office in Kenya.


---
**Page 109**
---

### GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk)

In case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF concentration risk remains within the limits established in the Risk Appetite Statement in terms of results areas, single proposal or AE concentration.

## Recommended risk rating

It is recommended that the Board consider the above factors in its decision.

| --- |
| Overall project | Medium |
| Accredited entity/executing entity capability to implement this project | Medium |
| Project-specific execution | Medium |

| Compliance | Low |

## Fiduciary

The Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO) will serve as the accredited entity (AE) for this project, responsible for overall management, including project approval, administrative oversight, financial supervision, and technical supervision throughout implementation. FAO will establish a Project Task Force to fulfill its AE functions, maintaining independence from executing entities.

The project will be co-executed by FAO and Agriterra as executing entities (EEs). FAO Kenya will serve as the lead EE for overall management of the project. As a global farmer advisory organization, it leverages its expertise in strengthening cooperatives and farmers' organizations. The Government of Kenya, through the National Treasury, will execute activities co-financed by GCF and the Kenyan government.

A Project Management Unit will be established in Nairobi with a regional antenna in Bomet County to manage project implementation on behalf of FAO and Agriterra. The Project Management Unit will coordinate with relevant stakeholders and support day-to-day activities.

The Project Steering Committee, chaired by the Principal Secretary of the Treasury and the Chief Executive Officer of Agriterra, will oversee overall project management. The Project Technical Coordinating Committee, chaired by FAO Kenya, will conduct monitoring, learning, reporting, and evaluation.

Regarding GCF proceeds, GCF proceeds received by FAO as AE will flow to the co-EEs (FAO Kenya and Agriterra) for project implementation. The arrangement is governed by various agreements between the GCF, FAO, and Agriterra, including a financial management and execution agreement between FAO and Agriterra, and co-financing agreement between FAO and Ministry of Foreign Affairs of the Kingdom of Denmark (KFA).

The Project Management Unit will follow FAO policies and regulations, utilizing its Global Resources Management System. FAO will oversee Agriterra's financial management and procurement processes to ensure compliance with GCF requirements. Audits will be performed by an internationally recognized auditing firm, selected competitively by FAO, in line with agreed standards and practices.

This pathway of change was clearly set out in the theory of change.

### 4.6 Legal assessment

The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the accredited entity on 1 June 2020 (the date of signature) and became effective on 1 October 2020. The accredited entity's five-year accreditation term lapsed on 3 October 2021 but was extended by Decision B/37.18, paragraph (g) until the earlier of three years from the date of lapse or the next year accreditation form or the date of signature of a new agreement with the GCF Secretariat. The Board approved the re-accreditation of the accredited entity pursuant to decision R/37.18 and negotiated the terms of the new agreement on 20 October 2021.

Therefore, it is recommended that the Board considers whether disbursements of GCF proceeds from this funding proposal should be subject to any restriction against litigation and expropriation in the country; or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities for GCF and its personnel.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP255

## Proposal name: Transforming Livelihoods through Climate Resilient, Low Carbon, Sustainable Agricultural Value Chains in the Lake Region Economic Bloc, Kenya*

Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations

Country: Kenya

Project/programme size: Small

### Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

1. This is the first submission of the funding proposal "Transforming Livelihoods through Climate Resilient, Low Carbon, Sustainable Agricultural Value Chains in the Lake Region Economic Bloc, Kenya" submitted to the independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) under the standard procedure for project proposals to the Global Environment Facility (GEF). FAO and Agrifrica will be executing entities (EEs) for GEF projects in Kenya. The Government of Kenya, acting through the National Treasury, will be the EE for sub-national projects.

2. This cross-cutting funding proposal is aimed at fostering the emergence of climate-resilient, low-carbon, environmentally sustainable, and financially viable agricultural value chains by supporting smallholder farmers to adopt new technologies and services with a focus on agrofood cooperatives as key agents to leverage rural change.

3. The expected mitigation outcome is 420,649 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (t CO2 eq) per year from the project activities. The project will address the mitigation impacts of forestry and land use to achieve this outcome.

4. The expected adaptation outcome includes 572,086 direct and 2,298,140 indirect beneficiaries in the climate-smart agriculture (CSA) adaptation result areas (ARRs): most vulnerable people and communities (AVPC), health and well-being, and food and water security (AWAS).

5. The project will achieve its mitigation and adaptation outcomes through activities organized in four components:

   * Development of adaptation and mitigation;
   * Sustainable resilient agricultural landscapes;
   * Resilient livelihoods; and
   * Scaling through climate-resilient, low-carbon, sustainable agriculture (CRLCSA) market and value chains.

6. The overall budget for the six years implementation period is USD 929,938,230 with a funding request to GEF of USD 215,309 as a grant. The Government of Kenya will contribute USD 1 million in cash and USD 47,060,000 in kind. The World Bank will provide USD 985,915 co-financing to this funding proposal, all in the form of grants.


---
**Page 112**
---

1. In its assessment, the ITAP considered all documents as submitted on 4 November 2024, the answers to the ITAP as received on 25 November 2024 and the additional information provided during the call between the ITAP, the AE and the GCF Secretariat on 27 November 2024.

1.1 **Impact potential** Scale: Medium to high

The funding proposal addresses climate change mitigation and adaptation in 14 countries of the Land-Use Research and Education System (LURES) network.

* **Component 1** focuses on improving the enabling conditions for CRLCSA across all 14 countries and enhancing the public agro-climate services support services. The project will develop and test a set of tools and methodologies to improve the capacity of CRLCSA to provide climate-related agricultural production and the cooperative system, prepare Climate Technology Green Lists (Green Lists) that include technologies approaches or practices that can face climate risks and enhance resilience in the sector.

* **Component 2** aims at reducing emissions from and increasing adaptive capacity of the sector on agriculture, forestry and other land uses (AFOLU), as well as an increasing ecosystem resilience to climate change. To achieve these outcomes the project will develop, implement and monitor a set of tools and methodologies that include technologies approaches or practices that can face climate risks and enhance resilience in the sector.

* **Component 3** focuses on increasing climate resilience of the smallholders' livelihoods. The project will facilitate the adoption of gender-responsive and socially inclusive CRLCSA by providing training and assets to farmers and farmers’ organisations, disseminating knowledge and best practices, supporting the development of a market for agricultural products, facilitating smallholders’ aggregation into cooperatives and supporting women’s participation.

* **Component 4** focuses on increasing climate resilience through market development and the development of gender-responsive and socially inclusive private finance tools. Activities under this component focus on identifying mechanisms to enhance market access for smallholder farmers in the six value chains selected for this project: tea, coffee, fruit trees (avocado and banana), African-leafy vegetables, dairy and poultry, as well as an analysis of the climate impact

13. The funding proposal includes detailed and robust information about the three key factors determining climate risks for the agricultural sector in LUBE: climate hazards, exposure and adaptive capacity.

14. The funding proposal documents historical trends in temperature, precipitation and extreme events over the last 50 years to assess current climate conditions at the agricultural sector based on the level of exposure and the vulnerability of crops, livestock and associated livelihoods. It also includes an analysis of projected climate impacts on agricultural systems for the next 30 years (2041-2070) in terms of temperature increase, precipitation change and extreme events. The analysis covers potential climate impacts on the six value chains selected for this project: tea, coffee, fruit trees (avocado and banana), African-leafy vegetables, dairy and poultry, as well as an analysis of the climate impact

## Mitigation potential

The updated NDC of Kenya (2020) states that the country seeks to abate its GHG emissions by 32 per cent and that, subject to national circumstances, Kenya intends to bear 21 per cent of this cost. The Government of Kenya has co-funded this project with 28 per cent of the total budget and that over *90* per cent of this contribution will be invested in field activities under Component 2.

1. See FAO (2016). RIMA II: Resilience Index Monitoring and Analysis II. <https://www.fao.org/3/a-i5874e.pdf>
2. https://www.greenclimatefunds.org/sites/gcf/files/publication/CRSLCA%20-%20Country%20Action%20Plan%20-%20Kenya%20-%20Final%20Draft%20-%2015%20May%202017.pdf
3. https://unfccc.int/sites/default/files/resource/CRSLCA_2017-2022_Kenya_National_Designated_Organization_NDC_2020.pdf
4. <https://www.greenclimatefunds.org/sites/gcf/files/publication/CRSLCA%20-%20Country%20Action%20Plan%20-%20Kenya%20-%20Final%20Draft%20-%2015%20May%202017.pdf>

21. Furthermore, the low carbon technologies and practices included in this proposal are aligned with the priority mitigation activities listed in the updated NDC 2020, especially those regarding making progress toward the achievement of at least 10 per cent of the land area under tree cover by 2030 through the use of agroforestry systems (AFS) and natural soil sinks (NSS) for mitigation and promoting climate-smart agriculture.

22. The funding proposal uses a comprehensive set of tools for quantifying the impact potential of the project. On one side, the EX-ACT Carbon Tool (EX-ACT) for crop-based activities and on the other Global Livestock Environment Assessment Model (GLEAM) for livestock emissions. Both models are based on methodologies behind them, have been developed by FAO, widely reviewed, open sourced and have been validated.

23. Annex 22a of the funding proposal explains the assumptions for quantifying the GHG emissions in the baseline and in the project scenario, differentiating by assumptions relevant to the specific activities covered under the project. The livestock emission factor (LEF) (to be calculated with EX-ACT) and management of dairy cattle and management of poultry (to be calculated using GLEAM) are based on the national reference emission factors (REFs), potential with EX-ACT and annex 22c: the calculation with GLEAM. The ITAP acknowledges the completeness, clarity and coherence of the methodological approach and the well-documented calculations.

24. Annex 11 explains how the project will monitor the mitigation impact of the project at the county level using the national MRV system. The AE stated that “the MRV system at the project level will align with Kenya’s integrated national MRV framework by adopting the same shorter reference timeline, consistent emission factors and reporting requirements, as well as similar monitoring timelines. Emission factors used in the project will mirror those applied in the national framework. However, the project will use specific county-level emission factors that are specific for the LEER but not captured by the existing national MRV default values through the use of decentralized carbon accounting methods and the possible use in NRW systems at other levels.” The AE further stated “the project will contribute to the national MRV system in Kenya. This approach ensures the project’s MRV system is consistent with the national MRV system, which enhances the accuracy and reliability of emissions reporting while supporting continuous improvement and alignment with Kenya’s climate action goals.” The project will collaborate with the county administrators to improve the capacity for MRV at the county level through the use of decentralized carbon accounting methods.

25. The ITAP is concerned about how the project will secure the timely distribution of seeds and other planting material essential for ensuring the adoption of CRCLCA practices outlined in the Green Climate Fund (GCF) proposal. The AE stated that “the project does not provide any information on how the project will ensure the availability of seeds and other planting material.” The AE also mentioned that the establishment of nurseries or the provision of seeds in the activities is not a requirement for CRCLCA practices, but it is recommended to work with community forest associations and cooperatives, but this answer does not clarify the availability of seeds and other planting material. The AE further stated “the project does not mention any measures that will ensure the availability of seeds and other planting material.” The AE also mentioned in the funding proposal. Reduced availability of seeds and other vegetal material can cause delays in achieving the adaptation and mitigation impact potential of this funding proposal.

1.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

Several aspects of this project can contribute to a shift towards sustainable climate-resilient and low-carbon commodity production LCRB. The funding proposal is designed to overcome these barriers and accelerate the transition to a climate-resilient, low-carbon pathway. These barriers include insufficient public services for farmers and cooperatives to enable value chain actors to adopt CRIECSA, limited access to land-use planning and landscape management at county level, limited capacity of smallholder farmers to access appropriate agricultural technologies and inputs, the mismatch between supply and demand of finance to support smallholder farmers' adoption of climate-resilient low-carbon production systems. The funding proposal is proposed to propose clear pathways to address and overcome these barriers.

Further, the funding proposal leverages Kenya's devolution process by strengthening the institutional capacity of county governments in providing public services and methodologies that support value chain actors in adopting CRIECSA. This way the country administration will be able to provide a conducive environment for the adoption of CRIECSA in the project area.

The project will also support smallholder farmers in increasing their competences, implementing climate-resilient, low-carbon landscape management, and sustainable agricultural technologies proposed in Components 2 and 3. and will strengthen their organizational capacity through improved access to markets and mobilize new financing mechanisms that facilitate upscaling the adoption of new business models.

The funding proposal includes various learning and exchange mechanisms, both as part of the implementation and as a part of monitoring, evaluation and learning.

Considering the above, the ITAP assesses the paradigm impact potential of this funding proposal as high.


1.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

The funding proposal can contribute to multiple Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), including SDG 2 - zero hunger, SDG 5 - gender equality, SDG 8 - decent work and economic growth, SDG 13 - climate action and SDG 15 - life on land.

Potential socioeconomic co-benefits of this project include better and more sustainable income generation for smallholder farmers through increased productivity and improvement of food produced for own consumption and access to better food due to increased income. In addition, the proposal introduces a gender-responsive and socially inclusive approach in the implementation of CRIECSA. The funding proposal will also present a detailed diagnostic of gender challenges at the national and county levels as well as along the six selected value chains. This will be used to develop a Gender Action Plan that addresses leadership and capacity by country and value chain. The proposal includes a Gender Action Plan development component in Component 1, which is expected to mainstream gender into CRIECSA. The activities mentioned in the Gender Action Plan are included in both the project description in section B of the main document and in the project budget (Annex 4). Annex 8 also


---
**Page 116**
---

clarifies project goals, the roles and responsibilities, the specific budget and the monitoring regarding the gender-responsive and socially inclusive activities.

35. The project can provide environmental co-benefits at both the landscape and farm levels: at the landscape level the reforestation activities (Component 2) can improve forest cover resulting in increased carbon sequestration and biodiversity conservation; and at the farm level, activities included in Component 3 will increase biological diversity and soil health.

36. Considering the above, the ITAP assesses the sustainable impact potential of this funding proposal as high.


### Needs of the recipient
Scale: High

37. The International Monetary Fund estimates that the population of Kenya is 2014 was 52.4 million with a rural population over 70 per cent of the total and a Human Development Index of 0.48.

38. Kenya, a lower-middle-income leading economy in East Africa, improved its economic performance thanks to both market reforms and a successful translation of economic growth into poverty reduction. The country’s GDP grew at an average rate of 5.7 per cent between 2010/16, but this trend was reversed initially after 2015/16 with a strong setback due to national circumstances (e.g., droughts and floods) and the global economic slowdown in 2014/16, followed by the pandemic, especially in urban areas. Major development challenges include: poverty reduction; improving access to basic services; climate variability and climate change impacts on rain-fed agriculture (annex 2 part B-C).

39. Over the last decade, Kenya has prioritized activities to mitigate and adapt to climate change through a number of initiatives aimed at increasing resilience in communities and improve readiness and a great urgency for action. According to the country index of the Notre Dame Global Adaptation Index (ND-GAIN), over 100 countries, Kenya is ranked 52nd most vulnerable and 146th in the readiness index.

40. The funding proposal focuses on 4 out of the LREB, where agricultural activities play a major role in the economy. The proposed project will be implemented through the LREB to is coordinate and facilitate service delivery; promote trade linkages, investment, and economic diversification; and improve access to markets for smallholder farmers. It also aims at social planning and coordination of activities. Importantly, the 4 counties of the LREB share a common vision of sustainable development and are mobilized to take joint action to tackle environmental challenges and climate change.

41. The funding proposal provides clear and well-documented information about the current situation in each county and the specific needs of the population living in populated regions of Kenya, and includes key development indicators for each county covered by the project.

42. In terms of institutional development, the funding proposal builds on the efforts by Kenya regarding the devolution process and the attempts at decentralizing the climate governance structure. However, the proposal suggests that while climate capacity is stronger at national level, it is important to strengthen it at local level. An example of this need is the

# Country ownership

## Scale: High

The funding proposal includes a no objection letter dated 7 July 2023 signed by Dr. Chris Kiptoo, CBS Principal Secretary, National Treasury of the Republic of Kenya (annex 1).

In its annex 2 part C(3) the funding proposal clarifies the alignment of the project with policies and strategies of the government of Kenya. The annex provides a detailed analysis of the alignment of the proposal with the national constitution (2010), the national gender and equality commission act, as well as forest, climate change policy and strategy. It also includes an overview of the project in relation to a detailed analysis of the alignment of the proposal with the climate change policy (see section 1.1). as well information about its alignment with the national strategy on smart agriculture and climate change risk management. The annex further provides evidence that this project will serve the objectives, help to achieve climate change policies, and improve institutional capacity.

The funding proposal mentions three EEs: FAO, Agriterra and the Government of Kenya. The ITAP acknowledges the capacity of FAO, accredited in October 2016, as EE for this proposal. Agriterra has a long standing experience with climate change adaptation projects in many of them implemented in countries in Africa35. Agriterra seeks to develop and strengthen cooperation with the Government of Kenya.

Agriterra is an international organization with headquarters in the Netherlands, which provides expert advice and training to cooperatives and partner organizations in emerging economies on sustainable land management practices and climate-smart agriculture (CSA). Furthermore, its previous experiences and partnerships, including cooperative development projects in Vietnam, Cambodia, Laos, and Mozambique, University International an “Climate Clever” coffee production in Viet Nam, or the Climate Resilient Agriculture project in Kenya, demonstrate an appropriate level of expertise and competencies as an EE for this funding proposal.

The Government of Kenya, acting through the National Treasury, will prescribe the activities to be undertaken by the implementing entity. The government plays a very important role in defining the national policy on climate change and has proven experience on complementing projects to this project such as “the Financing locally led climate action programme” (FLLoca).

FAO has a long standing experience is dealing with climate change mitigation and adaptation, both at the international level and in Kenya.

### Efficiency and effectiveness

**Scale: Medium to high**

The overall budget for the six-year implementation period is USD 49,992,238, with a funding gap of approximately USD 10 million. The Government of France will provide EUR 57,699,399 as a grant. The Government of Germany will contribute EUR 14 million, and the Government of Denmark will provide USD 579,910. FAO will provide USD 982,000 for implementing this funding proposal, all in the form of grants. This means a co-funding ratio of 1:0.71.

The funding proposal includes an economic and financial analysis (EFA) prepared following the guidelines provided by the GCF. The project-level economic internal rate of return is at 21 per cent with a benefit-cost ratio of 1.24; for RCP B5, it is 18 per cent with a benefit-cost ratio of 1.31 respectively and for RCP C6, it is 20 per cent with a benefit-cost ratio of 1.33 respectively for the 20-year period at 10 per cent economic discount rate. The project has the potential to reduce GHG emissions by a significant level with social net present value (SNPV) of USD 84 million and USD 156 million for RCP B5 and RCP C6 scenarios, respectively.

## The ITAP considers the high concessionality of the GCF investment to be justified based on the arguments presented in section 1.5, "Needs of the Recipient." Additionally, the ITAP emphasizes the significant co-funding contribution by the Government of Kenya and underscores the importance of leveraging existing public sector resources for blended financing for CRLCSA beyond project implementation. In this regard, the proposed capacity-building effort to support the Kenyan government in developing a national carbon market, coupled with collaboration with local financial institutions, are assessed as a promising and strategic approach.

However, much of the long-term success of the project depends on the improved income due to better or premium prices of CRLCSA commodities. This is challenging for two reasons: First, improving the quality of CRLCSA commodities requires that farmers be more prepared and able to respond promptly to short-term price fluctuations. While this can be achieved through tools such as a digital electronic market platform which displays "off" and "on" prices, the current draft project proposal does not include such a tool.

Secondly, certification or labeling services for sustainably produced commodities requires the implementation of a labeling or certification system that enables consumers to identify these products in the market. The ITAP recognizes that in some cases, such as certified coffee, there are premium markets where certified and non-certified producers can co-exist. However, commodities are available for Kenya. However, the evidence on the actual economic benefits that certification brings is limited. In other words, the evidence shows that certification does not necessarily lead to a higher price for CRLCSA commodities in the market.

The challenges, or lack of concrete evidence for securing a premium price, are highlighted by the fact that there are no examples of such markets (see Annexure 23).

The ITAP acknowledges both the risks and the potential value of promoting CRLCSA commodities and recognizes their likelihood of achieving better productivity under future climate change scenarios. However, the ITAP evaluates the efficiency and effectiveness of this proposal as modest in terms of its ability to attract a dedicated market to secure better incomes for farmers producing CRLCSA commodities.

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

This cross-cutting funding proposal aims to achieve a mitigation outcome of over 4 million metric tons carbon dioxide equivalent (tCO2e) over the 20-year lifespan of the project. The expected adaptation outcome includes 572,000 direct and over 2 million indirect beneficiaries in terms of improved livelihoods for farmers and other stakeholders. This is based on strong scientific foundation with local knowledge and institutional capacity within the country and the project region. It also recognizes that the project will engage a wide range of key stakeholders who are essential for climate-resilient, low-carbon development in the project region and beyond.

However, the ITAP identified two concerns that could affect how the project achieves the criteria on climate impact potential, and efficiency and effectiveness. First, the project's activities do not include provision for seed production or the establishment of nurseries, which


---
**Page 120**
---

are crucial for ensuring the timely availability of planting materials required to implement the climate-resilient, low-carbon sustainable agricultural (CRSLCA) practices outlined in the Green Lists. This can lead to significant delays in scaling up these practices. Second, there is a potential risk that farmers may not be able to afford the increased production costs associated with CRSLCA practices or if these premiums do not directly benefit the farmers and outweigh the increased production costs.

To address these concerns, the iTAP recommends the following to the AE:

(a) During the inception phase, the AE should assess whether the existing seed producers and nurseries in the region can provide sufficient planting material for all CRSLCA activities. If this is not possible, the AE should identify alternative sources of supply and monitor progress on the provision of seeds and other planting materials.

(b) Throughout the implementation phase, the AE should track price developments of the CRSLCA commodities and monitor whether the economic benefits to farmers outweigh the production costs.

(c) The iTAP recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal.

# EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

## Gender Challenges in Kenya

Kenya has made significant strides in enhancing gender equality and social inclusion (GESI) over the last decade. The Government of Kenya (GoK)'s introduction of Kenya's Constitution of 2010 and its development of a National Gender Policy have been key milestones. These policies, along with other reforms that have formally enabled greater gender equality across political, economic, and social spheres. The process of gender mainstreaming in the devolution of government authority, resources, and functions to Kenya's counties has also provided new opportunities for women and men, boys and girls, to participate as leaders and decision-makers at county level.

Despite these political commitments, on the ground realities at county level and across different sectors indicate that gender gaps and inequalities persist in Kenya. These gender gaps are evidenced in various equality-based indexes. The Gender Development Index (GDI) and Kenya's Gender Inequality Index (GGI) highlight persistent gender disparities in education, health, economic opportunities, and political empowerment.

Women in Kenya are incredibly diverse, with differences in age, marital status, socio-economic status, disability, ethnicity, religion, and geographic location. Women face greater vulnerability relative to men. In the 14 counties targeted by the CRLSRA project in the Lake Victoria Basin (LVB) and Lake Region Economic Block (LERB), there are four ethnic communities that form the majority of inhabitants: in Bomet County, the Kalenjin; in Busia County, the Luo; in Siaya County, the Luhya; in Homa Bay, Nzoia, and Vihiga (Luhya); and in Homa Bay, Kisumu, Migori, and Siaya (Luo). Specific local and historical dynamics and socio-cultural and gender norms mediate diverse women's access to and control over resources. The Gender Assessment provides a snapshot of the current situation for women and girls.

This Gender Assessment includes discussion of how gender interacts with age status (i.e., young women and men, older women and men), as well as how gender intersects with other social identities that influence participation, benefits, and use of adaptation and mitigation strategies in agricultural value chains. In this way, the assessment views both GESI as core strategies towards the achievement of diverse women's rights and agency.

Women's representation and meaningful participation in the decision-making process trails men at national and county level, despite Kenya’s Constitution of 2010 requiring a minimum of one-third representation of either gender in all elected and public posts. While the political participation rate for voting women is higher than that of men, their representation in leadership positions within national parliaments and local governments are lower. As of 2018, less than one-third of the National Parliament were women (31.8% of National Assembly [MPs] and 31.3% of Senators). For local government at the county level, only 45 out of 97 counties have a woman as their chairperson. Women hold 20% of seats in governments and 14.9% of Deputy Governors.[1]. Thus, while gender parity quotas are enshrined in Kenyan legislation, women continue to be underrepresented at all levels of government, limiting women’s ability to meaningfully and equally participate in policy-making and top decision-making and leadership roles.[2] Women who enter politics and leadership positions at national level, as well as within local governments, often face backlash from men. They are also more likely than men to experience gender-based violence (GBV) and intimidation, highlighting the continuing challenge of challenging discriminatory attitudes and practices towards women’s role outside the home in Kenya.[3]

Knowledge and resource gaps in women's healthcare and sexual reproductive health and rights (SRHR) persist in Kenya. While women have a greater life expectancy than men (65 to 60 years), women and girls have greater malnutrition-wasting rates, obesity rates, and HIV infection rates than men.[4] Anemia is also a significant public health problem among pregnant women and young children under five years of age. In the 2019 national census[5], while the maternal mortality and infant mortality rates have decreased in recent years, they remain high compared to other countries in East Africa. The Kenyan population has universal health coverage.[6] There are also clear gaps between women's needs and the reality of SRHR healthcare provision. Almost 1/3 of women of reproductive age do not have their need for family planning services met by a skilled provider, and only about half of women aged 20–49 years had an ideal level of understanding of reproductive rights.[7] A 2021 study by the African Women's Development and Communication Network found that married women in Kenya expressed that they experienced a lack of autonomy over their bodies. The study also revealed that many women and girls do not have access to women and girls lack access to information about their basic reproductive health, with only 12% of girls aged 12–19 and 38% of women ages 21-30 are knowledgeable about menstruation.[8]

Women in Kenya have higher rates of poverty than men, and rural women have the highest levels of poverty insecurity. The national population living below the national poverty line (young girls aged 0-17 years old; elderly women 70 years and older) are the most vulnerable and have the highest poverty levels (43% young girls and 39.1% elderly women are below the poverty line). Kenya has a 5% gender gap in primary school enrollment, with boys more likely to be enrolled than girls. The gap narrows between boys and girls at 2%, but a 3.1% gender gap remains in mathematics comprehension at the Grade 2/3 level (47.8% of boys vs. 44.7% of girls). In secondary school, there is a 50% gender gap with only 69.7% (120,795) women compared to 56.8% (276,906) men enrolled. According to the 2022 GGII Index, women had near-equal access to education and health services, but barriers for widows and daughters-in-law remain. Women have rights for access to land assets and other non-land-based economic participation, women have higher rates of unemployment (9.6%) compared to men (5.37%), and women earn 32% less than men in the formal sector. The informal sector is dominated by women with an average of $2.3 million, with women accounting for more than 50% of the total working population. According to the World Bank (2020) Report, Kenya's informal sector accounts for at least 87% of employment opportunities.

The informal sector in Kenya has been growing rapidly over the past decade, reaching an estimated 13 million Kenyans as of 2019 estimates, compared to the 2.9 million who work in the formal sector.[2] These 15 million Kenyans are domestic workers, cleaners, beauticians, mechanics, and street vendors, among many more. Besides, the informal sector in Kenya is highly unregulated, with women theres having limited or no social or labor protections.

Women in Agriculture

Recent estimates suggest that women account for approximately 75% of the agricultural labour force in Kenya and own 80% of its small-scale farms.[3] While agriculture is central to the Kenyan economy accounting for 34.2% of GDP and employing over 60% of the population, 70% who are living in rural areas,[4], women's participation as a producer is limited by gender norms and social barriers that have lagged behind men. This is largely due to discriminatory social and gender norms that hinder women's involvement in agricultural value chains by shaping gender roles and responsibilities, and directing which types of crops, livestock species, marketing opportunities, and networks are appropriate for women to access.[5] For example, traditional cash crops including coffee and tea, and the emergent formal dairy


---
**Page 130**
---

sector, are associated with men or are masculinized commodities, whereas informal food security crops and small livestock (e.g., poultry, rabbits) are feminized commodities.20

Barriers to women's participation and benefit in agricultural value chains include limited access to affordable inputs such as seeds, fertilizers, and pesticides; technology, limited access to extension services, limited access to markets, and lack of access to inputs. Structural inequalities continue to prevent women from accessing higher levels of value chains and trading opportunities both at national, intra- regional, and international levels.21 Women's participation in agricultural value chains is further complicated by rural women make it more difficult for them to engage in value chains that require large tracts of land to produce at scale (e.g., coffee, tea).22

International gender differences also influence aspirations and opportunities for engaging in agriculture and contributing to food security. For example, young women are disinclined to pursue agri- business activities when they are the least likely to have access to productive resources and assets, access to credit and financial services, or business support services compared to men, boys, or women.23 Young women are also less likely than men to be involved in decision-making within their households or collective organizations due to power dynamics that prioritize the opinions of elder men – this can also act as a disincentive.24 Women marginalized from participating in agricultural production more broadly, and the belief that they have unearned roles as consumers, marketers, or leaders.25

### Women and Climate Change in Agriculture

Women are disproportionately vulnerable to climate impacts based on existing inequalities in their roles, rights, and opportunities, which are shaped by gender norms and socio-economic status. For example, women tend to be more reliant on the natural resource base for securing their daily livelihoods (e.g., the collection of fuel wood for household energy).26 Women also have a greater vulnerability to the reduction in agricultural resources due to climate change affects them more directly. Given that climate change amplifies existing socio-economic inequalities (e.g., control over or access rights to resources), women smallholder farmers in Kenya's lack of secure land rights, livestock assets, and technologies
