constrain them from adapting and coping with climate shocks. Increased vulnerabilities include increased risk for GBV due to conditions such as longer distances to collect water and loss of livelihoods creating tensions within households.

While commercialization has been identified as a key strategy in assisting farmers adapt to climate change in Kenya, current business-as-usual practices in the value chain are gendered. The way commercialization is performed tends to weaken women's control over decision-making and previously controlled incomes. In Kenya, and specifically in Eastern Africa, women are often excluded from participating in the livestock sector due to cultural norms that assign gendered responsibility to certain agricultural activities and values chains as being "for men" (e.g., cash crops, coffee, tea, dairy) and others as "for women" (poultry, vegetables and fruits for household consumption). Women who participate in these activities are often those that are economically disadvantaged. While these activities become more profitable, men usually control the economic benefits gained from these activities.

This value chain up-grading, and agricultural intensification is the context of a changing climate must be gender-responsive and acknowledge the gendered trade-offs that guide women's decisions in whether to engage in increasing marketization and/or formal marketing of livestock products. Gender-sensitive approaches are needed to ensure that women have access to resources (such as land, credit, local women's groups and cooperatives societies) to identify appropriate strategies to ensure women are equitably benefiting from participation in the value chain. For example, adding agricultural diversification or market linkages to existing activities can be a more sustainable strategy for women than shifting women directly into e.g., poultry production) in addition to planned commercialization activities.

The Gender Action Plan (GAP) for the proposed project tackles gender inequalities across several priority areas in agri-climate adaptation and mitigation. These include closing gender gaps and existing inequalities in participation (at household level and within resource organization, markets), workload reduction (through agricultural technologies and practices that reduce adaptation that mitigate role workloads and negative impacts on women), access and use of productive resources (such as agri-climate information, technologies, livelihood income, credit), and collective action (working with women's groups as platforms for enhancing access, agency, and voice in climate-smart agriculture). In closing these gaps:

gender gaps, the project will use gender transformative approaches (GTAs) and more specifically, Gender Action Learning Systems (GALS) to address the underlying discriminatory socio-cultural and gender norms that perpetuate gender inequality and constrain women's capabilities. This requires a culturally sensitive, multi-level approach that includes women and men in all their diversity.

The proposed project will use eight core strategies to achieve its gender and social inclusion (GESI) objectives. These are: (1) Supporting and strengthening Kenya’s existing gender-responsive legal and institutional frameworks; (2) Supporting improved access to productive agricultural assets for vulnerable smallholder and community level (including within producer organizations, county agencies, and private sector); (3) Supporting greater participation of women in agriculture through inclusive agricultural value chains, including sustainable livelihoods, both women and men. In addition, climate-resilient and low-carbon production and processing practices, technologies, assets, and risk reduction mechanisms; (4) Strengthening women, PLWD, and other vulnerable people’s access to land and natural resources, particularly in cooperative societies and agricultural value chains; (5) Supporting agricultural extension to disseminate and demonstrate CRUSA knowledge, technologies and practices in ways that are gender-responsive and socially inclusive; (6) Promoting the use of gender-sensitive and climate-resilient agricultural inputs and climate indicators be collected, analyzed, and fed back into project activities iteratively and in a participatory manner. This will ensure that all project activities are gender responsive and that their organizations by increasing access to gender-responsive and socially inclusive financial products that support climate-resilient and low-carbon growth/Supporting gender-responsive and socially inclusive financial institutions and mechanisms that provide credit, insurance, and other financial services towards climate adaptation and mitigation; (7) Identifying and promoting gender-responsive and socially inclusive policies and programs that are designed to reduce the vulnerability of women, PLWD, and other vulnerable people to climate change. These will be developed, monitored and evaluated in a participatory manner and monitor gender and social safeguards to reduce climate risks for the most vulnerable, as well as risks to women’s health, wellbeing, and livelihoods from increased engagement in cooperative organizations and leadership roles. This will lead to shift of CDR and men’s conception of women’s role (and women’s value chain labour). The Gender Action Plan (GAP) should be further developed with the participation of women, girls, boys, youth, PLWD, and other vulnerable people who are most affected by these gender critical issues within the agricultural sector to ensure all planned activities are gendered and meet the rights, needs, and experiences of women, men, girls, boys, youth, PLWD, and other vulnerable people.12

PART I: GENDER ASSESSMENT

### 1. Introduction

This Gender Assessment and accompanying Gender Action Plan (GAP) are presented in relation to the proposed project “Transforming Livelihoods through Climate Resilient, Low Carbon, Sustainable Agriculture” (hereafter referred to as the Project). The assessment is intended to inform the mainstreaming of gender equality and demonstrates its importance as a cross-cutting theme and prioritized climate and development goal. As such, the proposal has integrated gender mainstreaming into all project outputs and activities, as evidenced in the Gender Action Plan.

12 For a full list of publications by the FAO Joint Programme on Gender Transformative Approaches for Food Security and Nutrition see: https://www.fao.org/3/ca8501en/gender-transformative-approaches/nutrition/en/

13 In alignment with key recommendations from the 2020 report “Review of the National and County Planning and Budgeting Processes with a Gender Responsiveness and Social Inclusion Lens in Agriculture Sector, Kenya”

**Page 133**

To illustrate that the project has meaningfully considered gender and will convene actions towards the achievement of greater equity through "gender-responsive" programming, this assessment provides a situational analysis of the relevant genetic dynamics in the program/project sites in the Lake Victoria Basin (LVB), also referred to as the Lake Region Economic Bloc or LREB. Targeting is at county level[6], and includes 12 counties: Busia, Homa Bay, Kakamega, Kericho, Kisii, Kirinyaga, Siaya, Nandi, Nakuru, Embu, Migori, Nandi, Nyamira, Siaya, Trans-Nzoia, and Vihiga, and across six value chains: coffee, tea, fruit trees, African leafy vegetables, livestock, and poultry. Climate change impacts these value chains in significant ways—increased temperatures, reduced rainfall, increased pests and diseases, and more frequent droughts affect coffee, tea, and African leafy vegetables, as well as dairy cows and poultry due to inadequate feed and fodder, water scarcity, heat stress, pest and disease outbreaks. There is generally a lack of sex- and gender-disaggregated data (qualitative or quantitative) that is collected and presented separately on men and women in the LVB counties. The assessment uses sex-disaggregated data where available. Sex-and age-disaggregated data is presented from the 2019 Kenya Census's national and county-level population estimates, as well as two quantitative surveys conducted in the Feasibility Study (i.e., climate risk survey and coping strategies). The results of the qualitative research conducted in the Feasibility study are presented in Section 5.2.7.

Gender refers to the socially constructed system of classification that attributes qualities of masculinity and femininity to people, which is not necessarily related to biological sex or gender identity. Gender is a social construct that varies across cultures and societies. We also recognize that gender interacts with other social categories (e.g., age, caste, class, ethnic community, disability, etc.) in ways that shape how people's engagement with agriculture and climate change adaptation takes place—relative privilege and socio-cultural norms.

Gender equality refers to the equal rights of women and girls as defined by the United Nations Institute for Gender Equality (UNIGE). 2012. Glossary and literature review. https://www.unige.it/en/research/series/unigeh358

Gender mainstreaming is a strategy that seeks to address gender inequalities in all policies, programs, and projects. It involves integrating women's perspectives into the planning process of development activities and ensuring that they are reflected in policy decisions and actions. Gender mainstreaming aims to ensure that women's needs and interests are taken into account at every stage of the decision-making process.

Gender equality is a fundamental human right and an essential element for sustainable development, poverty reduction, and social inclusion. (Source: Nelson, G. 2015). Gender Responsive National Communications Toolkit. United Nations Development Programme

Following the Kenyan Constitution 2010, the country is divided into 47 counties with devolved governments.

A total of three commercial value chains were selected for this assessment based on their importance to the LVB (e.g., coffee, tea, African leafy vegetables, and poultry). The selection was informed by the following criteria: (i) market demand; (ii) production volume; (iii) profitability; (iv) potential for growth; (v) value chain dynamics; (vi) gender equality in the value chains; (vii) climate change vulnerability; and (viii) availability of data. The selection process was informed by a desk review, key informant interviews, and focus group discussions.

In Kenya, men’s and women’s gender roles, relations, and responsibilities are intricately connected to their fragility and resilience to climate change. On the one hand, traditional gender norms exacerbate the negative impacts of climate change on women—their lack of access to resources, information, and technologies makes them more vulnerable to both rapid and slow-onset climate events[1]. On the other hand, women’s greater dependence on natural resources for their livelihoods increases their capacity to climate impact[2]. Thus, the gender-differentiated impacts and opportunities posed by climate change underscore women's vulnerability and disadvantage them in the face of a changing climate, as well as their role in adapting to and mitigating climate change. In line with these observations, approaches to climate change adaptation have recently identified the co-benefit of women’s empowerment[3].

In general, women face disproportionate climate change impacts given existing social inequalities. Even though women are key actors in agriculture production, they are often underpaid, excluded, and marginalized women's contribution to agricultural access across sectors and value chain nodes. While women and children spend significant amounts of time laboring in production, harvesting, and processing crops, they receive little or no compensation for their work[4]. Furthermore, most women and youth do not own the land or other farm assets and do not have decision-making power over cash resources[5]. In addition to these challenges, women are often excluded from accessing credit and loans that strengthen or expand their farming activities. Having fewer physical capital, financial resources to draw upon, and less access to information and technology exacerbates climate change impacts on agricultural production.

While gender equality is part of Kenya’s 2015 – 2030 climate change adaptation plan, in the LIV/REB women are facing social and gender inequalities that climate change is exacerbating. These gender-based challenges are further compounded by their lack of access to resources, information, and technologies. Women's work is often invisible in agriculture—they frequently receive no payment for agricultural labor and are often not considered farmers by agricultural extension staff[6]. Since women produce a large share of agricultural output and contribute significantly to household food security, gender equality is critical. Many successful agricultural intervention requires raising women’s empowerment and ensuring that gender-specific strategies are at the core of development programs.

To identify and create concrete steps towards addressing these challenges, gender analysis was used to explore the underlying causes, constraints, and opportunities that influence women's engagement in agricultural value chains in the context of a changing climate. Gender considerations are crucial to ensure the gender equality and empowerment of women[7]. This gender gap refers to "the underperformance of the agriculture sectors in many developing countries due to limited or unequal access to

In creating action-based recommendations towards strengthening gender equality in the intervention sites, the CRIECSA project is integrated with the local agri-climate information ecosystem and seeks to leverage the potential for agricultural cooperatives and producer organizations as sites of gender- responsiveness.19 Women’s groups have been formed; indeed, agricultural cooperatives have become centers of resilience for women, who have leveraged their power in cooperatives to emerge as key agents for rural change in Kenya.20 The role of women in climate change adaptation is well-documented and, empowerment from engaging in agricultural value chains is well-documented, including in Kenya. For example, by providing informational and technological resources, access to individual and communal credit schemes, and training on business skills, cooperatives can provide a platform for women to build women’s confidence and empower them with the tools needed to increase their adaptive capacity and resilience to climate change.21

### 2. Methodology

This gender assessment presents a situational gender analysis of Kenya and the 14 targeted countries in the CRIECSA project. Gender analysis is defined as: “a systematic, analytical process for organizing, collecting, analyzing, and interpreting data on the roles, responsibilities, and relationships between men and women, their access to resources, their activities, and the constraints they face relative to each other.” In identifying the different roles, needs, interests and opportunities for women and men, boys, and girls, a gender analysis can help to identify how these differences impact the achievement of gender equality (and social inclusion) in a particular intervention. In the context of agriculture and climate change interventions, this process can be used to identify gender inequalities within communities/households and other social inequalities, and to build on the diverse knowledge and capacities within communities/households that can be used to make them more resilient to climate-related shocks and risks. The alignment with each gender assessment tool that they use to understand gender power and resilience.

relationships that mediate access, control, and decision-making, as well as assets, resources, and workloads, is to recognize and address prevailing cultural norms, values, and beliefs that are expressed in the construction of gender identities and inequities." For additional guidance in performing gender-sensitive value chain studies, FAD has several key resources.10

The Gender Assessment focuses on the current situation of rural women in the counties targeted by the proposed project: Bonet, Bungema, Busia, Homa Bay, Kakamega, Kericho, Kisii, Kisumu, Migori, Nandi, Nyamira, Siaya, and Vihiga. The assessment is based on a review of existing literature, including recent literature on gender, climate change, cooperation, and agricultural value chains in Kenya. Academic and gray literature are reviewed to identify key issues and opportunities, as well as gaps. Secondary data and statistical tools are used to provide an empirical evidence base for the inclusion of women in the proposal. Primary data was also collected in the counties targeted via three fieldwork activities. The first quantitative survey is a household survey that explores women's participation, performance, and influential decision-making in crop and livestock activities. The second assessment is qualitative, and explores the gender barriers, opportunities, and dynamics regarding women's participation, performance, and empowerment within agricultural cooperatives and value chains. The third assessment examines women's perceived membership in cooperatives and value chains targeted in each county. The tools used for qualitative assessments are presented in the Appendix, while the quantitative data collection tools are annexed in the main report.

Qualitative interviews with cooperative members were designed to better understand the gender-based barriers and opportunities for women's engagement in the agricultural value chain, that influence their participation, performance, and empowerment within cooperatives. The focus group discussions were designed to identify the key gender issues and inequalities within specific agricultural cooperatives, and discuss solutions towards improving women's participation, performance, benefits, and empowerment. These dimensions were chosen based on the gender analytic approach of Rubin et al. (2019) in investigating relevant dimensions of women's engagement in agricultural value chains (see Figure 1).

## 2.1 Organisation of the Report

The report is intended as a "living document" whereby the assessment data herein and Gender Action Plan (GAP) should be updated periodically based on new information obtained during the inception and implementation phases of the project. The mid-term progress review, Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) system and the mid-term evaluation will consider progress and gaps in the implementation of the GAP and provide recommendations to adjust and improve it. The gender expert assigned to the project will be responsible for review of this document on a bi-annual basis.

The remainder of the Gender Assessment is organized as follows: Section 3 provides an overview of the legal and policy framework for the promotion of gender equality in Kenya. Section 4 presents a general


---
**Page 138**
---

overview of the status of women and gender equality in Kenya. Section 5 consists of a gender analysis in the context of the project implementation sites. Section 6 introduces project strategies to integrate gender equality and social inclusion, which are further elaborated in the GAF.

# Legal and policy framework for promotion of gender equality in Kenya

This section maps and assesses the existing institutional, policy, and legal frameworks pertaining to gender equality and women's rights in Kenya. It reviews the constitutional provisions on gender equality, national legal frameworks for the promotion of gender equality, and national climate-related policies. A brief analysis of the level of gender integration of the national climate-related policies is presented, before an overview of sectoral policies relevant to this proposal – namely, agriculture, livestock, and natural resources.

## 3.1 International Frameworks and Conventions on Gender Equality

There are multiple international instruments, policies, and declarations that compel states to develop and implement laws and policies aimed at the full realization of women's equality and empowerment rights. Article 20(1) of the Constitution of Kenya [2005] compels the Government to implement the obligations of the international treaties it has ratified. These include: the 1994 International Conference on Population and Development (ICPD); the 1995 Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action; the World Summit for Social Development (1995) and its review held in 2000; the BPA (1995); the United Nations Commission on the Status of Women; the Joint United Nations Programme on HIV/AIDS (UNAIDS) Action Framework on Women, Children, and AIDS; the UN Millennium Declaration; and the 2003 CEDAW Committee's Concluding Observations on Kenya.

The latest CEDAW Committee concluding observations on the eight periodic reports of Kenya to the Committee highlight that the Government has not yet ratified the Optional Protocol to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW). The Committee calls upon the Government to: (a) To promote the participation of rural women in decision-making processes and their access to high-quality health care, education and adequate water and sanitation; (b) To facilitate the access of rural women to land and other productive resources, including credit, and ensure equal access to land and establish a clear legislative framework to protect their rights to inheritance and land ownership; (c) To provide rural women with access to affordable and quality health care services as set forth in the agricultural sector development strategy covering the period 2010-2020; (d) To ensure access to high-quality health care for rural women, including through increased training of midwives; (e) To ensure that rural women have access to information on climate change and its impact on their livelihoods and climate change; and (f) To implement the Climate Change Act of 2016 in a manner that prioritizes women's rights. While progress has been made in implementing international treaties and conventions, the implementation of some provisions is still lacking or some are not yet fully implemented.

At the national level, Kenya has ratified the following declarations: the AU Charter and its Protocol on Human and People’s Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa (2003); and as a member of the Intergovernmental Authority on Development (IGAD). It recognizes that there are sharp gender inequalities in access to land, credit, extension services, inputs, markets, technology, education, health, and couples; coupled with education and health care. Despite these commitments, challenges for gender statistics in Kenya include: lack of harmonization among data sources; inadequate coordination; There is a need to harmonize methods and standards across producers and users of gender statistics in Kenya to address the gaps in sex-disaggregated and gender-specific statistics.

A recent review of gender in climate change and agriculture policies in the East African region found that while there is increasing gender responsiveness in the region, (i) gender issues are still interpreted as "women issues," (ii) there is disharmony in gender mainstreaming across governance levels, (iii) budgeting for gender equality remains low, (iv) implementation of gender equality policies is weak, (v) allocations to gender at sub-national level remain inconsistently low with sharp differences between estimated and actual budgets, and (vi) gender activities do not address any structural inequalities.16

### 3 National Legal Frameworks for the Promotion of Gender Equality

There are several legal frameworks to promote, enforce and monitor equality and non-discrimination based on gender in Kenya. These include: The Constitution of Kenya, 2010; Article 28(1), National Gender and Equality Commission Act, No. 15 of 2011, Section B; The Prohibition of FGM Act, 2013; Prevention against Domestic Violence Act, 2015.

The 2010 Constitution is the cornerstone of Kenya's legal framework on gender equality and women’s empowerment. Its provisions include:

- **Article 27**: Guarantees equality and freedom from discrimination. It prohibits discrimination based on gender, ensuring that men and women have equal opportunities in political, economic, cultural and social spheres.
- **Two-thirds Gender Rule (Article 81B)**: The Constitution mandates that not more than two-thirds of members of any public or appointive bodies should be of the same gender, promoting gender parity in political representation.
- **Affirmative Action (Article 27(1b))**: This allows for measures to be taken to promote women's representation and participation in all spheres of life including politics.
- **Bill of Rights (Chapter 4)**: Guarantees equal protection under the law for all citizens, including women, and prohibits any form of discrimination.

Several statutory laws have been enacted to implement these constitutional principles and promote gender equality:

- The Sexual Offences Act (2006): Provides comprehensive legal protection against sexual violence, aiming to safeguard women's bodily integrity and prosecute perpetrators of sexual offenses.
- The Matrimonial Property Act (1984): This law provides for the division of property acquired before or after divorce, recognizing both monetary and non-monetary contributions made during the marriage. The law also prohibits the practice of female genital mutilation and provides for the protection of women and girls from this harmful practice.
- The Employment Act (2007): Prohibits discrimination in employment on grounds of gender and marital status; equal pay for work of equal value, ensuring that women are treated fairly in the workplace.
- The Protection Against Domestic Violence Act (2013): Provides legal protection for victims of domestic violence, who are predominantly women, and facilitates prosecution of offenders.


---
**Page 140**
---

The National Gender and Equality Commission (NGEC) was established by the National Gender and Equality Commission Act, 2013 pursuant to Article 39(4) of the Constitution of Kenya. It is one of the three (3) successor commissions, NGEC, Commission on Administrative Justice and Kenya National Commission on Human Rights), to the Kenya National Human Rights and Equality Commission (KNHREC) established in Article 52 of the Constitution of Kenya. The NGEC was established by the National Gender and Equality Commission Act 2011. Its mandate is to promote and ensure gender equality, principles of equality and non-discrimination for all persons in Kenya with a focus on women, persons with disabilities, children, youth, people living with HIV/AIDS and other marginalized groups.

It monitors, facilitates and advises on the integration of the principles of equality and freedom from discrimination in all national and county policies, laws, and administrative regulations in all public and private institutions; and it also investigates and ensures compliance with legal frameworks and strategic plans.


### 3.3 National Climate-related Policies and Gender

Several government policies seek to improve gender equality and women's empowerment:
- The National Gender and Equality Commission (NGEC) established under the National Gender and Equality Commission Act, 2013 is a constitutional commission mandated to promote gender equality and freedom from discrimination. It monitors the implementation of laws and policies that affect women in Kenya.
- The Kenya Vision 2030: Kenya's long-term development blueprint emphasizes gender equity in economic, social, and political spheres. Its aim is to empower women through increased participation in decision-making processes at all levels of government and access to education, healthcare, and economic opportunities.
- The National Policy on Gender and Development (2018): This policy provides a framework for mainstreaming gender equality into all aspects of government policy and program implementation. It focuses on addressing gender inequalities in areas such as education, health, and political participation.
- Kenya Women's Economic Empowerment Strategy 2019–2032: This strategy focuses on creating opportunities for women’s economic participation by improving access to financial resources, markets, technology, and employment opportunities.


There are two recent national climatized policies that are particularly relevant to the proposed project. Each of these is discussed in turn, followed by a table analyzing the level of gender integration of each of these policies.

Central Bank of Kenya: Guidance on Climate-related Risk Management.* Revised 2018 In October 2013, the Central Bank of Kenya issued this guidance under section 3(4) of the Banking Act, which empowers the Central Bank of Kenya to guide institutions to maintain a stable and efficient banking and financial system. The guidance recognizes that climate change poses a substantial risk and can pose an opportunity for the banking sector. It provides guidance on how banks should integrate climate risks into their governance arrangements; incorporate the financial risks from climate change into their existing financial risk management practice; and develop an approach to disclose on the financial risks from climate change.

**Page 141**

National Climate Change Action Plan 2018-2022 (NCCAP) [1] (2018). This plan aims to strengthen the country's path towards sustainable, climate-resilient development while achieving low carbon climate resilient development. It builds on the previous Action Plan spanning the period 2013–2017. The NCCAP consists of three volumes: Volume I – National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP); Volume II – and a Mitigation Technical Analysis Report (volume III). The Plan seeks in particular to: 1) reduce risks to communities and infrastructure resulting from climate-related disasters such as droughts and floods, 2) increase the resilience of Kenya’s economy by reducing vulnerability to climate change impacts, 3) build sectoral resilience in low- carbon manner as possible; 4) Enhance resilience of the Blue Economy and water sector by ensuring access to clean drinking water and sanitation services for all Kenyans; 5) Increase forest cover and other urban green cover to 10% of total land area; rehabilitate degraded lands, including rangelands; increase resilience of the wildlife and tourism sector, 5) Mainstream climate change adaptation in national development planning processes; 6) Promote sustainable waste management and improved solid waste management in urban areas; 6) Improve energy and resource efficiency in the manufacturing sector, and 7) Climate-proof energy and transport infrastructure; encourage electricity supply based on renewable sources. The NCCAP is aligned with Kenya’s Vision 2030 and other national development plans.

Kenya Climate Smart Agriculture Strategy 2017-2026 [2] (2017). The broad objective of the Kenya Climate Smart Agriculture Strategy 2017–2026 (KCSAS) is to adapt to climate change, build the resilience of agricultural systems, and minimize emissions for enhanced food and nutritional security and improved livelihoods. The KCSAS has four broad strategic areas: (i) Adaptation and building resilience in agriculture; (ii) Mitigation of greenhouse gas emissions from key and minor sources in the agriculture sector; (iii) Establishment of an enabling policy, legal, and institutional framework for climate smart agriculture; and (iv) Building capacity to address the effects of underlying cross-cutting issues such as human resource capacity and finance, which would potentially constrain the realization of climate smart agriculture objectives.

National Policy on Climate Finance. This document proposes several goals. First, it describes the current state and policy framework for climate financing that is relevant for Kenya, focusing on both domestic and international sources. Second, it outlines the role that climate financing could play in each of Kenya’s key sectors: energy; transport; water supply and sanitation; agriculture; health; tourism, manufacturing, water and sanitation, disaster risk management, and research and innovation). Third, it describes the policy interventions the Kenyan government intends to make with respect to climate financing. These include establishing a Climate Change Trust Fund, identifying new financial sources and create a national system for tracking them, enhancing Kenya’s carbon trading system, and exploring the possibility of green bonds.

More recently, the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock, Fisheries and Cooperatives (MoALFC) has developed agricultural sector strategies that address the empowerment of women, youth, and other special-interest groups. For example, The Agricultural Sector Transformation and Growth Strategy (ASTGS, 2019-2029) is addressing the *unique challenges* and opportunities for women and youth in the sector by incorporating gender equality into all aspects of the strategy. The ASTGS further acknowledges that women and youth are underrepresented in agriculture and therefore, do not receive full benefits of the sector.* Policies and recommendations related to livestock are generally focused on increasing productivity and profitability of smallholder farmers. However, MoALFC has also developed a Dairy Sector Transformation Strategy (DSTS) for the dairy sector that is considering the role of gender to deliver benefits to women and other marginalized groups at large scale. The Kenyan Dairy NAMA includes “increasing on-farm dairy productivity through private sector investment in gender-inclusive extension services and fodder supply” as one of four components in its finalization.*


### 4. Overview of the Status of Women in Kenya

This section provides an overview of the status of women in Kenya. Information on specific sub-groups that are particularly vulnerable (e.g., girls, women-headed households, elderly women, and widows) is provided where possible.


#### 4.1 Gender Parity

Gender parity in Kenya can be assessed using data from indices and indicators that demonstrate women’s status in the country. These include the Human Development Index (HDI), Gender Development Index (GDI), Gender Inequality Index (GII), and Gender Inclusivity Index (GII). Each of these indices and their related indicators are described below.


**Human Development Index (HDI): 0.575 HDI, 152 HDI ranking (2021)**

The HDI is a summary measure for assessing long-term progress in the three basic dimensions of human development: a long and healthy life, knowledge, and a decent standard of living. Kenya’s HDI value for 2021 is 0.575— which puts the country in the Medium human development category— positioning it at 152 out of 191 countries and territories. Between 1990 and 2021, Kenya’s HDI value changed by about -3 percentage points. The number of children under five years old dying at birth changed by 2.8 years, mean years of schooling changed by 2.9 years and expected years of schooling changed by 3.1 years. Kenya’s Gini coefficient by poverty changed by about 29.7% between 1990 and 2021.

# Gender Development Index (GDI)

The GDI measures gender gaps in achievements in three basic dimensions of human development: health (measured by life expectancy at birth), education (measured by expected years of schooling for children and mean years of schooling for children and men years of schooling for adults aged 25 years and older) and living standards (measured by female and male estimated GNI per capita). It is a ratio of the female to the male value. The GDI for Kenya in 2019 is 0.537 in contrast with 0.602 for males, resulting in a GDI value of 0.941, placing it into Group 3.

**2021 GDI Value: 0.941**

**GDI change from 2020:** +0.004

### Gender Gap Index

The gender gap index is a measure of the difference between men and women in terms of opportunities, status, attitudes, and other variables. The Gender Gap Index ranges between 0 and 1. Hence, a score of 0 reflects equality between men and women in the variables considered in the index, while a score of 1 reflects significant disparity.

Indicators showing gender inequalities, such as the Gender Inequality Index (GII). The GII measures gender inequalities (the loss in human development due to inequality between female and male achievements) across four dimensions: reproductive health, empowerment, education, and economic status. A low GII value indicates low inequality between women and men, and vice-versa. Reproductive health is measured by maternal mortality ratio and adolescent birth rates; empowerment is measured by the shares of seats in parliament held by women and female labour force participation rate; education is measured by school enrolment at all levels; economic status is measured by the gender pay gap and labour market participation is measured by the labour force participation rates for women and men. Kenya has a GII value of 0.506, ranking it 128 out of 170 countries in 2021.[1]

### 4.3 Poverty and Food Insecurity

Wealth inequalities in Kenya are extreme, with 12.5% of all people living below 50% of median income, with a 9:1 ratio between women and men (15.27% and 13.1%, respectively). As of 2018, the proportion of men, women, and children of all ages living in poverty in all its dimensions according to national definitions was 53% national average, with 54% of women and 52% of men living in poverty.

In absolute terms, women (67.9%) are more likely than men (58.1%) to be poor. The elderly experience the highest levels of poverty at national level (41.5%), while children aged under five years old have the lowest levels of poverty (48.4%) compared to urban rates (20.3%). Girls aged 0–17 and elderly women over the age of 70 experience the highest levels of poverty at national level (41.5% and 39.1%, respectively).

### Population below the national poverty line (%)

| --- | --- |
| 0-17 years | 41.5% |
| 18-64 years | 29.1% |
| 36-59 years | 32.5% |
| 60-69 years | 36.2% |
| 70+ years | 39.1% |

Over half of Kenya’s population are food insecure, with the 2021 State of Food Security and Nutrition in the World ranking Kenya as a low-income food-deficit country[1]. The Food Insecurity Experience Scale (FIES), which rates the presence of moderate to severe food insecurity in the population, rates 56.5% of the population as experiencing moderate food insecurity and 19.1% of the population with severe food insecurity.[2] National census data from 2019 reported that the prevalence of anemia among women of reproductive age (18–49) is 27%. A 2021 report by the Commission for Sexual and Reproductive rights[3] found that 36% of women and girls under age 15 had experienced sexual violence, including rape or attempted rape. The majority of women and girls, irrespective of age, are unaware of the constitutional provisions on sexual reproductive health in Kenya. Only 20% of women aged 15-49 years know that they have a right to decide whether or not to be knowledgeable about contraception. Girls aged 15 and below are least knowledgeable on how to prevent unintended pregnancies. Women aged 21–30 are most knowledgeable on how to prevent unintended pregnancies. Most women expressed that they experienced lack of autonomy, choice, or decision-making in negotiating for safe sex or not to have sex. Respondents reported it is up to women, and not men, to take the necessary precautions to prevent pregnancies and STIs.

### 4.5 Gender-Based Violence

Twenty-one percent (21%) of girls and women aged 15-49 years have undergone female genital mutilation/cutting (FGM/C). The majority were between 10–14 years old, with 38% being under this age; 18% were between 25-29 years of age, 22.9% were between 30-34 years of age, 27.8% were between 35-39 years of age, 32.1% were between 40-44 years of age, and 40.9% were between 45-49 years old. Of the women who had undergone FGM/C, 60% reported that they were married or in a union when they were married before age 15, while 22.9% were married before age 18. As per 2014 reporting year[3], 72.2% of women and girls aged 15-49 years experienced psychological violence by others than an intimate partner in the previous 12 months. 22.7% national average (2014 reporting year)[3]. GBV is a critical issue in Kenya, across its many forms. For example, 81.1% of ever-partners reported that they experienced psychological violence by their current or former intimate partner in the previous 12 months, with 36.9% physical violence, 13.3% sexual violence and 40.7% emotional abuse. 50.7% of women aged 15-49 years reported as per 2014[3]. According to the 2022 GGGI, 86.4% of women will experience GBV in their lifetime.

### 4.7 Employment and Economic Participation of Women

As per 2018 reporting year, child labor exists in Kenya, with a national average of 13.1% of children aged 5-17 engaged in child labor. According to the 2022 GGII index for unemployment among adults (aged 15–64), females made up 5.85% and males made up 3.21%, with a national average of 5.53%.

### 4.8 Women's Representation and Political Participation

Women received the right to vote in 1963, and as of 2023 there has never been a female head of state, and the seats held in the upper house is currently 30%. Women’s representation and meaningful participation are guaranteed by law at all levels of government. At the national level, despite Kenya's Constitution of 2010 requiring a minimum of one-third representation of either gender in all electoral bodies, women hold only 18% of seats in Parliament (National Assembly) and 35% of Senate seats. At the county level, despite Kenya’s Constitution of 2010 requiring a minimum of one-third representation of either gender in all electoral bodies, women hold only 16% of seats in Parliament (National Assembly) and 22% of Senate seats. The proportion of seats held by women in national parliaments and local governments are low. As of 2018, less than one third of the National Parliament were women (13.8% of National Assembly MPs) and 31.5% of Senators. For local government at the county assembly level, women’s representation is 16%.


---

rates were significantly lower, with only 6.4% of governors and 14.9% of Deputy Governors.[32] Only Members of the County Assembly (MCA) representation had slightly over one-third (34.2%) of women MCA representatives.

Thus while gender equity quotas are enshrined in Kenyan legislation, patriarchal customs and socio-cultural norms hamper women's ability to meaningfully and equally participate in political culture and top decision-making roles.[10] Women who do enter politics and leadership positions at national level, as well as within county governments (e.g., MCA), often face challenges such as gender-based violence (GBV) and intimidation. ### 5.1 Gender and social inequalities in crop and livestock systems in the LREB

#### 5.1.1 Crop production

In the LREB is generally separated into crops that are produced and consumed at home versus those sold for profit. Crops grown by women and girls, such as root crops or tree species (e.g., vegetables, African leafy vegetables, fruit trees), crops that are sold for profit are generally clustered in livestock systems. Women and girls who grow these crops often require a significant amount of land to cultivate at scale, and given women’s lack of land rights in Kenya, they generally do not have ownership or decision-making ability for these crops. However, it is quite common for women and girls to be responsible for post-harvest activities such as processing and selling the produce.

The gender division of labor in production that determines who does what, and when. For example, post- harvesting and marketing activities are usually the purview of men, particularly for higher value commodities. Women and girls often have less access to resources such as land, water, and labor power for household consumption, which are often grown on small plots of land that have been allocated to them by their husbands or fathers. The majority of women’s and girls’ agricultural production is through patrilineal descent and is a limited resource; arable land is reserved for son’s wives; young girls almost never have land allocated to them for crop production for either home-based or market-based plant species.

The gender dimensions of crop production in the LREB have been documented in the context of Climate-Smart Agriculture (CSA) practices. A review of key issues found that gender differences in crop adaptation strategies were closely linked to husbands’ and wives’ roles and responsibilities, social norms, risk perception, and access to resources. Women are less likely than men to own land, inherit land, or have access to financial resources, access to digital technologies, CSA practices in crop production. In the context of crop production, women’s and girls’ participation is often limited by their lack of decision-making power at all levels; work burden; (3) access to and use of productive resources such as agricultural inputs, technology, livestock incomes, and credit; and (4) collective action to address and mitigate climate impacts.

#### 5.1.2 Livestock

In Kenya, livestock is raised under different agricultural systems (i.e., pastoral, and mixed-crop farming). In the LREB, mixed-crop farming is the most common investment in livestock value chains, including intensified dairy and poultry production in LREB, is ongoing, with a growing body of research in Kenya showing that empowering women in livestock value chains leads to healthier communities, animals, and


---
**Page 156**
---

In the LREB, cows, their related commodities (i.e., bulked milk and value-added products such as yogurt), and the commercialization process in collecting and bulking milk at cooperative societies are all influenced by social norms and practices. In contrast to other livestock species where women and youth participate in the dairy value chain, it is more common for them to participate in informal roles outside of structures and formal institutions. For example, young men may use motobikes to transport milk to local markets, and women sell bulked milk at their own local intermediaries. While participating in informal markets generally helps women better control the proceeds from livestock production, this can also lead to a loss of income for women when they enter new types of sales, as there has been a "crackdown" by the Kenyan Dairy Board (KDB) in retrospectors to outlaw such practices of selling unpasteurized milk in the name of public health and safety.102

Poultry and small ruminant production tend to be women's preferred value chain, as these species require less capital investment than cattle. Women also have a greater role in entering regarding capital and discriminatory socio-cultural norms that discourage women and youth engagement.103 A recent study found that livestock raising across a range of species (chickens, goats, sheep, rabbits, cows) increased the overall household income by 25% to 48%. Women also reported improved access to food security, social benefits, and time and labor savings. However, these benefits largely promoted long-term household resilience rather than immediate economic gains for women. The main constraints were factors such as the general daily care of livestock and provision of water and fodder, which were overwhelmingly borne by women and children. Climate shocks in the LREB, including drought, would further burden women and youth by increasing their workload and reducing access to resources. Women also reported that feeding fodder crops, or purchase feed inputs at reasonable costs (assuming crop scarcity would drive the price of feeds, which are already expensive for smallholders to afford). Despite this investment, women had limited livestock ownership in most cases, with only 10% owning cattle.104

Livestock ownership requires significant investments of household time and labor, which disproportionately burden women. Prevailing gender inequalities may therefore constrain the net benefit of livestock ownership for many women and their households in some contexts. Livestock development programs should consider these constraints when designing interventions to ensure that women's participation in livestock production led to improved individual and household outcomes.105

In Kenya, employment and national accounts data do not capture non-market activities (where women predominate) and therefore fail to demonstrate the full contribution of women to the care economy and the external trade sector. Women are time-poor because of their dual roles in the care economy and the labour market. On average, women in Kenya work longer hours (12.9 hours) each day than men do (8.5 hours). This is due to the fact that women spend more time on unpaid domestic work and in Kenya girls spend more time on non-remunerated work in the form of household work compared to boys. Women in rural areas of Kenya are burdened with household tasks such as pounding grain, collecting firewood, fetching water from distant sources, cooking meals for their families, cleaning houses, caring for children, and cooking for the family. Only 30% of households in Kenya have access to potable water and fetching water alone can account for up to 40 hours of a woman’s day, taking from it to 5:25 hours. Men in Kenya spend 258 minutes per week on unpaid domestic work compared to women who spend 1379 minutes.

Childcare is also an important burden for women in Kenya. Women's labour time and flexibility are therefore more constrained than men. The disproportionate cost borne by women in terms of child-rearing and family responsibilities also limits the time that they can devote to economic activities, which means that they have less access to income-generating opportunities. As a result, women become land-poor and be time-poor (combining family duties with running their businesses) and have limited access to financial services. Women are therefore unable to invest in their enterprises or expand them further to access business and trade opportunities. As a result, they are not able to access loans from formal financial institutions or even recover after a fire, theft, or any other setback, which limits the growth of their enterprises and their participation in national and intra-regional markets.

### 5.1.4 Land use and ownership

Land can be used in many ways, including for subsistence farming, cash crop farming, or as collateral for credit to start a trade business. Access to, control over, and ownership of land is influenced by diverse factors that include gender, age, and marital status. According to the 2012 GGII index, women have restricted rights for widows and daughters, and uneven rights for access to land assets and access to non-land assets. Women are also less likely than men to own livestock or other productive assets because these are held in trust for all in the household. Women hold only about 1% of registered land titles in Kenya, compared with 90% owned by men. In addition, women have limited rights over land that is in their names and in men's hands. Therefore even though women have access to use the land, most of them do not have freedom to make decisions about its use. These decisions are made by the husband or male relatives, or by the community if the land is communally owned; in most cases, these decisions are made in the absence of women.

Even under the new Constitution, which allows women to inherit land, customary land law still prevails because it is hard to change people's minds, and women still must fight for the same land rights as men. Equally, women are not always aware that it exists or demand for unmarried or divorced women who have returned to their parents' home, since women are expected to marry and inherit land from their

**Page 158**

## 5.1.5 Agricultural finance

According to a 2019 financial access (FAccess) household survey supported by The Kenya Institute for Public Policy Research and Analysis (KIPPPRA), access to agricultural finance is generally low for both women and men in Kenya, with only about one-third of the population having access to any form of credit or to agricultural finance (both formal and informal sources). Of those, 96.3% access agricultural finance through informal sources such as family members, friends, neighbors, and community networks; comprising local individual arrangements, while a whopping 84.1% of the agricultural finance accessed does not use any form of agricultural finance. Women mainly source finance for agricultural operations from non- productive assets, such as livestock or land, which are often owned by male relatives. Women also have little to no say in control over assets that could be used as collateral in accessing credit from formal sources. In assessing who has access to agricultural finance by gender and age cohorts, the study found that young women (16– 24 years old) were more likely than men of similar ages to have access to any form of credit or agricultural finance.

Women in rural areas were also more likely to access loans from informal sources compared to women living in urban areas. Women aged 35 and above had the highest levels of access to agricultural finance information, with women aged 40–34 years of age having the lowest levels of financial literacy and access to agricultural finance information. Female youth are often denied access to agricultural finance due to low savings, existing debts, lack of collateral, and bad credit history.

### 5.1.6 Access to agricultural training, climate information, and technologies

Degree of access to agricultural training, climate adaptation interventions, and ICT technologies is a function of multiple factors, including gender differences from the perspective of women and men. Existing studies suggest that there are gender differences in access to agricultural training and extension services, with women and youth less likely to receive visits from extension staff or attend trainings. There are several explanations for this trend: First, women have limited decision-making power over farm resources; second, it is often assumed that men are decision makers and female farmers only marginal producers. Larger farms, which tend to be operated by men, are more likely to benefit from targeted training programs, leading to greater efficiency in service provision. Furthermore, time constraints related to the double burden of household tasks and work, as well as social norms affecting their mobility, may negatively affect women’s ability to participate in farmer training.

Climate change is a significant threat to agriculture-related livelihoods, and its impacts amplify preexisting gender inequalities. Climate information services (CIS) are crucial enablers in adapting to climate change

## 5.1.7 Enabling Environment for Gender Mainstreaming in Building the Climate Resilient Value Chain


---
**Page 160**
---

While efforts are being made to create an enabling environment for gender mainstreaming building climate resilient value chains on challenge remain. There’s a lack of data that specifically examines the gendered impacts of climate change agreements and policies making it difficult to understand the unique challenges women farmers face and tailor interventions accordingly.

A significant challenge is the absence of comprehensive gender-disaggregated data in agricultural policies and planning. Without this data, it is difficult to track the specific needs and contributions of women in agriculture. The Kenya Climate Smart Agriculture (KCSA) Implementation Strategy 2017-2027 notes that while there are some efforts to collect gender-disaggregated data at national level, counties have not yet fully implemented disaggregation in their decentralized programs. This is a major problem beyond the collection of basic gender-disaggregated data on agricultural social indicators, for general mainstreaming and impact evaluation information services would require the monitoring of a larger suite of indicators[1]. The Kenya Climate Smart Agriculture (KCSA) Implementation Strategy 2017-2027 notes that this is due to “a misunderstanding of gender issues” among civil society and other actors in the agriculture space, which could be in part fueled by this lack of data. This is also recognized in the CSA implementation plan (2023–2027) where it states that the establishment of ‘gender sensitive’ CSA knowledge at the community, county and national levels[1].

Additionally, there is limited representation from women within the agriculture services themselves, hindering county level administrations' ability to define and provide gender-tailored services. The Climate Smart Agriculture (CSA) Implementation Strategy 2017-2027 notes that in addition to the Social Protection and Safety net programmes to cushion women, youth and vulnerable groups from the impacts of climate change[2], there are presently no mechanisms of participation by these same vulnerable groups in the development of policies and plans destined for them.

Although many countries in the Lake Region Economic Bloc (LREB) have developed climate change policies and action plans, these policies often focus primarily on environmental and climate resilience without explicitly addressing the gender-specific needs of women. The policies tend to prioritize broader climate resilience or adaptation strategies that do not necessarily address how climate impacts women differently than men. For example, while counties like Bomet and Bungoma have developed climate change action plans, they are not specific enough in terms of targeting specific gender-focused actions that would address women’s vulnerabilities in agriculture and resource management[3]. The documents indicate that county officials and climate change committees have been trained on the importance of gender equality but there is a lack of follow-up to ensure implementation. As further illustration, only 7of the existing 13 climate change action plans (Kericho, Nyamira, Kakamega, Siaya, Bomet, Bungoma, and Homa Bay) explicitly mention women for particular focus on agriculture, energy, and capacity-building. The other counties either target vulnerable groups broadly or do not provide explicit gender-sensitive strategies for women[4].

Extension services and agricultural training programs in Kenya still often fail to reach women due to social norms, lack of access to information and resources. The International Food Policy Research Institute (IFPRI) highlights that “women farmers are often not reached by extension services, either because they are not targeted or because the timing and content of the service are not suitable for them”[5]. Additionally,

[1] FAO, State of Agriculture: Women and Agriculture 2011.
[2] Government of Kenya, CSA Implementation Strategy 2017-2027.
[3] Based on an analysis of County climate change action plans.


---
**Page 161**
---

women farmers may face language barriers and gender discrimination from male extension workers, which further discourages their participation.[10] Many countries have an overall shortage of staff, particularly in sectors such as promoting agricultural innovation and adoption. Women also face additional barriers as extension workers, due to low female representation in technical staff. This creates gaps in providing training for delivery, as male-dominated staff may lack the training and awareness to address women's unique needs.[10]

Structural barriers in agriculture and agribusiness severely limit women’s ability to achieve equity, equality, and representation. One of the most significant issues is limited access to land and financial resources. Women often lack land ownership rights, which hinders their ability to use land as collateral for loans, preventing them from accessing these vital services.[10] Furthermore, financial institutions tend to view women as higher-risk borrowers due to the smaller size of their enterprises and their limited access to capital, perpetuating the financial exclusion of women in agribusiness.

In addition, gender bias in leadership and decision-making roles within agricultural cooperatives and businesses further entrenches inequality. Women are underrepresented in leadership positions and key decision-making roles, which often results in policies and initiatives that do not adequately address their needs.[10] This imbalance in representation prevents women from influencing policies and initiatives that affect agricultural value chains, further marginalizing their role in the sector. Cultural expectations often relegate women to less powerful roles within households, limiting their participation in decision-making and profitable markets[10].

Lastly, access to markets, finance and agricultural extension services are another structural issue. Women frequently lack the networks and market access necessary to sell their products on a larger, more profitable scale.[10] This not only limits their income but also a defines bias (both cultural and structural) against women in financial institutions, which feeds the perception that women are “less solvable” than men; for all the reasons cited above.

### 5.1.8 DEB Case 1: Gender Analysis of Climate Risk and Value Chains Consultation Survey

The following gender analysis draws on the Climate Risk and Value Chains Consultation Survey (completed as part of the formulation process of the project), which queried 115 stakeholders (85 males and 28 females) from various sectors, including agriculture, livestock, fisheries, forestry, and water. The survey collected climate information, gender differentiation in types of climate information needed, and gendered intra-household decision-making in crop, livestock, and poultry production. Given the sample size of the survey participants is not large enough to yield inferential statistics, descriptive statistics are reported by gender of the respondent.

Value chain representation among male and female respondents (n=115) is shown in the table below.
Most female respondents were from the Dairy value chain (12), followed by African leafy vegetables (6), fruit trees (4), and coffee (3). Most male respondents were from a wide variety, with the majority in African leafy vegetables (31), followed by dairy (18), poultry (17), fruit trees (12), coffee (3), and tea (2).

### Equal access to climate information.

For the first question, "Do you think women have equal access to climate information as compared to men during input supply, food production and harvesting?", respondents were able to choose yes, no, or unsure. Cross tab analysis by gender revealed that most male (39.5%) and female (42.2%) respondents reported that women do not have equal access to climate information too. 60.5% of males (18/30) and 57.8% of females (42/73) reported that men had equal access to climate information, with 16.7% of males (5/30) and 13.3% (9/68) of female respondents reporting uncertainty.

Results for the application of climate information for decision-making in poultry production show that most of both male and female respondents answered decisions were made "together as a family." This was followed by women only, and by men only. These results echo the findings for both dairy and crop production. The results also suggest that women are more involved in poultry production than they are for the other two types of production. For future lines of questioning related to gender and decision making on agricultural changes using climate information, it would be helpful to assess the level of commercialization in the household (e.g., whether the family is a "commercial" or "subsistence" farm), the factors leading to success' related to women's involvement in poultry production, and whether they can maintain decision-making power only if their production and sales are 'supplementary' to other sources of male-derived income in the home. It would also be helpful to clarify what types of decisions, over which nodes of the value chain Are made, disaggregated by sex- and age.

### 5.2 Gendered participation, performance, and empowerment in the six CRLCSA priority value chains

This section's analysis integrates data and studies from the desk review, as well as data collected as part of a qualitative study during the CRLCSA project proposal development in 2022. The selected value chains


---
**Page 166**
---

(coffee, tea, fruit trees, indigenous bark vegetables, poultry, and dairy) have distinct gender dynamics, with women and men having different roles and responsibilities across the value chain. Across these value chains, there are differences in the level of market integration, access to resources, domestic accessibility, and women's participation in the value chain, their position on the value chain, and in their access to resources for decision-making and governance. These gendered trade-offs have important implications for CRLCSA’s practices for gender and social inclusion.

Studies in East Africa, including Kenya have found that gender issues permeate every node of the value chain, and that existing gender and social inequalities have real consequences regarding who are the 'winners' and 'losers'. These gendered trade-offs work to encourage or discourage women from engaging in certain aspects of value chains, including informal versus formal markets. They also influence women's access to information and communication of products, or whether to pursue entrepreneurship and small business.

Most pertinent to the study sites are gender issues in the commercialization of agricultural and livestock products. A recent study in East Africa found that as smallholder farmers increasingly marketed and commercialized their produce, women were more likely to lose out on proceeds and decision-making they had previously had access to.10

Despite specificities across sectors, similar barrier limits the benefit women receive from agricultural and livestock development. These constraints, which occur at multiple levels, include: the invisibility of women's contributions in production; gendered division of labor that reinforces women's limited and precarious control over resources; discriminatory social and gender norms that hinder women's voice and influence in decision-making and governance, and exclusionary institutions such as resource access and land tenure. In addition to these constraints at the individual level within food systems, changes in each sector are required in women's agency, access to and control over resources, gender norms, and policies and governance.10 Such changes can improve dietary outcomes, gender equality and women's empowerment, economic livelihood outcomes, resilience and environmental sustainability.

## 5.2.1 Coffee

Coffee is responsible for an estimated 15% of employment in the agricultural sector, having declined from 21% in the mid-1990s. The national production landscape comprises both smallholder and large-scale estates. Smallholder coffee farmers are estimated to number around 700,000 smallholder farmers. These smallholder farmers have an average farm size of only 0.25 ha per hectare yield at well below 400kg of green coffee. At the beginning of the chain are producers, comprising both estates and smallholders. The majority of these producers are organized into cooperatives that are functionally mobilization units for primary processing and marketing. [16] Gender equality within coffee cooperatives suggests there are significant gaps between women's participation and their influence from coffee production to consumption.

Acceptability of women’s participation in the coffee value chain. While coffee is traditionally considered to be a man’s cash crop, recent efforts have been put towards mainstreaming gender and youth in smallholder coffee supply chains. Women represent 50% or more of the workforce on estates but largely concentrated on their labor contribution to coffee production, as over 95% of coffee farms are owned by men. Thus, the group who produce Kenya’s coffee have little or no access to the income from the commodity. Government has sought to create equity among farmers and youth with respect to active and voluntary engagement in coffee production.

Gendered positions in the coffee value chain. In Kisoro County, age and gender norms shape gendered positions in cooperative societies. For example, the average age for coffee cooperative society members was 57 years old, and that norms around elder men's dominance hampered young women from ascending to leadership roles. The same norms also prevented young men from assuming leadership roles, while men farmers in Kenyan cooperatives differently due to gender rules and highly separated divisions of labor. A recent study by the International Coffee Organization (ICO) found that Fairtrade certification had only marginal alteration of current inequitable gendered roles, nor did Fairtrade challenge women's subordination to a significant degree – thus Fairtrade interventions only partially empower women and address some of their barriers to participation in coffee cooperatives. In addition, women with disabilities are disadvantaged in terms of coffee production, as these subgroups often lack the land, capital, technical knowledge, and technical skills necessary to farm, and that the institutional context for coffee production is highly masculinized, with elder men predominating in terms of land rights and derivative income from the sale of coffee.

Gendered Access to resources in the coffee value chain. Gendered arrangements within coffee cooperatives in Kenya have also mediated the role of gendered market orientation. In a recent study from Kenya, women farmers were found to be less likely than men to access markets opportunities, limited access to resources, and have less say over coffee production’s planning and supply segment. Women continually face significant barriers to accessing information about coffee prices, quality standards, and technical information. These barriers are caused by interesting intersections of socio-cultural practices, land tenure system that privileges men, and discriminatory beliefs that hinder women and youth participation in cooperatives.

Coffee® value chain includes picking coffee cherries and delivering to pulping stations/factories. Primarily processing is carried out by farmer-owned factories. Inputs purchased from agro-dealers or directly from the cooperatives are bought by full-time farmers for their own factory activities. Marketing contracts are made with actors registered with the Coffee Board of Kenya. Climate effects such as prolonged drought have affected productivity over time. Technologies to address this include irrigation and agro-forestry.

### 5.2.1.6

In Kenya, smallholder farmers grow tea in contract with the Kenya Tea Development Authority (KTDA). KTDA is an independent and private entity owned by smallholder tea farmers and offers management and professional services to individual factories and companies. The Tea Board of Kenya (TBK) also works together with the farmers by offering management, professional services, leadership on production, and marketing. In the 2015-2016 TBK Strategic Plan, there is no mention of gender roles in either the KTDA or TBK strategic plans. Despite the significant role women play in smallholder tea production, there are extreme gender inequalities in access to and control over the benefits derived from tea production. In a study conducted by the Kenya Agricultural Research Institute (KARI), women were discriminated against in access to and control over income derived from tea; however, women-headed households had access to and control over tea-derived income. The Kenya Tea Development Authority (KTDA) has been working with KARI on gender equality issues within its factories since 2014. The study established that women from male-headed households had more roles in tea production than their husbands. The women were assigned the roles that were tedious and took many hours like tea plucking and transporting, while the men undertook seasonal roles like tea planting and pruning.

### Gender roles in tea production

| Activity | Men | Women |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Land preparation | X |  |
| Tea planting | X | X |
| Weeding |  | X |
| Pruning |  | X |
| Picking |  | X |
| Tea transportation |  | X |

Factors such as KTDA policy of requiring men to be owners, community norms, lack of title deeds by women, and cultural taboos make it difficult for women to materially benefit from tea production. Whilst were able to access and control tea benefits because they had acquired the tea owners' rights after the demise of their husbands and/or inheritance from their fathers.

### Gendered Positions in the tea value chain

A recent survey in Nyamira county of 132 Tea SACCO members across five tea factories ranked barriers to women's participation as lack of shares in the tea factory; lack


---
**Page 170**
---

of land ownership rights; fear to run against men in elections; low levels of education; gender roles; and lack of experience in politics in general, including women head households, young women, and women with disabilities. Such weaknesses are terms of tea production, as these subsectors often lack land, seed, capital, resources, networks, and technical skills necessary to farm, and that the institutional context for coffee production is highly masculinized, with elder men predominating in terms of land rights and derivative income from the sale of coffee.

Gender Access to resources in the tea value chain. Land registration and contestations over land ownership are common in Kenya’s tea sector. The gendered division of labor within households and gender dynamics influence access and control of benefits accrued from tea farming.175 A recent study found that intra-household dynamics can be a major barrier for women to participate effectively in the tea value chain, as they do not have the knowledge or skills required to properly care for the tea crop. For example, manure or fertilizer which is intended for the tea cash crop is diverted by women without the knowledge of men to feed crops which they feel have control over.

This affects their income and food security.


Findings from CRUSA Fieldwork: Tea is grown in mainly large estates and smallholder farms. Value chain actors include smallholder farmers (majority older males) farming an average of 2 acres. Farmers source inputs from large factories on credit advanced via financial providers such as SACCOs. Climate smart technologies are used to improve yields, especially on sloping land – to prevent runoff and increase infiltration. Terraces or also in place to curb erosion.


5.2.3 Fruit Trees


Acceptability of women’s participation in the fruit tree value chain: Fruit tree, African leafy vegetables, and poultry are value chains that are traditionally viewed as appropriate for women – women customarily have had greater autonomy and decision-making power in these activities and are more able to retain income earned from them.176 However, a recent study found that while women’s participation is higher than that the profits from these activities will remain small, not did compromise the position of the male head of household as the primary breadwinner. Thus, the social acceptability of women’s participation in these value chains has been limited. In contrast to fruit trees and African leafy vegetables, which are seen as the banana-value chain, women’s ability to participate in income sharing involved complex intra-household dynamics. Women were found to be more likely than men to engage in activities that are framed as being more appropriate for men; such as exotic avocados which have a major export market in Kenya.177 However, new initiatives such as the African Continental Free Trade Area (African) could


---
**Page 171**
---

provide gender-and youth-responsive solutions to export market barriers through targeted investments.104

Gender Positions in the fruit tree value chain. What results of a recent study in Kenya's avocado value chain suggest is that in the more commercialized and well-developed export chains, upgrading strategies vary for the different typologies of women (women-headed households versus women in male-headed households). While women in women-headed households may require limited efforts such as tailoring financial services to meet their needs, women in male-headed households are more likely to need support from their produce to allow them to produce quality fruits and access lucrative markets. Women in male headed households also face a greater risk of losing their livelihoods because of the dependence of producer groups to enable them to upgrade as chain integrators and chain owners. In less commercialized and less optimistic avocado value chains, limited efforts may be required to upgrade women along the chain, but the need to change gender norms is still present.105 This is also reflected in the work of other organizations, thereby calling for the need to institutionalize gender-sensitive policies in the governance of existing organizations and groups as a platform to introduce the new variety.106

Gendered Access to resources in the fruit tree value chain. What results from this research show is that women have traditionally been excluded from decision-making processes related to the commercialization of fruit trees but has led to a dynamic shift in production with more men taking active roles in the value chain (for example, bananas, avocados). This has led to the displacement of women from the high value chain with men taking up these roles.107 The study also found that women's participation in decision-making processes was limited and that the participation of women in income sharing was partially determined by the presence of off-farm income, so that women in male-headed households had a higher probability in taking part in household decision making.108

2.4 African Leafy Vegetables

Acceptability of women's participation in the African leafy vegetables value chain. Traditional food crop marketing, including African leafy vegetables, tends to follow distinct gender roles, with women having greater social acceptability in cultivating and retaining the sale of these plants from other value chains.109 However, recent evidence suggests that although African leafy vegetables have traditionally been considered a woman's crop, women vendors in emerging markets are not necessarily empowered to earn equal income as men.

Gender Positions in value chain. Compared to crops that have historically been commercialized, there is less data on the gendered positions of women in the African leafy vegetable value chains in South Asia. However, findings from the CRILCA fieldwork suggest that the value chain is dominated by women over the age of 53. It has also shown how gender production of African leafy vegetables changes with different life stages.
