### Gendered Access to Resources in the African Leafy Vegetables Value Chain

The resources required to engage in leafy vegetables production vary significantly between Eastern and Western Kenya.

#### Findings from CRISCA Fieldwork:

- **In Nyakach**: Vegetables such as cabbages, fodder plants, and black nightshades are grown by women. Chugua (Vegetable) occupy less than one acre of the average 1-acre family farm.
- **Value chain dominated by adults over 35 years with majority being women**.
- **Production is hampered by unpredictable seasons that are dependent on rainfall production**.

This makes it difficult for farmers to plan and invest in their farms. The market price can set immediately after production (when prices are low) to avoid food loss. Climate smart technologies in place include sun-drying, use of organic manure, use of certified seed and on-farm practices such as spacing.

#### 5.2.3 Poultry

##### Acceptability of Women's Participation in the Poultry Value Chain:

Women’s participation in poultry production in the tropics is significant. In Western Kenya, virtually every household keeps small flocks of between 5-30 chickens. Poultry production, both of indigenous chickens and commercial varieties is crucial to the wellbeing of rural households as it provides (and egg) production are important sources of inexpensive animal protein for the family diet.

Indigenous chicken breeds in Western Kenya are highly adaptable and less capital and labour intensive than other livestock species such as large ruminants. Indigenous chickens have been shown to be more productive, disease resistant, and highly accepted and expected of rural women. While women are increasingly taking on enterprise ownership in indigenous poultry production, there are emerging challenges regarding their participation as enterprises become commercially successful.

As the commercialization of poultry production becomes more commercially successful, there is a growing risk that women will lose out on previously controlled incomes and decision-making power.

Gendered Positions in the poultry value chain. Women are generally responsible for the feeding and care of birds, and do not face socio-cultural or economic barriers regarding poultry ownership.120 Women's preferences thus shape the demand for poultry feed in Kenya123 and traditional gendered knowledge and attitudes shape poultry feeding practices.124

Gendered Access to resources in the poultry value chain. While women have easy access to poultry animals, they face challenges related to production information, access to markets, extension services, and agro-vet services, including vaccines.125

Findings from CRICSA fieldwork: "The poultry value chain is attractive because of several factors such as its ability to involve many households and as a source of protein, hence a ready market. The improved keratiy is also an added advantage since it matures faster than the local breeds. It takes 12 weeks to mature and the ready market. Producers face the challenge of getting chicks, as well as high costs for feeds." To increase competitiveness producers such as the Victoria Kuku Cooperative are focusing on interventions such as improved feed management, vaccination, and breeding. These interventions have led to an improvement in production practices. Climate smart technologies include manure management through poultry units, water harvesting, use of incubators for hatching and making home rations."

### 5.2.6 Dairy

Acceptability of women's participation in the dairy value chain. Recent reviews indicate that gender roles and dynamics greatly influence dairy production practices in Kenya. Women tend to be responsible for most management tasks on smallholder farms such as feeding, milking, cleaning, housing, providing veterinary health, knowing when a cow is in heat and requires mating or artificial insemination (AI), manure removal, and milk the cow. Yet, despite their contributions to dairy labour, women are often marginalized from decision-making processes related to livestock production. They have limited access to advisory services and training, decision-making associated with the animals (buying/selling), and do not receive commensurate income from the sale of milk. It is therefore, then, that because the burden of daily work falls on them, women are often seen as "working women" of the household.126 However, it must be considered that a new technology that may have an impact on women. Furthermore, consideration must be given to the gendered effects of a shift from traditional extensive to intensive commercial system.

process. The dairy value chain in Kenya has been a large source of potential investment in recent years, with dairy income promoting for environmental sustainability and animal welfare.

Gender positions in the dairy value chain. Women are also customarily responsible for decisions regarding livestock care and feeding. In addition to this, women have increasingly involved in the cultivation of improved forages and feeds for cows as required for low emission dairy development. However, women are still not fully recognized by their communities for engaging in dairy production and intensification, but that their opportunities for leadership are curtailed by age related discrimination. Women tend to benefit more directly from their involvement in informal dairy value chains than from formal ones.

Gendered access to resources in dairy value chain. Women have been able to gain access to agricultural inputs, include improved feeds, vaccines, and other supplies through producer organizations. However, evidence that direct payments incentivize women's participation and empowerment in dairy development is limited. The lack of information regarding women's access to resources, ownership over dairy cows, and decision-making rights to cattle has highlighted the importance of intra-household relations, and whether there can be a shift within an household regarding who "owns" dairy.

Findings from CRICSA fieldwork: Many farmers are practicing dairy and there is huge potential to increase productivity. Production is challenged by poor breeds kept by farmers and low knowledge of breeding (e.g., heat detection, lack of fodder preservation). One of the successful dairy cooperatives in Kenya is a women's cooperative. The women's cooperative uses the hub-model to mobilize members, bulk milk and provide services such as AS drugs etc to members for a fee. This model has been successful because it addresses issues related to education and husbandry. These include zero grazing, fodder production, conversion of manure to biogas and stage making. Other interventions have been the change of breed to Ayrshire from the Fresian which is a highly feeder.

# 5.2.7 UREB Case 2: Qualitative Gender Assessment - Gendered Participation, Performance, and Empowerment in Agricultural Value Chains in Target Sites

Qualitative data collected from leaders of cooperatives in different value chains in the targeted countries yielded a number of insights that are consistent with the research findings from existing studies. Leaders representing two dairy cooperatives, one coffee cooperative, 1 African leafy vegetable cooperative, and 1 poultry cooperative were interviewed. A summary of their responses is provided below.

## What are the barriers to entry and/or requirements for men's and women's active engagement at any node of the value chain?

Socio-cultural norms play a key role in determining the nature of involvement of men and women in the value chains. In general, men control the productive assets and income while women participate more in the production/management, while the youth dominate transport. In the coffee value chain, the youth were found to be involved in activities such as planting, weeding, harvesting, transporting to factory.

For dairy, women and youth were found to participate more in feeding of the animals, milking, and transport while men largely make decisions on how the income is used.

Non-ownership of production assets especially land by women and youth limit their participation in production and subsequently engagement at different nodes of the VC. In most cultures within UREB countries, women are not allowed to own or inherit land hence they have no control over resources on land owned by men. The land itself is rarely subdivided hence the youth are unlikely to have a say on how it is used.

Limited capital to start a business and buy inputs required at the various points of the value chain. For example, animal feed and supplements remain unaffordable to many households thereby affecting production. Poor prices paid on the farmers' produce has not helped matters either.

Long-distances to the cooperatives or collection points especially for the dairy value chain is a problem that plagues most women in the targeted sites as was reported in both Handi and Tandu Ntosi as a major challenge, more so in the afternoon and evening.

In terms of opportunities to serve in the management of cooperatives, it was felt that both men and women have opportunities to participate, but women are not able to fully seize the opportunities due to competing household responsibilities. Women were generally expected to prioritize taking care of household chores as opposed to serving in the cooperative.

## What are the disparities in men's and women's ability to maintain or improve their position in the value chain?

Most of the cooperatives visited provide several benefits to members, included are collection and value addition; agrovet services (feed supplements, drugs, fertilizers, seeds etc.); transport; loans; access to markets; extension services including capacity building on climate smart technologies.


---
**Page 176**
---

Due to the low income and limited access to inputs, women end up not using the recommended inputs such as fertilizers, certified seeds, fertilizers, etc. This limits their ability to improve their position in the value chain and increase their overall gross earnings.

What are the differences in men's and women's ability to access and control income, assets, or other facets of well-being derived from value chain participation?

While women manage the assets including land, livestock etc., men generally own and control the assets and income realized from the same. Examples given include payments from dairy, tea and coffee cooperatives which are channeled to men; yet it is the women and youth who take care of crops and livestock.

Social beliefs and practices tend to favor women as far as poultry and African leafy vegetables are concerned. The true value chains are not held in high esteem by men hence allowing women more room to make decisions on how they want to participate in the value chain.

What steps/changes are needed so that women can control the benefits of their participation in agricultural value chains to make and carry out strategic decisions about their own lives?

Sensitization and mobilization to enable women and youth to participate more in the value chain remains critical. Awareness creation and sensitization are important in highlighting the benefits associated with being a member of cooperatives. In some cases, there were cultural barriers to participation including leadership within the coops. In the case of Uchelwa Dairy in Nandi County, sensitization and mobilization efforts saw the number of women increase from less than 10% in 2009 to over 85% by 2016. The current membership stands at over 8000 members. Four women currently sit on the board which consists of 11 members.

In a bid to enhance access and control of the proceeds by women in agricultural value chains, some cooperatives like Meebod are taking pro-active steps like making payments in the evenings when both men and women are at home. This has helped women make decisions about how they want to spend their money. Provision of transport especially for collecting milk from far off and difficult terrains using motor bikes is helping women as it significantly reduces the time they would otherwise spend delivering milk to the dairy cooperatives, thus enabling them to engage in other income generating activities.

The dairy cooperatives give women loans to buy motorbikes for use in collection and delivery of milk. Capacity building is also needed equip members with the necessary skills and best practices to enhance their productivity and profitability. The above strategies can be adapted within their respective value chain structures. Flexibility in payments: Women prefer weekly payments to enable them deal with household/subsistence needs. Their participation in decision making has seen Lechigo Dairy Cooperative adopt weekly payments to members to meet this need.

5.3 Gender dynamics of cooperative membership, leadership, and capacity in the LREB by county and value chain

### 5.3.1 Cooperatives and producer organizations

Shifts in global agriculture have led more women into formal roles in agricultural and livestock industry as small holder producers and cooperative members. Inclusion of women in these institutions, however, does not necessarily translate to their empowerment or increased participation in decision-making roles. A transformative change in gender relations not only requires changes in women and men’s attitudes and capacities and in the relationship between men and women, but also progress at the household, community, and institutional levels. The literature on cooperative membership and leadership in Kenya found that empowering women as productive cooperative members requires not only technical training and access to credit but also an inclusive social and political environment that supports expanded choices for women and men.

Access to information and collateral challenges are some of the other barriers women face in agricultural financing, but increasingly women are coming together in groups to try to bridge this gender gap. Women groups are often more cohesive than men’s groups and have a better chance at success. However, existing women groups, which are stable and outlive most projects and initiatives, but are largely unsupported. Directly involving women in production initiatives has the potential to increase such production.

For example, in Bonet and Nandi counties in Western Kenya, there is a strong male dominance in dairy cooperatives because cows culturally belong to men. Even though many women are highly involved in dairy production, they are often not recognized as leaders of their co-ops. Women who have been trained in leadership skills are often less confident in their leadership abilities than men and may be hesitant to be elected as leaders of co-ops. At producer organization level, experts stated that men are often not willing to nominate women

# 5.2 UEB Case 3: Quantitative Analysis of Cooperatives

This analysis looks at intra-group comparisons between men, women, male youth, and female youth members among the cooperatives surveyed. "Youth" members were defined as those between the ages of 18-34.

## Analysis 1: Gendered membership rates

In assessing gendered membership rates by county (Tables 1,2), the cooperative survey found that Kisi, Burgoma, and Nyamira have the highest numbers of female members. Kisumu, Busia, and Transazia have the lowest numbers of female members. For female youth members, Nandi, Kericho, and Bungoma were the highest, while Homa Bay, Siaya, and Transazia had the lowest number of female youth members. For youth membership, Kericho, Burgoma, and Kisii have the highest numbers of youth, while Homa Bay, Busia, and Transazia have the lowest (for both total youth and female youth).

In assessing gendered membership rates by value chain (Tables 3-4), the cooperative survey found that coffee, dairy, and tea had that highest numbers of female and total youth membership. ## Summary analysis of cooperative survey findings

Gender data from the Cooperative census reveals the existing inequalities between women and men as cooperatives across. The 14 target counties, where women constituted 78.8% of members, only 20.5% of cooperatives were led by women compared to 69.5% being led by men. The gender gap is also present among youth, with 13.2% of cooperative members being young women compared to only 5% of members being young men. In evaluating gendered membership rates by value chain, only poultry and indigenous vegetables cooperatives had a higher proportion of female members than male members in formal, high-value commodity chains, men made up 73.6% of coffee members, and 91.2% of tea members. Dairy value chains had a closer gender parity with 46.4% women members. Among the less commercialized value chains, there was a higher proportion of female members in indigenous vegetables and tea cooperatives as well of female and male youth participation. However, gender gaps still existed when youth as young women were the least represented in membership across all six value chains.

# Summary of gender-based constraints by value chain

## All value chains

Women, especially female-headed households and single women living with disabilities, widowers, and young women, have limited access to land and technology, inhibiting their capacity to adopt climate-resilient value chain practices.

Women in all value chains face challenges related to lack of access to climate information, agricultural market information, advisory networks, including information disseminated via traditional agricultural extension services, and financial resources.

Women have disproportionate labor responsibilities and are often clustered in less productive sectors such as livestock production, post-harvest, and value-added activities. Women also face barriers in accessing inputs for their enterprises from them out of expensive farming technologies (e.g., irrigation), and limited financial resources to access smallholder credit schemes.

Gender norms and socio-cultural practices limit women, including married women, in income-generating activities that would compete with the care responsibilities they have at home.

## Commercial value chains/traditionally male-centric

Due to a lack of recognition of women's contributions, women are discouraged from entering the most productive nodes of the value chain. Women are prevented from accessing land for grazing and lack access to veterinary services, including vaccines, supplements, and feeds. Limited infrastructure for feed storage and transportation, and for sale of livestock products also discourages women.

Institutional constraints via the Kenyan Dairy Board

Women are not encouraged to participate in tea plantations (especially among male managers and female workers). The women were assigned the roles that were tedious and took much longer than those given to men. Women were often subjected to gendered sexism like tea planting and pruning.

Despite their significant role play in smallholder tea production, there are extreme gender inequalities in access to and control over the benefits of tea production. In particular, women from FHT (female-headed households) were able to access and control tea benefits because they had inherited or received gifts after the demise of their husbands and/or inheritance from their fathers.

Institutional constraints via Kenya Tea Development Authority (KTDA)

Women are often excluded from participating in KTDA community norms, lack of title deeds by women, gender-biased culture, and attitudes towards women's participation in tea production.

## Coffee

Leadership is being both a prerequisite for membership, and a critical barrier for women due to structural laws reinforced by socio-cultural norms.


---
**Page 185**
---

Even in situations where women are widowed, structural and legal barriers may constrain their ability to assume ownership of their family land. In many African countries, women have limited access to credit, training, and other livestock production; the barriers to initial entry are lower. However, the institutional context for poultry production is masculinized, making it difficult for married women and young women (living at home) to maintain income received from poultry.

African Leafy Vegetables

Traditional food crop marketing, including African leafy vegetables, tends to be dominated by men. Women have traditionally been responsible for planting, cultivating, and harvesting these plants but are often excluded from the decision-making process that leads to their sale. This is because women's roles in agriculture are traditionally considered a woman's work.

Poultry

Even in situations where women are widowed, structural and legal barriers may constrain their ability to assume ownership of their family land. In many African countries, women have limited access to credit, training, and other livestock production; the barriers to initial entry are lower. However, the institutional context for poultry production is masculinized, making it difficult for married women and young women (living at home) to maintain income received from poultry.

### Strategies to integrate gender in CRILSA

In acknowledging the challenges and necessity of addressing existing gender and social inequalities in Kenya, all project outcomes and activities have corresponding gender outcomes and activities, presented in the GAP. To achieve the project's impact, outcomes, and co-benefits, GAP proposes activities that are gender-responsive within a broad gender-transformative approach (GTA). Gender-responsive activities are those that address existing gender inequalities in agriculture by addressing root causes of these inequalities (e.g., strategies, techniques, practices) that reduce gender gaps in agriculture related to decision-making, labor burden, access to resources, and technology. The GTA is an approach that seeks to develop a broader gender-transformative approach means that those gender-responsive activities are designed around the fundamental aim of addressing the root causes of these gender gaps and social inequalities to ensure long-term sustainability.

The Gender Action Plan (GAP) for the proposed project tackles gender inequalities across several priority areas: in agri-climate adaptation and mitigation[1]. These include closing gender gaps and existing inequalities in participation (at intra-household level and within producer organizations/cooperatives), workload distribution, access to technology, and decision-making. Gender-responsive activities aim to reduce workloads and negative impacts on women's access and use of production resources (such as agri-climate


---
**Page 187**
---

The project addresses gender dimensions within its design and implementation, identifies and integrates interventions to provide gender-responsive and transformative results through the GtA approach. It aims to address the underlying discriminatory socio-cultural and gender norms that currently perpetuate inequality and constrain women’s and men’s access to resources and opportunities in rural areas. The project will use a gender transformative approach (GtA) to address the root causes of these inequalities, which are deeply embedded at multiple levels: individual, household, community, multi-level approach that includes women and men across the project - at farm, cooperative, private sector or government level. It will also ensure that the project does not create new inequalities in a social safeguard 'backdrop' to ensure that women can concretely benefit from the project and existing inequalities are not exacerbated so that no one is left behind from efforts to address climate change.

Specific activities will draw upon tested GtA methodologies, for example, GALS to engage in capacity building and training exercises among beneficiaries and project facilitators to discuss the issues underlying gender inequality and how these can be addressed at different levels: individual, household, community, multi-level approach that includes women and men across the project - at farm, cooperative, private sector or government level. It will also ensure that the project does not create new inequalities in a social safeguard 'backdrop' to ensure that women can concretely benefit from the project and existing inequalities are not exacerbated so that no one is left behind from efforts to address climate change.

The project explicitly seeks to redefine and transform gender norms and relationships to redress existing inequalities. For example, the project pursues to challenge and change gender roles and responsibilities and cultural practices that perpetuate inequality and limit women’s access to resources and opportunities to mitigate and adapt climate resilient technologies. The logic for using a GtA approach is in alignment with the latest FAO Policy on Gender Equality 2020-2030[1], which states that, “across regions rural women still face major gender inequalities in terms of access to resources, services and decision-making power, and are unable to reap the full benefits of their work. The root cause of these discriminations lies in social norms, attitudes, and beliefs, which are deeply embedded at multiple levels: individual, household, community, multi-level approach that includes women and men across the project - at farm, cooperative, private sector or government level. It will also ensure that the project does not create new inequalities in a social safeguard 'backdrop' to ensure that women can concretely benefit from the project and existing inequalities are not exacerbated so that no one is left behind from efforts to address climate change.

[1] FAO. 2020. FAO Policy on Gender Equality, 2020-2030. Rome

**Page 188**

### Gender transformative approaches address not only gender roles and power dynamics, but also institutional arrangements and inclusion as key barriers to equality, justice, and the achievement of global development outcomes.

A main goal of gender transformative approaches involves the creation of an enabling social environment and more equitable inclusion in formal and informal institutions that support expanded opportunities for women. The GALS (Gender Analysis and Learning System) methodology is a transformative approach from others efforts to integrate gender into agricultural development involving: (1) Development of an understanding of people's context, perceptions, and experiences which can reflect choices and constraints; (2) Engagement with both women and men, as both have a role and stake in gender transformative change; (3) Commitment to addressing unequal power relations and to challenging oppressive norms and practices that are often rooted in cultural traditions. The GALS methodology is based on the belief that gender equality is not only an end in itself but also a means of increasing productivity and improving livelihoods.

GALS is a transformative methodology that goes beyond the symptoms of gender inequality to tackle the underlying causes of inequalities (norms, attitudes, behaviors) and generates positive change in areas of awareness, behavior, and action at multiple levels: individual, household, community, organization, and institutions. GALS was chosen as it is designed to be used at project level and has been used in projects working on value chains, agribusiness, and enterprise development, agriculture and rural finance, and in the context of climate change adaptation.

The GALS methodology has been used by more than 100 organizations around the world. Some have used GALS methodology to improve the livelihoods and change gender relations among more than 200,000 women and men, mainly in Africa and Asia.


Gender transformative approaches in agriculture can be framed as seeking to foster change at multiple levels: household, community, organization, institution. Gender transformative approaches focus simultaneously on the level of the individual with individual capacities, attitudes, agency and actions; at the relational level with the expectations that shape relationships between people in the home, in groups and in organizations; and at the structural level with norms and practices that are often rooted in cultural traditions.

The GALS methodology has been used by more than 100 organizations around the world. Some have used it to improve livelihoods and change gender relations among more than 200,000 women and men, mainly in Africa and Asia.


Figure 1 provides a visual perspective of the potential target changes in smallholder agriculture about where efforts and resources may need to focus for the design and implementation of interventions that seek to contribute to gender transformative change processes. These are the entry points for GTA that the CRLCA project aims to tap into:

- Exploring opportunities to engage with financial service providers, input dealers, and leaders governing formal or informal land tenure may be necessary to transform structure and dynamics rooted in institutions and the value chain.

## GTA Design and Implementation Fundamentals

Within the project, GTA will be mainstreamed across activities to support improvements in social inclusion and women's meaningful participation in CRLCSA value chains. This activity is designed to ensure that project activities, technology transfer, and support to cooperatives also contribute to the achievement of more inclusive and gender-transformative results for women farmers at various stages.

As noted in the Gender Assessment and GAP, ensuring equitable participation and inclusion requires a more strategic approach to GTA implementation across all project activities and at different levels and various stages. The activity therefore aims to influence change in the way in which women, youth, and PWD participate in agriculture (beyond the primary production stage), benefit from their work (ensuring they receive fair compensation for their labor), and have a say in decisions that affect them, including in households, farmer organizations, cooperatives and community or country policies, where gender relations and women's empowerment is boosted, contributing to SDGs.


---
**Page 190**
---

The project will ensure that Gender and Social Inclusion (GSI) principles are mainstreamed and integrated into all activities, training, materials, consultations, and processes. Gender focal points will be identified in country and district EE, as well as within farmer organizations. At the start of the project staff at the executing entities, program coordination unit, counties and financial institutions will receive mandatory training on GSI principles, including gender equality, gender-based violence (GBV), gender-responsive agriculture (GRA), Gender-Based Violence (GBV) and in the application of the FAO Grievance and Redress Mechanism (GRM) to handle such incidents and ensure safe working conditions for women, PLWD, and vulnerable groups (vuln. agri. workers). The project will also develop a gender management plan (GMP), including specific procedures for SEA, including confidential reporting and safe and ethical documentation. To ensure the GMP is accessible, several ways to access the GMP (such as through a contact point) will be written and anonymous feedback mechanisms will be established. A survey/questionnaire on clear information on how to access the GMP will be widely disseminated among stakeholders. The GMP will be designed to be easily understandable for all actors involved in the project, including those working directly with gender issues and increasing awareness of women, PLWD, and vulnerable groups on their rights.

The project supports the design of tailored climate information services and technologies (activity 1.1.2, 1.1.4 and 1.1.5) for women to address the unique challenges which hinder women’s access to information and knowledge about climate change and its impacts. The project will also support the country’s organization over action climates. When providing training and support to farmer organizations, the project will also ensure (under activity 3.1.1 and 3.1.2) that cooperatives and other business units have gender equality policies in place. To ensure women are able to participate fully in their activities, the project supports the formation and strengthening of women farmer group to provide a platform for women to share experiences and knowledge on climate change adaptation and mitigation. Targeted trainings for FFS and cooperatives, following the tested Agriterra and FAD approaches to develop leadership, participation and meaningful inclusion of women, youth and persons living with disabilities in agricultural activities will be provided. The training will focus on gender equality and how they were discouraged by the gender norms, including trading and selling agriculture commodities, decision making in adoption of new technologies and practices to address climate impacts. The approaches are the Women’s Empowerment in Agriculture Index (WEAI) and the Gender Equality Assessment Learning System (GEALS). These trainings will focus on all aspects of the value chain, accelerating the inclusion of women into agricultural activities. The training will be provided by a gender expert who will support the process of building female leadership in agricultural cooperatives during an assessment a clear need to improve inclusivity arises, to address pervasive gender norms and increase the women’s participation in decision-making processes.

During the Women's Leadership Workshops the role of women as members, entrepreneurs and leaders are discussed and together with male and female farmers an action plan is developed to improve the participation of women in the cooperative. Previous Agriterra Women leadership workshops have led to the following solutions:

- Reduced reluctance to women’s participation in the cooperative and changed gender based by-laws.
- Changed the minimum amount a potential lender has to deliver to the cooperative before they can be offered credit from 1000 Kgs to 500 Kgs. Women often own fewer coffee trees or have smaller plots than males, therefore the threshold of 1000 kgs is a larger barrier for them to get elected than their male counterparts.
- Reduced reluctance to transfer ownership and benefits of coffee trees to women within a family.
- Plan workshops at more suitable times for women/mothers


---
**Page 191**
---

- Enable extension officers to give advice equally to men and women.
- Reduce migration pressure on women in the Primary Cooperative
- Discourage cultural and social norms
- Build the capacity of current and potential women leaders of cooperatives, equipping them with leadership skills and knowledge
- Create a women's council to improve the position of women and source talent for leadership positions. Hired more women for cooperative jobs, for instance as extension officers, nursery staff and bio-composters.

At the end of the project, the expected result of these efforts should that at least 10,725 women, youth and PLWD accede to roles of meaningful participation in the targeted value chains. This activity will be executed by FAO with GCF funding, in close collaboration with Agriterra and the Government of Kenya.

The project will use eight core strategies to achieve its gender and social inclusion (GSI) objectives these are: [1] Supporting and strengthening Kenya’s existing gender-sensitive legal and institutional frameworks related to cooperatives; [2] Strengthening women’s access to resources, services, markets, and decision-making within producer organizations, county agencies, and private sector; [3] Supporting gender-responsive social inclusion policies and practices in the agricultural value chains; [4] Promoting climate-resilient and low-carbon production and processing practices, technologies, assets, and risk reduction mechanisms; [5] Strengthening women, PLWD, and youth representation and participation in decision-making processes at all levels of cooperatives and other organizations; [6] Developing tools to disseminate and demonstrate CRESA knowledge, technologies and practices in ways that are gender-responsive; [7] Collecting, analyzing, and using gender-disaggregated data on project activities, outcomes, and climate indicators so collected, analyzed, and fed back into project activities iteratively and in a participatory manner; [8] Support GESI agrifinance climate for vulnerable smallholders and their organizations to access financial services that support climate-resilient and low-carbon growth/supporting gender-responsive and socially inclusive financial services, climate finance, and bundled services for enhancing women, PLWD, and youth actions towards adaptation and mitigation. The project will also promote the development of tools to identify locally inclusive adaptation and mitigation technologies, markets, and labour practices; [8] And Promote and monitor gender and social safeguards to reduce climate risks for the most vulnerable.

The project includes strategies for addressing gender-related barriers in each output, which are highlighted in Box 2. Feasibility Study, Chapter 5.


The process of integrating gender: The information gathered from the gender analysis/assessment should be considered in all stages of the project cycle: design, formulation, implementation and monitoring and evaluation. In each of these stages, CRLCSA staff will be trained on gender and keep a ‘gender lens’ in mind throughout their work. This will ensure that the project addresses the root causes of gender inequalities that emerge from the project; ensure the different needs, interests and opportunities of women are considered at all levels of the project design, implementation, monitoring and evaluation, capacity development; ensure equal participation of women and men in management arrangements and as beneficiaries, partners and key stakeholders; and ensure women’s equal participation in decision-making processes. The CRLCSA will also provide gender training to its staff and partners related to the importance of addressing gender issues to achieve the gender-related project objectives via dedicated training sessions led by the Gender Specialist(s). The perspectives of women's women's organizations will be continuously sought throughout the project cycle, including through a gender advisory group for gender mainstreaming throughout the project implementation.

In Monitoring and Reporting, CILCSA project staff will collect sex- and age-disaggregated baseline data at county-level that will be monitored throughout implementation (mid-line) and evaluation (end-line) surveys. Qualitative assessments will also be periodically conducted on the gender-specific benefits that can be expected from the interventions. This will be incorporated in the annual Project Implementation Report, Mid-Term Report, and Terminal Evaluation.

# Annex 2: Data Collection for Gender Assessment

## Instrument I - Questionnaire Guide for Co-op Members

### Target population: Cooperative members**
**Prioritize the following value chains:** Fruit trees, African leafy vegetables

### Sample size: 2 women from each value chain targeted

### Location: Kenya (countries targeted by project)

### Purpose of questionnaire: To identify and understand barriers and opportunities for women's engagement in the agricultural value chain, that influence their participation, performance, benefits, and empowerment.

## Table 1: Circumstances of Interview (Fill out for each interviewee)

| Code number (gender, location, date, e.g., female, Bomet County, Kenya, 02/23) | Date (dd/mm/yyyy) | Name of Cooperative | Rural/Urban |
|---------------------------------------------------------------------------|------------------|--------------------|------------|
| Age | Marital Status | Father's Name/Ethnic Community/Identity | Religious Affiliation | Type of Agricultural Value Chains Involved in (e.g., dairy, vegetables, tea), Telephone Number (for following up with the results of the interview) |

### A. Participation Questions

Big question: What are the barriers to entry and/or requirements for men's and women's active engagement at any node of the value chain?

A1: What type of work do women and men in the co-op typically do? Are there certain types of jobs that are never done by men or women? What about differences between older and younger women?

A2: Are there any activities in this value chain that are considered more socially acceptable "appropriate" for women or men? Why?

A3 If so, what is the group (men and women), can women actively participate? Why? Has this changed in the past 10 years(s) for change?

### B. Performance Questions

Big question: What are the disparities in men's and women's ability to maintain or improve their position in the value chain?

B1: Do women access informal markets (outside the co-op) to sell or buy VC products (e.g., selling milk to neighbors, working as "hawkers" or street vendors, selling outside of formal cooperatives)?

B2: Is there a "ceiling for success" for women in the value chain? For example, if a woman starts with a small business and it becomes more successful, will a spouse/male relative step in to assist in running the business?


---
**Page 197**
---

C. Benefits Questions

Big question: What are the differences in men’s and women’s ability to access and control income, assets, or other forms of well-being derived from value chain participation?

C1: Regarding the income you make, what generally happens to the money you make? Who keeps it, decides about how to spend, or save it? Have these dynamics changed over time?
C2: Are there benefits beyond additional income that you derive from being a member of the co-op?

D. Empowerment Questions

Big question: What steps/actions are needed so that women can control the benefits of their participation in value chain/cultural value chains to make and carry out strategic decisions about their own lives?

D1: What types of services could the co-op offer that would contribute to women’s empowerment?
D2: Are you aware of any policies (national or county-level) that contribute to gender equality?

## Instrument II - Questionnaire Guide for Co-op Leadership

### Target population: Cooperation leaders
- Sample size: At least 1 woman in a co-op leadership position from each value chain
- Location: Kenya (counties targeted by project)
- Purpose: To identify the key gender issues and inequalities within specific agricultural cooperatives, and discuss solutions towards improving women's participation, performance, benefits, and empowerment

## Table 1: Circumstances of Interview [86 out for each interviewee]

| Field | Data |
|---|---|
| Co-op ID | (e.g., Bomet County, 17 Nov 2022) |

| Name of Cooperative | Rural/Urban |
| Name of Interviewee | Age |

## A: Participation Questions

Big question: What are the barriers to entry and/or requirements for men's and women's active engagement in the value chain?

A1: What are the specific challenges women face in participating in the VC/co-op?
- Has the co-op adopted any strategies to foster the equitable participation of men, women, and youth across the value chain? If yes, what have been the challenges and gains associated with these strategies?
- At co-op management level, do women have opportunities to serve as leaders? If yes, what types of opportunities are available?
- Has the co-op ever had a woman's leadership at 'chairman' level (i.e., head of governance)?

## B: Performance Questions

Big question: What are the disparities in men's and women's ability to maintain or improve their position in the value chain?

B1: What are the types of services the co-op provides to members? Are there any services women have difficulty accessing compared to men?
- Do women typically own the means of production in the VC (i.e., cows, chickens, land)?
- Do women commonly rely on both informal and formal markets in the VC?

## C: Benefits Questions

Big question: What are the differences in men’s and women’s ability to access and control income, assets, or other facets of well-being derived from value chain participation?

C1: Are there any gender norms that influence women's ability to benefit from being a co-op member?
C2: Does the co-op provide equitable benefit-sharing mechanisms (e.g., bank accounts, credit services) to ensure that women benefit financially and can control those benefits?

D: Empowerment Questions
Big question: What steps/changes are needed so that women can control the benefits of their participation in agricultural value chains to make and carry out strategic decisions about their own lives?

D1: What types of services could the co-op offer that would contribute to women's empowerment?
D2: Is there alignment with any policies (national or county level) that contribute to gender equality?
D3: Has the co-op received gender equality/women’s empowerment training? If yes, fill in contact details for organization

# PART II: GENDER ACTION PLAN

## Introduction

1. The Gender Assessment above describes the current state of women in Kenya compared to men, and to a lesser extent based on limited data, how gender intersects with age, socioeconomic status, household headship status, and disability status to influence participation and benefit from engaging in agricultural change practices. While recognizing that the project is designed around a gender and development practice, it is not enough to target "women" as a homogeneous group in terms of project beneficiaries[1]. From the Gender Assessment, female youth[2], female-headed households (including those with children), women living with disabilities, and women belonging to vulnerable groups in CSA value chains, based on how their gender interacts with other axes of social differentiation and inequality. Thus, the project's gender mainstreaming strategy makes a concerted effort to consider intersectionality when designing and implementing activities. Women, men, youth[3], people with disabilities, and females living with disabilities are equally represented in all project activities. Given this approach, gender equality is achieved by ensuring that 50% of each activity is female, as well as the number of and % of female farmers disaggregated by age, household headship status, and disability status; number and % of male farmers disaggregated by age, household headship status, and disability status[4].

2. The Gender Action Plan (GAP) for the proposed project tackles gender inequalities across several priority areas: access to resources, land tenure, decision-making power, and income generation. It seeks to address existing inequalities in participation [at intra-household level and within producer organizations/cooperatives], workloads (prioritizing agricultural technologies and practices for adaptation and mitigation that reduce workload), education (including access to information and communication technology, such as mobiles, internet information, technologies, livelihood incomes, credit), and collective action (working with women's groups as platforms for enhancing access, agency, and voice in climate-smart agriculture). In including these gender gaps, the project seeks to address the underlying causes of inequality. Additionally, culturally Action Learning Systems (GALS) to address the underlying discriminating socio-cultural and gender norms that perpetuate inequalities in the agricultural sector. This approach respects a culturally sensitive, multi-level approach that includes women and men in all their diversity.

3. The Gender Action Plan details how the goal of gender equality will be mainstreamed in two ways: participation in activities and the content of activities. Intersecting factors will be taken into account when targeting high-risk genders and when designing and implementing activities. Women, female youth[5], female-headed households (including those with children), women living with disabilities, and women belonging to vulnerable groups in CSA value chains are equally represented in all project activities.

[1] While the project considers intersectional socio-economic factors, it does not take a formal intersectionality approach as outlined by Crenshaw. However, the project recognizes that gender intersects with other axes of social differentiation and inequality in various ways. For example, women living with disabilities face additional barriers to accessing resources through existing programmes and projects. Sartaj. https://doi.org/10.34067/CE2R8

[2] Masanja, H., & Mwakikagile, G. (2015). Gender equality in agriculture: The role of gender mainstreaming in the CGIAR system. In CGIAR Research Program on Maize (pp. 9-34). International Food Policy Research Institute.

[3] Masanja, H., & Mwakikagile, G. (2015). Gender equality in agriculture: The role of gender mainstreaming in the CGIAR system. In CGIAR Research Program on Maize (pp. 9-34). International Food Policy Research Institute.

[4] According to the Kenyan Constitution of 2010, youth are defined as people aged between 18–35; a revision from the 2007 National Youth Policy which defines youth as those aged between 16 and 35. The African Union (AU) defines youth as those under the age of 24 years, and the East African Community (EAC) defines youth as those between 18 and 35 years. In most countries, the proportion of youth is 20-44% for Africa, Asia, and Oceania; and below these ages make up 40% of the total population.

[5] Adaptation to climate change is a gendered issue that exacerbates climate change impacts, while mitigation deals with addressing the root causes of climate change (i.e., Greenhouse Gas Emissions).

headed households, and females living with disabilities all have specific quotas that are required for each activity. Mainstreaming in terms of participation will be ensured by setting the target proportion of participants from each of these intersections to be roughly equal to the relative population sizes at the national level: 50% women (of which 33% are FGM-16.5% of total project population), 1% for women LWG, and in-corporating gender transformative approaches into all training materials. Mainstreaming in terms of youth participation quotas is 25% (25% of total project population) and 1% for men LWG (.5% of total project population). Mainstreaming in terms of content will be ensured by integrating gender transformative approaches, such as the inclusion of gender-transformative questions in all surveys, questionnaires, and training materials. These training materials include the "gender- and youth-specific" trainings, such as the Specialized Agripreneurs Training Programmes in Women's Leadership and Youth Leadership, as well as more 'general' trainings on gender equality.

4 For gender-responsive and transformative project activities to be successfully implemented and have impact, capacity-building, awareness, and sensitization around leadership and governance, at county level and within cooperative societies and producer organizations is required. Giving the support from local political leaders and community members is critical for addressing gender inequalities and social exclusion in agriculture. Thus, capacity building will be mandated for all county and in-corporating gender transformative approaches into all training materials.

In the context of a changing climate, and data collection and MEL teams will receive specialized training in the collection of gender, sex-and age-disaggregated data. Given that more than half of the targeted population is female (50% women, 1% for women LWG, and 25% for youth, including those with 3% disability), the successful implementation of the Gender Action Plan requires half of the total project budget. The relationships among the project objectives, actions, indicators, targets, and baselines are as shown in table below.

5 The day-to-day implementation of the GAP for the project will be led by the National Gender and Social Inclusion Specialists, who will be recruited by the project. Given the implementation of GAIS and GTAs more broadly, knowledge and experience in GTA is a requirement in the TORs for these positions.

## GAP Expected Outcomes, Activities and Targets

### Component 1 - Enabling local government support for adaptation and mitigation

**Expected gender outcome:** Component 1 addresses capacity gaps in public agro-climate service providers to support women, men, and youth farmers in proactive adaptation. This will be achieved by providing training on climate change services to extension workers, women, men, PLWD, and youth farmers with access and benefit from gender-sensitive and socially inclusive public agro-climate services for CRLCSA. This means building capacity on both the local administration side (i.e., county governments and agricultural extension service providers) as well as the farmer side (women, men, PLWD, and youth). The training will also address issues of gender equality by considering intersecting social factors. These activities will ensure women, PLWD, and youth have strengthened capacities on climate-resilient agriculture and access to public agro-climate services for CRLCSA. This will be achieved through the following GAPs (see the LURE).

**GAP 1.1.1: Women, PLWD, and youth's participation, leadership, and decision-making in cooperative societies and value chains is strengthened via enhanced gender-responsive and socially inclusive local administrations.**
